Abstract categorical residues and Calabi-Yau structures Thanks: University of Georgia, Athens, GA 30602, USA. Email: yuangaohl@gmail.com
Yuan Gao

Abstract
. Inspired by the simple fact that a compact n n -dimensional manifold-with-boundary which satisfies Poincaré-Lefschetz duality of dimension n n has a boundary which itself satisfies Poincaré duality of dimension n − 1 n-1 , we show that the categorical formal punctured neighborhood of infinity, a canonical categorical construction associated to every A ∞ A_{\infty} -category, has a weak proper Calabi-Yau structure of dimension n − 1 n-1 whenever the original A ∞ A_{\infty} -category admits a weak smooth Calabi-Yau structure of dimension n n . Applications include proper Calabi-Yau structures on Rabinowitz Fukaya category of a Liouville manifold and Orlov’s singularity category of a proper singular Gorenstein scheme of finite type.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Rabinowitz Fukaya categories

The Rabinowitz Fukaya category
\mathcal{RW}(X)
of a Liouville manifold
X
,
introduced by
[
28
]
and appearing in
[
6
]
,
is an
A_{\infty}
-category which is geometrically constructed using methods of Floer theory and measures the failure of Poincaré duality to hold for the wrapped Fukaya category
\mathcal{W}(X)

[
2
,
3
,
29
]
.
This measurement originates from a classical analogy in topology,
which says cochains on the boundary of a compact oriented manifold-with-boundary measures the failure of Poincaré duality between chains and cochains on the compact manifold-with-boundary.
As noted, the boundary of a compact manifold-with-boundary is itself a closed manifold,
on which Poincaré duality always holds.
Since the Rabinowitz Fukaya category behaves in a manner as an invariant of the boundary-at-infinity and is also a measurement for failure of Poincaré duality,
a natural question arises: does the Rabinowitz Fukaya category have a similar property, i.e., satisfy Poincaré duality itself?

The construction of the chain-level
A_{\infty}
-operations on the morphism spaces in
\mathcal{RW}(X)
,
named the Lagrangian Rabinowitz Floer complexes defined by mapping cones of Floer continuation maps from a very negative Hamiltonian to a very positive one,
was realized by
[
28
]
using variants of popsicles originating from
[
2
,
47
]
.
Algebraically, compared to works of
[
18
,
17
]
which study product and coproduct structures on homology of mapping cones aiming at understanding algebraic structures on the closed-string Rabinowitz Floer cohomology,
the algebraic structures in the open-string theory tend to have a particularly simple construction realizing all of them simultaneously on the chain level.

Return to the question about Poincaré duality.
Numerous important works concerning this question on higher-algebra levels are ultimately related to geometry,
including but not limited to
[
26
,
14
,
54
,
53
,
33
]
and many more for which one cannot name all,
lead us to consider the notion of a
weak proper Calabi-Yau structure
on an
A_{\infty}
-category,
a homotopy version of Poincaré duality for
A_{\infty}
-categories that are mostly essentially well-defined up to homotopy.

Definition 1.1

(Definition
2.27
)
.

A weak proper Calabi-Yau structure on an
A_{\infty}
-category
\mathcal{C}
of dimension
n
is a chain map of degree
-n

(1.1)

\phi:\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C})\to{\mathbf{k}}[-n],

or equivalently a cycle in
\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C})^{\vee}[-n]
,
such that the induced pairing

(1.2)

H^{*}(\hom_{\mathcal{C}}(X,Y))\otimes H^{n-*}(\hom_{\mathcal{C}}(Y,X))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle[\mu_{\mathcal{C}}^{2}]}}{{\to}}H^{n}(\hom_{\mathcal{C}}(Y,Y))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle[i]}}{{\to}}\mathrm{HH}_{n}(\mathcal{C})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle[\phi]}}{{\to}}{\mathbf{k}}

is nondegenerate,
where
\mu_{\mathcal{C}}^{2}
is the product, as part of the
A_{\infty}
-structure maps of
\mathcal{C}
,
and
i:\hom_{\mathcal{C}}(Y,Y)\to\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{C})
is the canonical inclusion of chain complexes.

In this paper, we study further categorical structures on the Rabinowitz Fukaya category of a Liouville manifold and prove that the Rabinowitz Fukaya category, as an
A_{\infty}
-category, admits a chain-level weak proper Calabi-Yau structure
\phi
.
Recall from
[
29
]
that a Liouville manifold is said to be
nondegenenrate
,
if it admits a finite collection
\mathcal{B}
of exact cylindrical Lagrangians satisfying Abouzaid’s generation criterion
[
3
]
,
meaning that the
open-closed map
(a canonical geometrically defined map for Fukaya categories which will be recalled in §
8
)

(1.3)

\mathcal{OC}:\mathrm{HH}_{*-n}(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{B})\to SH^{*}(X)

hits the unit in symplectic cohomology.
We note that Definition
1.1
is meaningful even for non-proper
A_{\infty}
-categories,
although it does not say in the usual case of a proper
A_{\infty}
-category that the weak smooth Calabi-Yau structure induces a quasi-isomorphism from the diagonal bimodule
\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}
to its linear dual
\mathcal{C}^{\vee}
.

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
{\mathbf{k}}
be a field of arbitrary characteristic.
Let
X
be a non-degenerate Liouville manifold with
c_{1}(X)=0
.
Then the Rabinowitz Fukaya category
\mathcal{RW}(X)
admits a weak proper Calabi-Yau structure of dimension
n-1
.

Some comments on Theorem
1.2
are in order.
This theorem seems quite puzzling at the first glance,
because the Rabinowitz Fukaya category
\mathcal{RW}(X)
is almost always non-proper,
as is the wrapped Fukaya category
\mathcal{W}(X)

[
30
,
28
]
.
But as is already mentioned above,
nondegeneracy of the pairing (
1.2
) does not rely on properness of the category,
yet for a non-proper category it might not be equivalent to the data of a quasi-isomorphism between the diagonal bimodule and the linear dual bimodule in the usual sense.
A related issue was addressed in
[
19
]
,
where it is shown that the (closed-string) Rabinowitz Floer cohomology of any Liouville domain is a self-dual locally linearly compact vector space in the sense of Lefschetz
[
35
]
, or equivalently a Tate vector space in the sense of Beilinson-Feigin-Mazur
[
9
]
.
However, our result is independent of the use of the theory of topological vector spaces,
but is expected to reflect this structure in an appropriate sense.
For example, we include a discussion on the local linear compactness of the Rabinowitz Floer complex that we considered in this paper (Proposition
5.12
).

In
[
6
]
, it is proved that if the wrapped Floer cohomology for every pair of Lagrangians is finite dimensional in each degree,
then the derived Rabinowitz Fukaya category, as an ordinary category,
is Calabi-Yau of dimension
n-1
in the sense that there exists a non-degenerate pairing of degree
1-n
between the morphism spaces.
They constructed a pairing on the Rabinowitz Floer complexes object-wise,
and showed that the induced maps are quasi-isomorphisms on cohomology;
however, this type of duality statement is irrelevant to the
A_{\infty}
-structure of
\mathcal{RW}(X)
.
It is not clear from the construction of the object-wise map whether there are higher order pieces of information that would yield a closed element in the Hochschild chain complex.
In this sense, Theorem
1.2
can be regarded as a chain-level improvement that is compatible with the
A_{\infty}
-structure.
But as it turns out, a direct geometric construction of the desired trace map (
1.1
) using holomorphic disk counts seems to be out of reach of current techniques.

Theorem
1.2
is in fact much stronger than a cohomology-level statement,
as the induced pairing on
\mathcal{RW}(X)
has to be compatible with the
A_{\infty}
-structure in a very strong way.
In addition, the existence of a Calabi-Yau structure implies non-trivial relations between Hochchild invariants of the category,
on which there are TQFT operations
[
34
]
.
In addition, a proper Calabi-Yau structure on the Rabinowitz Fukaya category appears as a first piece of evidence of the existence of a more general type of algebraic structures on the wrapped Fukaya category,
namely the pre-Calabi-Yau structures introduced by
[
34
]
.

Remark 1.3
.

We expect that Theorem
1.2
holds even over
{\mathbb{Z}}
.
But since the duality issues are more delicate,
and since we also follow the construction of the weak smooth Calabi-Yau structure for the wrapped Fukaya category in
[
29
]
which only did it over a field (though seems valid over
{\mathbb{Z}}
),
we will restrict ourselves to the case where
{\mathbf{k}}
is a field.

Going in another direction, we ask if there is a lift of the weak proper Calabi-Yau structure in Theorem
1.2
to a
strong
proper Calabi-Yau structure.
Recall that

Definition 1.4
.

A strong proper Calabi-Yau structure of dimension
n
on
\mathcal{C}
is a chain map

(1.4)

\tilde{\phi}:\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C})_{hS^{1}}\to{\mathbf{k}}[-n]

such that the composition
\tilde{\phi}\circ pr
is a weak proper Calabi-Yau structure,
where

(1.5)

pr:\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C})\to\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C})_{hS^{1}}

is the canonical projection to homotopy orbits.

Conjecture 1.5
.

The weak proper Calabi-Yau structure from Theorem
1.2
admits a lift to a strong proper Calabi-Yau structure on
\mathcal{RW}(X)
.

Although it is not a priori guaranteed that the compatibility between the
A_{\infty}
-structure and the pairing on
\mathcal{RW}(X)
as strong as the conditions required by a
cyclic
A_{\infty}
-category
(
[
20
,
26
,
13
]
,
if Conjecture
1.5
is proved, over a field of characteristic zero there is not much essential difference due to
[
33
]
,
which roughly says that the
A_{\infty}
-structure can be made strictly compatible with the pairing up to some canonical quasi-equivalence.
In addition, it is shown in
[
34
]
that an
n
-dimensional pre-Calabi-Yau structure on an
A_{\infty}
-algebra
A
is equivalent to a cyclic
A_{\infty}
-algebra structure on
A^{\vee}[1-n]\oplus A
of dimension
n-1
.
The Rabinowitz Fukaya category behaves in a way similar to
A^{\vee}[1-n]\oplus A
(as we will see through §
5.6
),
but it is too much to ask for strict cyclic symmetry as well as a full pre-Calabi-Yau structure in the relevant setups of Floer theory in the non-compact case.
In this sense Theorem
1.2
provides a weak version, even in the infinite-dimensional case,
of the existence of an additional structure on the wrapped Fukaya category
\mathcal{W}(X)
that would be close to an honest pre-Calabi-Yau structure (which is expected to exist but hard to be implemented due to lack of symmetries).

Some careful treatment of the chain-level circle actions on the wrapped and Rabinowitz Fukaya categories and also on their closed-string counterparts, using ideas and techniques of e.g
[
31
]
and/or even
[
1
]
,
should lead to an answer to Conjecture
1.5
.

1.2.
Orlov’s singularity category

Somewhat surprisingly, our method for proving Theorem
1.2
is very general,
and will lead to other important examples from algebraic geometry,
among which we’d like to address the Orlov’s category of singularities
[
39
]
.
Recall that for a separated Noetherian scheme
X
over a field
{\mathbf{k}}
,
the Orlov’s singularity category is defined to be the Verdier quotient

D^{b}_{sg}(X):=D^{b}Coh(X)/\mathrm{perf}(X),

the bounded derived category of coherent sheaves on
X
modulo the full subcategory of perfect complexes (in the derived category).
This has a natural dg enhancement

(1.6)

\mathcal{D}^{b}_{sg}(X)=Coh(X)/\mathrm{Perf}(X)

given by the Drinfeld quotient of the dg enhancement
Coh(X)
by the full dg subcategory of perfect complexes.

It is known by
[
38
]
that if
X
is finite type over
{\mathbf{k}}
,
Gorenstein
and has isolated singularities,
the bounded derived category
D^{b}_{sg}(X)
has a Serre functor given by a shift
(-)[n-1]
,
where
n
is the (Krull) dimension of
X
,
and moreover provides a closed formula for the non-degenerate pairing.
The existence of duality actually reduces to a local computation dating back to
[
5
]
.
It is therefore an interesting question whether this can be promoted to a proper Calabi-Yau structure on the dg enhancement.
In fact, we deduce the following result, without assuming singularities being isolated,
but will require a global trivialization of the canonical bundle:

Theorem 1.6
.

Let
{\mathbf{k}}
be a perfect field,
and
X
a Gorenstein scheme of finite type over
{\mathbf{k}}
, of dimension
n
.
Suppose the canonical bundle of
X
admits a trivialization.
Then the natural dg enhancement
\mathcal{D}^{b}_{sg}(X)
has a proper Calabi-Yau structure of dimension
n-1
.

The existence of such a proper Calabi-Yau structure can be seen as a dg enhancement of a Serre functor on the derived category of singularities,
and will follow from the general abstract machinery Theorem
1.7
,
to be discussed in the following subsection,
as well as the feature that the Orlov’s category is in the form of a dg quotient.
Importantly, we will find a chain-level graded symmetric non-degenerate pairing on
\operatorname{Hom}
spaces of some other category,
quasi-equivalent to the dg category
\mathcal{D}^{b}_{sg}(X)^{op}
,
the opposite of Orlov’s singularity category.
More details will be given in §
4
.

On the other hand, Orlov’s singularity category is also closely related to matrix factorization category.
In fact, Orlov
[
39
]
proved that for
W:U\to\mathbb{A}^{1}_{{\mathbf{k}}}
a regular function from an affine scheme
U
over a field
{\mathbf{k}}
of characteristic zero,
the matrix factorization category
MF_{w}(U,W)
,
as a triangulated category,
is exact equivalent to the singularity category
D^{b}_{sg}(X_{w})
,
where
X_{w}=W^{-1}(w)
,
for any critical value
w
of
W
(in fact,
MF_{w}(U,W)
is trivial if
w
is a regular value).
It is proved in
[
50
]
that over
{\mathbf{k}}={\mathbb{C}}
,
the
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-graded dg category of matrix factorizations has a proper Calabi-Yau structure in
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-graded sense,
by constructing an explicit cyclically symmetric pairing.
It would be interesting to understand this from a conceptual point of view using Theorem
1.6
and Orlov’s equivalence.
Although our method does not apply immediately because the category of matrix factorizations is
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-graded,
and Orlov’s equivalence also breaks the
{\mathbb{Z}}
-grading down to
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-grading,
we expect that the method (i.e., Theorem
1.7
) based on which Theorem
1.6
is proved,
can be extended to the
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-graded setting.

1.3.
The categorical residue

As already mentioned previously,
such a Calabi-Yau structure in Theorem
1.2
does not seem to be reachable by a direct Floer-theoretic construction via holomorphic curves.
It is therefore inevitable to reinvestigate the problem in a more general and abstract framework,
where the duality result is independent of the presentation of the relevant
A_{\infty}
-category as a Fukaya-type category.
The strategy of proof of Theorem
1.2
is therefore to appeal to a purely categorical structure that may be interpreted as the ‘boundary’ of an
A_{\infty}
-category with a weak smooth Calabi-Yau structure.
The nature of the construction,
for which details are carried out throughout §
3
also based on results in §
2
,
suggests an appropriate name for this invariant -
the residue
.
One reason for this is that the chain level maps are defined in a way similar to Tate’s construction of abstract residues
[
51
]
using traces of certain operators on infinite-dimensional spaces,
where the theory is further generalized by Beilinson
[
8
]
.

The
residue
on the
categorical formal punctured neighborhood of infinity
,
is a canonical Hochschild invariant associated with any
A_{\infty}
-category equipped with a weak smooth Calabi-Yau structure.
The notion of the categorical formal punctured neighborhood of infinity is first introduced by
[
23
]
in the case of dg categories,
which has a straightforward generalization to
A_{\infty}
-categories further discussed in
[
28
]
.
Recall that for an
A_{\infty}
-category
\mathcal{C}
,
its
(algebraizable) categorical formal punctured neighborhood of infinity
,
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}
,
is defined to be the essential image of the induced Yoneda functor

(1.7)

\bar{y}:\mathcal{C}\to\mathrm{mod\!-\!}\mathcal{C}=\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{op},\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathbf{k}}})\to\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{op},\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathbf{k}}}/\mathrm{Perf}_{{\mathbf{k}}}),

where
\mathrm{Perf}_{k}
is the subcategory of
\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathbf{k}}}
consisting of perfect chain complexes.
The target
\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathbf{k}}}/\mathrm{Perf}_{{\mathbf{k}}}
is an algebraic analogue of the notion of the Calkin algebra
[
12
]
,
measuring in the non-proper case the difference between all ‘bounded linear operators’ and ‘compact operators’.
In this sense, the non-triviality of
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}
measures the failure of
\mathcal{C}
to be proper.

The categorical formal punctured neighborhood of infinity has received recent attentions in both symplectic geometry
[
28
]
and string topology
[
41
]
.
The general philosophy along the lines of these results says that Calabi-Yau structures on
\mathcal{C}
induce product structures on
\mathrm{HH}_{*-n}(\mathcal{C},\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})
, or equivalently
\mathrm{HH}^{*}(\mathcal{C},\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})

which in turn induce products on Hochschild homology of
\mathcal{C}
and coproducts on reduced Hochschild homology.
This naturally leads to algebraic approaches to understanding structures on and relations between symplectic cohomology, symplectic homology, Rabinowitz Floer cohomology v.s. loop homology, loop cohomology, Rabinowitz loop cohomology (
[
17
]
),
where the two sides of the theories are related by Viterbo’s theorem
[
55
,
4
,
7
]
.

The definition
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}
as the essential image of (
1.7
) makes sense for any
A_{\infty}
-category, smooth or not.
However, the structure of
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}
is not always well behaved for arbitrary
\mathcal{C}
.
We will explore its properties and find, when
\mathcal{C}
is a weak smooth Calabi-Yau category of dimension
n
,
that there is a canonical map

(1.8)

\mathrm{res}:\mathrm{CC}_{n-1}(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})\to{\mathbf{k}}

defined in §
3.3
.

The following theorem gives a purely categorical account for the philosophy that the measurement of the failure of Poincaré duality for Poincaré-Lefschetz duality must itself satisfy Poincaré duality of one dimension less.

Theorem 1.7
.

Suppose
\mathcal{C}
is a smooth
A_{\infty}
-category having a weak smooth Calabi-Yau structure of dimension
n
.
Then its categorical formal punctured neighborhood
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}
carries a weak proper Calabi-Yau structure of dimension
n-1
,
given by the residue
\mathrm{res}
(
1.8
).

Remark 1.8
.

Despite the similarity of the names,
the reader should not confuse the induced pairing (which would naturally be called the residue pairing) with K. Saito’s higher residue pairing
[
43
]
or Shklyarov’s categorical analogue on negative/period cyclic homology
[
49
]
.

To relate this algebraic invariant to geometry, in particular the situation of Theorem
1.2
,
we introduce the following definition.

Definition 1.9

(Definition
2.30
)
.

Let
\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}
be
A_{\infty}
-categories with weak proper Calabi-Yau structures
\phi,\psi
of the same dimension
n
.
An
A_{\infty}
-functor
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
is called a weak proper Calabi-Yau functor if
[\psi\circ F]=[\phi]\in\mathrm{HH}_{n}(\mathcal{C})
.

F
is called weak proper Calabi-Yau equivalence if there exists a weak Calabi-Yau functor
G:\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{C}
such that the two way compositions are both homotopic to the identity.

Now let us go back from homological algebra to geometry.
Another main result of
[
28
, Theorem 1.1]
is to give an algebraic interpretation of the Rabinowitz Fukaya category,
which says there is a canonical
A_{\infty}
-functor

(1.9)

\Phi:\mathcal{RW}(X)\to\widehat{\mathcal{W}(X)}_{\infty},

which is a quasi-equivalence whenever the Liouville manifold
X
has
c_{1}(X)=0
and is nondegenerate in the sense of
[
29
]
; see Theorem
5.15
.
We shall use this quasi-equivalence to produce a weak proper Calabi-Yau structure on
\mathcal{RW}(X)
by pulling back the residue from Theorem
1.7
.
In §
6
we verify that the induced pairing agrees with the tautological pairing,
and is therefore nondegenerate.

By construction, the functor
\Phi
will be a weak proper Calabi-Yau equivalence in the sense of Definition
1.9
(Definition
2.30
).
Nonetheless, we expect there is a possible way of constructing a proper Calabi-Yau structure purely in terms of Floer theory,
which would potentially lead and/or be related to a geometric pre-Calabi-Yau structures on the wrapped Fukaya category.
This geometric proper Calabi-Yau structure should induce the same pairing,
such that the quasi-equivalence
\Phi
is a weak proper Calabi-Yau functor between these weak proper Calabi-Yau structures.
We leave this to a future research as the current method of using popsicles (§
5.2
) to construct the
A_{\infty}
-structures does not seem to be applicable to disks with more than two outputs.

Regarding the algebro-geometric result Theorem
1.6
,
it is shown by
[
23
, Theorem 9.2]
that for a separated scheme
X
over finite type over a perfect field
{\mathbf{k}}
,

\mathcal{D}^{b}_{sg}(X)^{op}
is quasi-equivalent to
\widehat{\mathcal{D}^{b}Coh(X)}_{\infty}
.
For
X
Gorenstein of dimension
n
with a trivialization of the canonical bundle,
the dg category
\mathcal{D}^{b}Coh(X)
admits a smooth Calabi-Yau structure of dimension
n
.
So by Theorem
1.7
, we get a proper Calabi-Yau structure of dimension
n-1
on
\mathcal{D}^{b}_{sg}(X)
.
More details will be given in §
4

Theorem
1.7
will follow from a more general study of a system of product structures on Hochschild cochain complexes with arbitrary bimodule coefficients,
as well as pairings and their induced maps on Hochschild chain complexes,
We carry these out throughout §
2
and §
3
,
especially §
2.5
, §
2.6
, §
2.7
, §
2.8
, §
3.3
, §
3.4
.

1.4.
Relation to closed-string theory

Rabinowitz Floer theory originates from a closed-string theory
[
16
,
15
]
,
which assigns to every Liouville manifold
X
a (co)chain complex

(1.10)

RFC^{*}(X)

called the
Rabinowitz Floer complex
,
whose cohomology is called the
Rabinowitz Floer cohomology
and denoted by

(1.11)

RFH^{*}(X).

The definitions will be given in §
7
.
On the Rabinowitz Floer complex there is also a tautological pairing

(1.12)

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{taut}:RFC^{*}(X)\otimes RFC^{2n-1-*}(X)\to{\mathbf{k}}

defined in (
7.16
),
which is nondegenerate.

This is also closely related to our theory of Rabinowitz Fukaya categories.
In
[
28
, Theorem 1.6]
we showed that when the Liouville manifold
X
is nondegenerate and has
c_{1}(X)=0
,
then there is an isomorphism

(1.13)

\mathrm{HH}_{*-n}(\mathcal{W}(X),\mathcal{RW}(X))\cong RFH^{*}(X),

whenever the usual open-closed map to symplectic cohomology

\mathcal{OC}:\mathrm{HH}_{*-n}(\mathcal{W}(X),\mathcal{W}(X))\to SH^{*}(X)

is an isomorphism.
But our first approach in
[
28
]
is somewhat indirect;
in particular, we did not construct a geometric map realizing this isomorphism,
and therefore could not discuss its further properties.
One additional structure, in comparison to (
1.12
), is that the Hochschild chain complex
\mathrm{CC}_{*-n}(\mathcal{W}(X),\mathcal{RW}(X))
also has a canonical pairing

(1.14)

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\sigma}:\mathrm{CC}_{*-n}(\mathcal{W}(X),\mathcal{RW}(X))\otimes\mathrm{CC}_{(2n-1-*)-n}(\mathcal{W}(X),\mathcal{RW}(X))\to{\mathbf{k}},

defined in terms of the weak smooth Calabi-Yau structure
\sigma
on
\mathcal{W}(X)
;
see §
8.4
for the detailed construction.
For every exact cylindrical submanifold
L
,
this induces a pairing

(1.15)

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\sigma,L}:\mathcal{RW}^{*-n}(L,L)\otimes\mathcal{RW}^{n-1-*}(L,L)\to{\mathbf{k}}

via the canonical inclusion map of chain complexes
i:\mathcal{RW}^{*}(L,L)\to\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{W}(X),\mathcal{RW}(X))
.
A priori, this could be different from the pairing on
\mathcal{RW}
induced from the weak proper Calabi-Yau structure on
\mathcal{RW}
from Theorem
1.2
.
To better understand the relationship between the Rabinowitz Fukaya category and the Rabinowitz Floer cohomology,
especially the fruitful algebraic structures on both sides,
we have the following:

Theorem 1.10
.

Floer theory gives rise to a geometrically defined chain map

(1.16)

\mathcal{OC}_{R}:\mathrm{CC}_{*-n}(\mathcal{W}(X),\mathcal{RW}(X))\to RFC^{*}(X).

If
X
is a nondegenerate Liouville manifold with
c_{1}(X)=0
,
then
\mathcal{OC}_{R}
is a quasi-isomorphism and respects the pairing (
1.14
) on
\mathrm{CC}_{*-n}(\mathcal{W}(X),\mathcal{RW}(X))
and the pairing on Rabinowitz Floer cohomology
RFH^{*}(X)
induced by the tautological pairing
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{taut}
(
1.12
).

We expect that the induced map on homology

(1.17)

\mathcal{OC}_{R}:\mathrm{HH}_{*-n}(\mathcal{W}(X),\mathcal{RW}(X))\to RFH^{*}(X),

is an isomorphism of unital rings,
where the left hand side carries a product dual to the cup product on
\mathrm{HH}^{*}(\mathcal{W}(X),\mathcal{RW}(X))
defined in (
3.29
) in §
3.2
.
In addition, the full open-closed map (
1.16
) should respect the pairings on the two-sides,
where the pairing on the left hand side is induced by the product structure as well as the weak proper Calabi-Yau structure on
\mathcal{RW}
.
There is a chain-level construction on a product on
\mathrm{CC}_{*-n}(\mathcal{C},\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})
in
[
41
]
which nonetheless requires that
\mathcal{C}
have a pre-Calabi-Yau structure (at least up to
4
-truncation),
but that has not been fully established on the wrapped Fukaya category.

Overview of the paper

In §
2
, we provide the necessary homological algebra background for
A_{\infty}
-categories and multi-modules that will be relevant to various key notions throughout the paper.
In particular, we include a discussion of multi-tensor products and Hochschild homology/cohomology with coefficients in multi-modules,
and provide a general framework for product structures on Hochschild cochain complexes with various bimodule coefficients.
In §
3
, we recall the definition of the categorical formal punctured neighborhood of infinity,
and construct our main invariant - the residue on Hochschild chain complex of the categorical formal punctured neighborhood of infinity,
and prove Theorem
1.7
.
In §
4
, we include a concise discussion on the Calabi-Yau structure on Orlov’s singularity category.
In §
5
, we review the construction of the Rabinowitz Fukaya category of a Liouville manifold,
as well as some key features of it, among which in particular is the quasi-equivalence between
\mathcal{RW}(X)
and
\widehat{\mathcal{W}(X)}_{\infty}
.
In §
6
, we use the results developed earlier to endow
\mathcal{RW}(X)
a residue which induces a nondegenerate pairing on cohomology,
and therefore complete the proof of Theorem
1.2
.
In §
7
, we provide a chain-level definition of closed-string Rabinowitz Floer cohomology compatible with our geometric framework of the Rabinowitz Fukaya category.
Based on this, in §
8
we explore an open-closed relationship between the Rabinowitz Fukaya category and the closed-string Rabinowitz Floer cohomology,
and define an extension of the usual open-closed map.

2.
Homological algebra related to
A_{\infty}
-categories

The goal of this section is to review several important definitions and results surrounding
A_{\infty}
-categories,
and develop algebraic tools using which we will prove our main results Theorem
1.2
and Theorem
1.7
.
In addition, we discuss a system of operations on Hochschild chain and cochain complexes with different bimodule coefficients,
and relate such to pairings between bimodules.
We shall be very careful about signs and the opposite categories.

2.1.
A_{\infty}
-categories, functors and modules

We begin by recalling some basic definition about
A_{\infty}
-categories, functors, modules and bimodules,
for which references are by now relatively standard,
e.g.
[
lefevre
,
46
,
45
,
33
,
25
,
27
,
29
]
,
while mention that there are many more references.

Fix a ground field
{\mathbf{k}}
of arbitrary characteristic.
In this paper all
A_{\infty}
-categories will be small, linear over
{\mathbf{k}}
, graded by
{\mathbb{Z}}
, and cohomologically unital.
All unspecified tensor products are understood to be taken over
{\mathbf{k}}
.
The notation for the grading of an element
x
is

\deg(x)=|x|\in{\mathbb{Z}}.

Remark 2.1
.

Many of the constructions work for
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-graded
A_{\infty}
-categories,
including the construction of the Rabinowitz Fukaya category (§
5
),
the closed-string Rabinowitz Floer cohomology (§
7
),
and the open-closed map (§
8
).
However, the statements on quasi-equivalence/quasi-isomorphisms are not valid without a Calabi-Yau structure on the wrapped Fukaya category,
which only exists when
c_{1}(X)=0
and the category is
{\mathbb{Z}}
-graded.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a small
A_{\infty}
-category,
which has a set of objects
\mathrm{ob\ }\mathcal{C}
,
and composition maps of degree
2-k

(2.1)

\mu_{\mathcal{C}}^{k}:\mathcal{C}(X_{k-1},X_{k})\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{C}(X_{0},X_{1})\to\mathcal{C}(X_{0},X_{k})[2-k],

or equivalently the following maps of degree
0

(2.2)

\mu_{\mathcal{C}}^{k}:\mathcal{C}[1](X_{k-1},X_{k})\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{C}[1](X_{0},X_{1})\to\mathcal{C}[1](X_{0},X_{k}),

satisfying the
A_{\infty}
-equations

(2.3)

\sum_{i,j}(-1)^{\maltese_{1;i}}\mu_{\mathcal{C}}^{k-j+1}(x_{k},\ldots,x_{i+j+1},\mu_{\mathcal{C}}^{j}(x_{i+j},\ldots,x_{i+1}),x_{i},\ldots,x_{1}),

where

(2.4)

\maltese_{1;i}=|x_{1}|+\cdots+|x_{i}|-i

is the Koszul sign obtained by commuting
\mu_{\mathcal{C}}^{j}
to the right end of the expression (
2.3
).

\mathcal{C}
is said to be
strictly unital
if for every
X\in\mathrm{ob\ }\mathcal{C}
there exists
e_{X}\in\mathcal{C}(X,X)
such that

(2.5)

\begin{split}\mu^{1}_{\mathcal{C}}(e_{X})&=0,\\
(-1)^{|x|}\mu^{2}_{\mathcal{C}}(e_{X},x)&=\mu^{2}_{\mathcal{C}}(x,e_{X})=x,\\
\mu^{k}(\cdots,e_{X},\cdots)&=0\text{ for all }k>2.\end{split}

\mathcal{C}
is said to be
cohomologically unital
if the cohomology category
H(\mathcal{C})
is unital as an ordinary graded category.
In §
2
, we shall assume all
A_{\infty}
-categories in consideration are cohomologically unital.
For notational simplicity, we define

(2.6)

\mathcal{C}(X_{0},\ldots,X_{k}):=\mathcal{C}(X_{k-1},X_{k})\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{C}(X_{0},X_{1}).

Definition 2.2
.

For an
A_{\infty}
-category
\mathcal{C}
, its opposite category

(2.7)

\mathcal{C}^{op}

is defined to be the
A_{\infty}
-category with
\mathrm{ob\ }\mathcal{C}^{op}=\mathrm{ob\ }\mathcal{C}
,
morphism spaces

(2.8)

\mathcal{C}^{op}(X,Y)=\mathcal{C}(Y,X),

and
A_{\infty}
-structure maps

(2.9)

\mu^{k}_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})=(-1)^{\maltese_{1;k}}\mu^{k}_{\mathcal{C}}(x_{k},\ldots,x_{1}).

Let
\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}
be
A_{\infty}
-categories.
An
A_{\infty}
-functor
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
consists of a function
F:\mathrm{ob\ }\mathcal{C}\to\mathrm{ob\ }\mathcal{D}
,
together with maps

(2.10)

F^{k}:\mathcal{C}(X_{k-1},X_{k})\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{C}(X_{0},X_{1})\to\mathcal{D}(F(X_{0}),F(X_{k}))[1-k]

for all
X_{0},\ldots,X_{k}\in\mathrm{ob\ }\mathcal{C}
,
satisfying the
A_{\infty}
-functor equations

(2.11)

\begin{split}&\sum_{s\geq 1}\sum_{i_{1}+\cdots+i_{s}=d}\mu_{\mathcal{D}}^{s}(F^{i_{s}}(x_{d},\cdots,x_{i_{1}+\cdots+i_{s-1}+1}),\cdots,F^{i_{1}}(x_{i_{1}},\cdots,x_{1}))\\
=&\sum_{i,j}(-1)^{\maltese_{1;i}}F^{d-j+1}(x_{d},\cdots,x_{i+j+1},\mu_{\mathcal{C}}^{j}(x_{i+j},\cdots,x_{i+1}),x_{i},\cdots,x_{1}).\end{split}

The functor
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
is said to be
cohomological unital
,
if the induced functor on cohomology categories
H(F):H(\mathcal{C})\to H(\mathcal{D})
is unital.
In §
2
, we shall only consider cohomologically unital
A_{\infty}
-functors.

The set of all
A_{\infty}
-functors from
\mathcal{C}
to
\mathcal{D}
is denoted by
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
, which also forms an
A_{\infty}
-category (
[
46
]
),
in which the degree
g
part of the morphism space is

(2.12)

\hom_{\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})}^{g}(F,G)=\prod_{X_{0},\ldots,X_{k}\in\mathrm{ob\ }\mathcal{C}}\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{C}(X_{k-1},X_{k})\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{C}(X_{0},X_{1}),\mathcal{D}(F(X_{0}),G(X_{k}))[g-k]).

Elements in
\hom_{\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})}(F,G)
are called
A_{\infty}
-pre-natural transformations from
F
to
G
.
The
A_{\infty}
-structure maps are

(2.13)

\begin{split}&\mu^{1}_{\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})}(T)^{k}(x_{k},\ldots,x_{1})\\
=&\sum_{r,i}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}s_{1},\ldots,s_{r}\geq 0\\
s_{1}+\cdots+s_{r}=k\end{subarray}}(-1)^{\dagger}\mu_{\mathcal{D}}^{r}(G^{s_{r}}(x_{k},\ldots,x_{s_{1}+\cdots+s_{r-1}+1}),\ldots,\\
&G^{s_{i+1}}(x_{s_{1}+\cdots+s_{i+1}},\ldots,x_{s_{1}+\cdots+s_{i}+1}),T^{s_{i}}(x_{s_{1}+\cdots+s_{i}},\ldots,x_{s_{1}+\cdots+s_{i-1}+1}),\\
&F^{s_{i-1}}(x_{s_{1}+\cdots+s_{i-1}},\ldots,x_{s_{1}+\cdots+s_{i-2}+1}),\ldots,F^{s_{1}}(x_{s_{1}},\ldots,x_{1}))\\
&-\sum_{m,n}(-1)^{*_{n}+|T|-1}T^{k-m+1}(x_{k},\ldots,x_{n+m+1},\mu_{\mathcal{C}}^{m}(x_{n+m},\ldots,x_{n+1})x_{n},\ldots,x_{1}),\end{split}

where

(2.14)

\dagger=(|T|-1)(|x_{1}|+\cdots+|x_{s_{1}+\cdots+s_{i-1}}|-s_{1}-\cdots-s_{i-1}),

and

(2.15)

\begin{split}&\mu^{k}_{\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})}(T_{k},\ldots,T_{1})^{k}(x_{k},\ldots,x_{1})\\
=&\sum_{r,i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}s_{1},\ldots,s_{r}\geq 0\\
s_{1}+\cdots+s_{r}=k\end{subarray}}(-1)^{\circ}\mu^{r}_{\mathcal{D}}(F_{k}^{s_{r}}(x_{k},\ldots,x_{k-s_{r}+1}),\ldots,F_{k}^{s_{i_{k}+1}}(\ldots),\\
&T_{k}^{s_{i_{k}}}(\ldots),\ldots,T_{2}^{s_{i_{2}}}(\ldots),F_{1}^{s_{i_{2}-1}}(\ldots),\ldots,F_{1}^{s_{i_{1}+1}}(\ldots),\\
&T_{1}^{s_{i_{1}}}(x_{s_{1}+\cdots+s_{i_{1}}},\ldots,x_{s_{1}+\cdots+s_{i_{1}-1}+1}),F_{0}^{s_{i_{1}-1}}(\ldots),\ldots,F_{0}^{s_{1}}(x_{s_{1}},\ldots,x_{1}))\end{split}

where

(2.16)

\circ=\sum_{p=1}^{k}(|T_{p}|-1)\cdot\maltese_{1;s_{1}+\cdots+s_{i_{p-1}}}

and
1\leq i_{1}\leq i_{2}\leq\cdots\leq i_{k}\leq r
.

A left (resp. right)
A_{\infty}

\mathcal{C}
-module
\mathcal{N}
is an
A_{\infty}
-functor
\mathcal{C}\to\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathbf{k}}}
(resp.
\mathcal{C}^{op}\to\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathbf{k}}}
).
The category
\mathcal{C}\mathrm{\!-\!mod}=\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathbf{k}}})
(resp.
\mathrm{mod\!-\!}\mathcal{C}=\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{op},\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathbf{k}}})
) is called the category of left (resp. right) modules over
\mathcal{C}
, or simply
\mathcal{C}
-modules.
There are Yoneda functors

(2.17)

\begin{split}y^{l}:\mathcal{C}&\to\mathcal{C}\mathrm{\!-\!mod},\\
y^{r}:\mathcal{C}&\to\mathrm{mod\!-\!}\mathcal{C},\end{split}

which are both cohomologically fully faithful (since everything is assumed to be cohomologically unital);
see e.g.
[
46
, §(2g)]
.
The image of an object
X\in\mathrm{ob\ }\mathcal{C}
under these are denoted by
\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X}
and resp.
\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X}
,
called the left and resp. right Yoneda module of
X
.

2.2.
A_{\infty}
mult-functors and multi-modules

An
A_{\infty}

\mathcal{C}-\mathcal{D}
-bimodule
\mathcal{P}
is an
A_{\infty}
-bilinear functor (or a bi-functor)
\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{D}^{op}\to\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathbf{k}}}
(
A_{\infty}
-morphism with two entries in the sense of
[
37
]
).
To give a systematic treatment of bi-functors, bimodules and even more general objects,
we recall the notion of
A_{\infty}
multi-functors by
[
37
]
; see also
[
48
,
27
]
.

Definition 2.3
.

Let
m\geq 1
be a positive integer and
\mathcal{C}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{C}_{m}
and
\mathcal{D}
be
A_{\infty}
-categories.
An
A_{\infty}
multi-functor
F:\mathcal{C}_{1}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{C}_{m}\to\mathcal{D}
consists of a map
F:\prod_{i=1}^{m}\mathrm{ob\ }\mathcal{C}_{i}\to\mathrm{ob\ }\mathcal{D}
,
together with a collection of linear maps

(2.18)

F^{k_{1},\ldots,k_{m}}:\bigotimes_{i=1}^{m}\mathcal{C}_{i}(X_{i,1},\ldots,X_{i,k_{i}})\to\mathcal{D}(F(X_{1,1},\ldots,X_{m,1}),F(X_{1,k_{1}},\ldots,X_{m,k_{m}})),

satisfying the following equations

(2.19)

\begin{split}&\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}s\geq 1\\
i_{j,1}+\cdots+i_{j,s}=k_{j}\end{subarray}}(-1)^{*}\mu_{\mathcal{D}}^{s}(F(x_{1,i_{1,1}+\cdots+i_{1,s}},\ldots,x_{1,i_{1,1}+\cdots+i_{1,s-1}+1};\ldots;x_{m,i_{m,1}+\cdots+i_{m,s}},\ldots,x_{m,i_{m,1}+\cdots+i_{m,s-1}+1}),\\
&\ldots,F(x_{1,i_{1,1}},\ldots,x_{1,1};x_{2,i_{2,1}},\ldots,x_{2,1};\ldots;x_{m,i_{m,1}},\ldots,x_{m,1}))\\
=&\sum_{i,j,r}(-1)^{\maltese}F^{k_{1},\ldots,k_{i}-j+1,\ldots,k_{m}}(x_{1,k_{1}},\ldots;x_{i,k_{i}},\ldots,\mu_{\mathcal{C}_{i}}^{j}(x_{i,r+j},\ldots,x_{i,r+1}),x_{i,r},\ldots,x_{i,1};\ldots;\ldots,x_{m,1}).\end{split}

Here the sign
*
is the Koszul sign associated to re-ordering the elements in the expression to appear in the standard order

x_{1,k_{1}},\ldots,x_{1,1};\ldots;x_{m,k_{m}},\ldots,x_{m,1},

and the sign
\maltese
is the Koszul sign similar to (
2.4
),
but obtained by commuting
\mu_{\mathcal{C}_{i}}^{j}
all the way to the right end of the expression.

Let
\mathcal{C}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{C}_{r}
and
\mathcal{D}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{D}_{s}
be
A_{\infty}
-categories.
For notational simplicity we denote them by ordered tuples of
A_{\infty}
-categories

(2.20)

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{\mathcal{C}}

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{C}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{C}_{r}),

(2.21)

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{\mathcal{D}}

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{D}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{D}_{s}).

Definition 2.4
.

An
A_{\infty}

\overrightarrow{\mathcal{C}}-\overrightarrow{\mathcal{D}}
multi-module is an
A_{\infty}
multi-linear functor

(2.22)

\mathcal{A}:\prod_{i=1}^{r}\mathcal{C}_{i}\times\prod_{j=1}^{s}\mathcal{D}^{op}_{i}\to\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathbf{k}}}.

To make formulas for bimodule structure maps look concise and clean, we introduce some notations.
Let
k,l\geq 0
be non-negative integers.
For
x_{i}\in\mathcal{C}(X_{i-1},X_{i}),x^{\prime}_{s}\in\mathcal{D}(Y_{s},Y_{s-1})
,
define

(2.23)

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}_{i;j}

\displaystyle=x_{j}\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{i},\vskip 14.22636pt1\leq i\leq j\leq k,

(2.24)

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{s;t}

\displaystyle=x^{\prime}_{s}\otimes\cdots\otimes x^{\prime}_{t},\vskip 14.22636pt1\leq s\leq t\leq l,

where if
i>j
or
k=0
,
\mathbf{x}_{i;j}
is understood to be empty,
if
s>t
or
t=0
,
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{s;t}
is understood to be empty.

Definition 2.5
.

The diagonal bimodule
\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}
is the canonical
\mathcal{C}-\mathcal{C}
bimodule associate with an
A_{\infty}
-category
\mathcal{C}
,
with underlying complex
\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}(X,Y)=\mathcal{C}(Y,X)
and bimodule structure maps,

(2.25)

\mu^{k,l}_{\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}}:\mathcal{C}(X_{0},\ldots,X_{k})\otimes\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}(X_{0},Y_{0})\otimes\mathcal{C}(Y_{l},\ldots,Y_{0})\to\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}(X_{k},Y_{l})

(2.26)

\mu^{k,l}_{\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}}(\mathbf{x}_{1;k},c,\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;l})=(-1)^{\maltese^{\prime}_{1;l}+1}\mu_{\mathcal{C}}^{k+l+1}(\mathbf{x}_{1;k},c,\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;l}),

where
x_{i}\in\mathcal{C}(X_{i-1},X_{i}),y^{\prime}_{j}\in\mathcal{C}(Y_{j},Y_{j-1})=\mathcal{C}^{op}(Y_{j-1},Y_{j})
, and

(2.27)

\maltese^{\prime}_{1;l}=\sum_{j=1}^{l}|x^{\prime}_{j}|-l.

Definition 2.6
.

The linear dual bimodule
\mathcal{C}^{\vee}
is the
\mathcal{C}-\mathcal{C}
bimodule whose underlying complex is

(2.28)

\mathcal{C}^{\vee}(X,Y)=\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{C}^{-*}(X,Y),{\mathbf{k}})

with the opposite grading on
\mathcal{C}(X,Y)=\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}(Y,X)
,
and bimodule structure maps induced by the
A_{\infty}
-structure maps of
\mathcal{C}
in the following way

(2.29)

\mu^{k,l}_{\mathcal{C}^{\vee}}:\mathcal{C}(X_{0},\ldots,X_{k})\otimes\mathcal{C}^{\vee}(X_{0},Y_{0})\otimes\mathcal{C}(Y_{l},\ldots,Y_{0})\to\mathcal{C}^{\vee}(X_{k},Y_{l})

(2.30)

\mu^{k,l}_{\mathcal{C}^{\vee}}(\mathbf{x}_{1;k},f,\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;l})(w)=(-1)^{\maltese_{1;k}\cdot(|f|+\maltese^{\prime}_{1;l}+|w|-1)+|w|-1}f(\mu_{\mathcal{C}}^{k+l+1}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;l},w,\mathbf{x}_{1;k})).

The sign comes from the Koszul sign by moving
\mathbf{x}_{1;k}
on the left hand side of (
2.30
) all the way to the right of the expression,
where one can apply the action of
\mu^{k+l+1}_{\mathcal{C}}
as done on the right hand side of (
2.30
).

Definition 2.7
.

For an object
X\in\mathrm{ob\ }\mathcal{C}
, its left Yoneda module is
\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X}
is the left
\mathcal{C}
-module with chain complexes
\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X}(Y)=\mathcal{C}(X,Y)
and structure maps

(2.31)

\mu^{k}_{\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X}}(\mathbf{x}_{1;k},w)=\mu_{\mathcal{C}}^{k+1}(\mathbf{x}_{1;k},w).

Its right Yoneda module
\mathcal{Y}^{r}_{X}
is the right
\mathcal{C}
-module with chain complexes
\mathcal{Y}^{r}_{X}(Y)=\mathcal{C}(Y,X)
and structure maps

(2.32)

\mu^{l}_{\mathcal{Y}^{r}_{X}}(w,\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;l})=(-1)^{\maltese^{\prime}_{1;l}}\mu_{\mathcal{C}}^{l+1}(w,\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;l}).

Definition 2.8
.

For a
\mathcal{C}_{1}-\mathcal{D}_{1}
bimodule
\mathcal{P}
and a
\mathcal{C}_{2}-\mathcal{D}_{2}
bimodule
\mathcal{Q}
,
the linear tensor product

(2.33)

\mathcal{P}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}\mathcal{Q}

is a
(\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{D}_{1}^{op})-(\mathcal{C}_{2}^{op},\mathcal{D}_{2})
quadmodule,
whose underlying chain complex is

(2.34)

(\mathcal{P}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}\mathcal{Q})(X_{0},Y_{0};X_{1},Y_{1})=\mathcal{P}(X_{0},Y_{0})\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}\mathcal{Q}(X_{1},Y_{1}),

where
X_{i}\in\mathrm{ob\ }\mathcal{C}_{i},Y_{i}\in\mathrm{ob\ }\mathcal{D}_{i}
,
with multi-module structure maps twisted by the usual rule for Koszul signs.

Definition 2.9
.

Let
\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{C}^{op}_{\Delta}
and
\mathcal{Q}=(\mathcal{C}^{op})^{\vee}
,
both
(\mathcal{C}^{op},\mathcal{C}^{op})
-bimodules where
\mathcal{C}^{op}
is the opposite category of
\mathcal{C}
.
We get a
(\mathcal{C}^{op},\mathcal{C}^{op})-(\mathcal{C}^{op},\mathcal{C}^{op})
quadmodule
\mathcal{C}^{op}_{\Delta}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{C}^{op})^{\vee}
,
which by insertion specializes to a
\mathcal{C}^{op}-\mathcal{C}^{op}
bimodule

(2.35)

\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{1}}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{0}})^{\vee}=(\mathcal{C}^{op}_{\Delta}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{C}^{op})^{\vee})(X_{1},-;X_{0},-),

whose underlying chain complex, for
Y_{1},Y_{0}\in\mathrm{ob\ }\mathcal{C}^{op}
, is

(2.36)

(\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{1}}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{0}})^{\vee})(Y_{1},Y_{0})=\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{1}}(Y_{1})\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{0}})^{\vee}(Y_{0})=\mathcal{C}^{op}(X_{1},Y_{1})\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}\mathcal{C}^{op}(X_{0},Y_{0})=\mathcal{C}(Y_{1},X_{1})\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}\mathcal{C}(Y_{0},X_{0}).

Here the notation
\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X}
denotes the left Yoneda
\mathcal{C}^{op}
-module
\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X}(K)=\mathcal{C}^{op}(X,K)=\mathcal{C}(K,X)
.
The bimodule structure maps are induced from the structure maps on the linear tensor product over
{\mathbf{k}}
of Yoneda modules,
which are

(2.37)

\mu^{k,l}_{\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{1}}\otimes(\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{0}})^{\vee}}(\mathbf{x}_{1;k},z\otimes f,\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;l})(w)=\begin{cases}(-1)^{|f|-1}\mu^{1}_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}(z)\otimes f(w)+(-1)^{|w|-1}z\otimes f(\mu^{1}_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}(w)),&\text{ if }k=l=0,\\
(-1)^{|f|-1}\mu^{k+1}_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}(\mathbf{x}_{1;k},z)\otimes f(w),&\text{ if }k>0,l=0,\\
(-1)^{|w|-1}z\otimes f(\mu^{l+1}_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;l},w)),&\text{ if }k=0,l>0,\\
0,&\text{ if }k>0,l>0.\end{cases}

Here
\mathbf{x}_{1;k}
are tensors of composable elements in
\mathcal{C}^{op}
,
and
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;l}
are tensors of composable elements in
(\mathcal{C}^{op})^{op}=\mathcal{C}
.

Definition 2.10
.

For a
\mathcal{C}_{0}-\mathcal{D}_{0}
bimodule
\mathcal{P}
and a
\mathcal{C}_{1}-\mathcal{D}_{1}
bimodule
\mathcal{Q}
,
the space of
{\mathbf{k}}
-linear homomorphisms from
\mathcal{P}
to
\mathcal{Q}
is a
(\mathcal{D}_{1},\mathcal{D}_{0}^{op})-(\mathcal{C}_{0},\mathcal{C}_{1}^{op})
quadmodule,
whose underlying chain complex is

(2.38)

\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})(Y_{1},Y_{0};X_{0},X_{1})=\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{P}(X_{0},Y_{0}),\mathcal{Q}(X_{1},Y_{1})).

Definition 2.11
.

Let
\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{Q}=\mathcal{C}^{op}_{\Delta}
, both
(\mathcal{C}^{op},\mathcal{C}^{op})
-bimodules.
We get a
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}^{op})-(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}^{op})
quadmodule
\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{C}^{op}_{\Delta},\mathcal{C}^{op}_{\Delta})
,
which by insertion specializes to a
\mathcal{C}^{op}-\mathcal{C}^{op}
-bimodule

(2.39)

\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{0}},\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{1}})=\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{C}^{op}_{\Delta},\mathcal{C}^{op}_{\Delta})(-,X_{0};-,X_{1}).

Here the right
\mathcal{C}^{op}
-action acts on the domain of
\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}
, i.e., on
\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{0}}
.
In particular, the chain complex underlying this bimodule is defined, for each
Y_{1},Y_{0}\in\mathcal{C}^{op}
, as

(2.40)

\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{0}},\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{1}})(Y_{1},Y_{0})=\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{0}}(Y_{0}),\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{1}}(Y_{1}))=\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{C}^{op}(X_{0},Y_{0}),\mathcal{C}^{op}(X_{1},Y_{1})).

The bimodule structure maps are

(2.41)

\mu^{k,l}_{\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{0}},\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{1}})}(\mathbf{x}_{1;k},\phi,\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;l})(w)=\begin{cases}(-1)^{|w|-1}\phi(\mu^{1}_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}(w))+(-1)^{|w|-1}\mu^{1}_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}(\phi(w)),&\text{ if }k=l=0,\\
(-1)^{|w|-1}\mu^{k+1}_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}(\mathbf{x}_{1;k},\phi(w)),&\text{ if }k>0,l=0.\\
(-1)^{|w|-1}\phi(\mu^{l+1}_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;l},w)),&\text{ if }k=0,l>0.\\
0,&\text{ if }k>0,l>0.\end{cases}

Remark 2.12
.

The signs are particularly simple because the bimodule structures are induced from the left Yoneda module structures,
whose structure maps are given by (
2.31
) without any signs.

An elementary but important property is that there is a canonical map of quadmodules

(2.42)

i:\mathcal{C}^{op}_{\Delta}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{C}^{op})^{\vee}\to\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{C}^{op}_{\Delta},\mathcal{C}^{op}_{\Delta}),

which specializes to a map of bimodules

(2.43)

i:\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{1}}\otimes(\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{0}})^{\vee}\to\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{0}},\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{1}}),

such that for every pair of objects
Y_{1},Y_{0}\in\mathrm{ob\ }\mathcal{C}^{op}
, the map on chain complexes is

(2.44)

i^{0,0}:(\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{1}}\otimes(\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{0}})^{\vee})(Y_{1},Y_{0})\to(\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{0}},\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{1}}))(Y_{1},Y_{0})

(2.45)

z\otimes f\mapsto i(z\otimes f)=\phi_{z,f},\text{ where }\phi_{z,f}(w)=f(w)z.

The higher order terms of (
2.42
) and (
2.43
) are all zero.

Lemma 2.13
.

The maps
i^{r,s}
with
i^{0,0}
being (
2.44
) and
i^{r,s}=0
for
r>0
or
s>0
form an
A_{\infty}
-bimodule morphism (
2.43
).

Proof.

This is a straightforward computation based on the formulas (
2.37
) and (
2.41
).
One sees that the assignment
z\otimes f\mapsto\phi_{z,f}
intertwines all the bimodule structure maps,
by keeping track of the signs following
|\phi_{z,f}|=|z|+|f|
.
∎

Remark 2.14
.

The map
i^{0,0}
is the canonical inclusion of subspace of finite rank linear homomorphisms to the space of all linear homomorphisms.
Under this identification, the bimodule structures for
\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{1}}\otimes(\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{0}})^{\vee}
and
\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{0}},\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{1}})
are in fact exactly the same,
up to the sign taken care by the sign twist of the map
i
.
In other words,
i
is the strict inclusion of a subspace which is closed under bimodule structure maps of
\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{0}},\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{1}})
.
This is why all higher order terms vanish.

2.3.
Tensor products and duality

Various notions of tensor products play important roles in studying
A_{\infty}
-bimodules and mult-modules.
The first one is tensor product of two multi-modules over a single side, known as the
convolution tensor product
.
To write signs in a consistent manner, we introduce the following notations

(2.46)

\displaystyle\maltese_{i;j}

\displaystyle=\sum_{s=i}^{j}|x_{s}|-(j-i+1),

(2.47)

\displaystyle\maltese^{\prime}_{i;j}

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=i}^{j}|x^{\prime}_{t}|-(j-i+1),

where we notice that
\maltese_{1;i}
is the sign (
2.4
).

Definition 2.15
.

Define the convolution tensor product of a
\mathcal{C}-\mathcal{D}
-bimodule
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{D}-\mathcal{E}
-bimodule
\mathcal{Q}

(2.48)

\mathcal{P}\otimes_{\mathcal{D}}\mathcal{Q}

to be a
\mathcal{C}-\mathcal{E}
-bimodule whose underlying chain complex is

(2.49)

(\mathcal{P}\otimes_{\mathcal{D}}\mathcal{Q})(X,Z)=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}l\\
Y_{0},\ldots,Y_{l}\in\mathrm{ob\ }\mathcal{D}\end{subarray}}\mathcal{P}(X,Y_{0})\otimes\mathcal{D}(Y_{0},Y_{1})\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{D}(Y_{l-1},Y_{l})\otimes\mathcal{B}(Y_{l},Z),

where
X\in\mathrm{ob\ }\mathcal{C},Y_{0},\ldots,Y_{l}\in\mathrm{ob\ }\mathcal{D},Z\in\mathrm{ob\ }\mathcal{E}
.
The grading of an element
p\otimes\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;l}\otimes q
is

(2.50)

|p\otimes\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;l}\otimes q|=|p|+\maltese^{\prime}_{1;l}+|q|.

The structure maps
\mu_{\mathcal{P}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{Q}}^{0,0}
are defined as follows.
The
(0,0)
-th order structure map is

(2.51)

\begin{split}\mu_{\mathcal{P}\otimes_{\mathcal{D}}\mathcal{Q}}^{0,0}=&\sum(-1)^{\maltese^{\prime}_{j+1;m}+|q|-1}\mu_{\mathcal{P}}^{0,j}(p,\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;j})\otimes\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{j+1;m}\otimes q\\
+&\sum p\otimes\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;m-i}\otimes\mu_{\mathcal{Q}}^{i,0}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{m-i+1;m},q)\\
+&\sum(-1)^{\maltese^{\prime}_{i+j+1;m}+|q|-1}p\otimes\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;i}\otimes\mu_{\mathcal{D}}^{j}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{i+1;i+j})\otimes\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{i+j+1;m}\otimes q,\end{split}

(2.52)

\mu_{\mathcal{P}\otimes_{\mathcal{D}}\mathcal{Q}}^{k,0}(\mathbf{x}_{1;k},p,\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;m},q)=\sum(-1)^{\maltese^{\prime}_{j+1;m}+|q|-1}\mu_{\mathcal{P}}^{k,j}(\mathbf{x}_{1;k},p,\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;j})\otimes\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{j+1;m}\otimes q.

(2.53)

\mu_{\mathcal{P}\otimes_{\mathcal{D}}\mathcal{Q}}^{0,l}(p,\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;m},q,\mathbf{y}^{\prime}_{1;l})=\sum p\otimes\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;m-i}\otimes\mu_{\mathcal{Q}}^{i,l}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{m-i+1;m},q,\mathbf{y}^{\prime}_{1;l})

and
\mu_{\mathcal{P}\otimes_{\mathcal{D}}\mathcal{Q}}^{k,l}=0
for
k>0,l>0
.

There is another way of forming a two-sided tensor product,
which gives rise to a chain complex, defined as follows.

Definition 2.16
.

For a
\mathcal{C}-\mathcal{D}
-bimodule
\mathcal{P}
and a
\mathcal{D}-\mathcal{C}
-bimodule
\mathcal{Q}
,
their bimodule tensor product

(2.54)

\mathcal{P}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}-\mathcal{D}}\mathcal{Q}

is defined to be the chain complex whose underlying graded vector space is

(2.55)

\begin{split}\mathcal{P}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}-\mathcal{D}}\mathcal{Q}=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\geq 0\\
X_{0},\ldots,X_{k}\in\mathrm{ob\ }\mathcal{C}\end{subarray}}\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}l\geq 0\\
Y_{0},\ldots,Y_{l}\in\mathrm{ob\ }\mathcal{D}\end{subarray}}&\mathcal{P}(X_{0},Y_{0})\otimes\mathcal{D}(Y_{0},Y_{1})\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{D}(Y_{l-1},Y_{l})\\
&\otimes\mathcal{Q}(Y_{l},X_{k})\otimes\mathcal{C}(X_{k-1},X_{k})\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{C}(X_{0},X_{1}),\end{split}

and whose differential is

(2.56)

\begin{split}&d_{\mathcal{P}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}-\mathcal{D}}\mathcal{Q}}(p\otimes\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;l}\otimes q\otimes\mathbf{x}_{1;k})\\
=&\sum(-1)^{\#_{r,s}}\mu_{\mathcal{P}}^{k-r,l-s}(\mathbf{x}_{1;k-r},p,\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;l-s})\otimes\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{l-s+1;l}\otimes q\otimes\mathbf{x}_{k-r+1;k}\\
+&\sum(-1)^{\maltese_{1;k}+|q|+\maltese^{\prime}_{i+j+1;l}}p\otimes\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;i}\otimes\mu_{\mathcal{D}}^{j}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{i+1;i+j})\otimes\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{i+j+1;l}\otimes q\otimes\mathbf{x}_{1;k}\\
+&\sum(-1)^{\maltese_{1;k-r}}p\otimes\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;l-s}\otimes\mu_{\mathcal{Q}}^{s,r}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{l-s+1;l},q,\mathbf{x}_{k-r+1;k})\otimes\mathbf{x}_{1;k-r}\\
+&\sum(-1)^{\maltese_{1;i}}p\otimes\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;l}\otimes q\otimes\mathbf{x}_{i+j+1;k}\otimes\mu^{j}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathbf{x}_{i+1;i+j})\otimes\mathbf{x}_{1;i},\end{split}

where

(2.57)

\#_{r,s}=\maltese_{1;k-r}\cdot(|p|+\maltese^{\prime}_{1;l}+|q|+\maltese_{k-r+1;k})+\maltese^{\prime}_{l-s+1;l}+|q|-1+\maltese_{k-r+1;k}.

For any
\mathcal{C}
-bimodule
\mathcal{P}
, there is a canonical collapse map

(2.58)

\mu_{\Delta,\mathcal{P}}:\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{P}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathcal{P},

defined by

(2.59)

\mu^{s;k,l}_{\Delta,\mathcal{P}}(x_{k},\ldots,x_{1},c,z_{1},\ldots,z_{s},p,y_{1},\ldots,y_{l})=(-1)^{\circ^{s}_{-l}}\mu_{\mathcal{P}}^{k+s+1,l}(x_{k},\ldots,x_{1},c,z_{1},\ldots,z_{s},p,y_{1},\ldots,y_{l}),

where

(2.60)

\circ^{s}_{-l}=\sum_{i=1}^{s}|z_{i}|-s+|p|-1+\sum_{j=1}^{l}|y_{j}|-l.

There is a similar collapse map

(2.61)

\nu_{\Delta,\mathcal{P}}:\mathcal{P}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathcal{P}

with the Koszul sign from elements to the right hand side of the input from
\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}
.

Lemma 2.17

(
[
29
, Proposition 2.2]
,
[
28
, Lemma 2.6]
)
.

The collapse maps
\mu_{\Delta,\mathcal{P}}
(
2.58
) and
\nu_{\Delta,\mathcal{P}}
(
2.61
) are quasi-isomorphisms.

∎

The
bimodule dual

\mathcal{P}^{!}
is defined to be

(2.62)

\mathcal{P}^{!}=\hom_{\mathcal{C}\mathrm{\!-\!mod\!-\!}\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{P}^{!},\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}).

If
\mathcal{P}
is perfect so is
\mathcal{P}^{!}
, and there is a natural quasi-isomorphism

(2.63)

\mathcal{P}^{!}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}-\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{Q}\simeq\hom_{\mathcal{C}\mathrm{\!-\!mod\!-\!}\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q}).

For an
A_{\infty}
-category
\mathcal{C}
, its
inverse dualizing bimodule
is defined to be

(2.64)

\mathcal{C}^{!}:=\hom_{\mathcal{C}\mathrm{\!-\!mod\!-\!}\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{C}_{\Delta},\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}).

2.4.
Hochschild invariants

Let
\mathcal{C}
be an
A_{\infty}
-category, and
\mathcal{P}
an
A_{\infty}

\mathcal{C}
-bimodule.
The Hochschild chain complex of
\mathcal{C}
with coefficients in
\mathcal{P}
is defined to be

(2.65)

\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P})=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\geq 0\\
X_{0},\ldots,X_{k}\in\mathrm{ob\ }\mathcal{C}\end{subarray}}\mathcal{P}(X_{0},X_{k})\otimes\mathcal{C}(X_{k-1},X_{k})\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{C}(X_{0},X_{1})

with grading

(2.66)

|p\otimes\mathbf{x}_{1;k}|=|p|+\maltese_{1;k},

where
\mathbf{x}_{1;k}
is (
2.23
).
For the ease in keeping track of grading,
we may also write it using a shifted complex:

(2.67)

\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P})=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\geq 0\\
X_{0},\ldots,X_{k}\in\mathrm{ob\ }\mathcal{C}\end{subarray}}\mathcal{P}(X_{0},X_{k})\otimes\mathcal{C}[1](X_{k-1},X_{k})\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{C}[1](X_{0},X_{1}).

The differential on
\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P})
is called the
Hochschild chain differential
,
defined by

(2.68)

\begin{split}d_{\mathrm{CC}_{*}}(p\otimes\mathbf{x}_{1;k})=&\sum(-1)^{\star_{i}^{j}}\mu^{i,j}_{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbf{x}_{1;i},p,\mathbf{x}_{k-j+1;k})\otimes\mathbf{x}_{i+1;k-j}\\
+&\sum(-1)^{\maltese_{1}^{i}}p\otimes\mathbf{x}_{i+j+1;k}\otimes\mu^{j}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathbf{x}_{i+1;i+j})\otimes\mathbf{x}_{1;i},\end{split}

where

(2.69)

\star_{i}^{j}=\maltese_{1;i}\cdot(|p|+\maltese_{i+1;k})+\maltese_{i+1;k-j}.

The Hochschild cochain complex of
\mathcal{C}
with coefficients in
\mathcal{P}
is defined to be

(2.70)

\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P})=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\geq 0\\
X_{0},\ldots,X_{k}\in\mathrm{ob\ }\mathcal{C}\end{subarray}}\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{C}(X_{k-1},X_{k})\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{C}(X_{0},X_{1}),\mathcal{P}(X_{k},X_{0}))

with grading

(2.71)

\mathrm{CC}^{s}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P})=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\geq 0\\
X_{0},\ldots,X_{k}\in\mathrm{ob\ }\mathcal{C}\end{subarray}}\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{C}(X_{k-1},X_{k})\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{C}(X_{0},X_{1}),\mathcal{P}(X_{k},X_{0})[k-s])

where
\hom^{0}_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\cdot,\cdot)
denotes the subspace of degree zero maps between graded modules.
The differential on
\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P})
is called the
Hochschild cochain differential
is defined as follows.

(2.72)

\begin{split}d_{\mathrm{CC}^{*}}(\phi)^{k}(\mathbf{x}_{1;k})=&\sum(-1)^{\dagger_{s}}\mu_{\mathcal{P}}^{r,s}(\mathbf{x}_{k-r+1;k},\phi^{k-r-s}(\mathbf{x}_{s+1;k-r}),\mathbf{x}_{1;s})\\
-&\sum(-1)^{\maltese_{1;i}}\phi^{k-j+1}(\mathbf{x}_{i+j+1;k},\mu_{\mathcal{C}}^{j}(\mathbf{x}_{i+1;i+j}),\mathbf{x}_{1;i}).\end{split}

where

(2.73)

\dagger_{s}=(|\phi|-1)\maltese_{1;s}.

Lemma 2.18
.

Let
F,G:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
be
A_{\infty}
-functors. Then we have a strict equality of chain complexes

(2.74)

\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C},(F,G)^{*}\mathcal{D}_{\Delta})=\hom_{\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})}(F,G),

where the right hand side is the morphism space in the functor category
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
carrying the differential
\mu^{1}_{\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})}
(
2.13
).

Proof.

This follows immediately from the definition of the morphism space in
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
and the definition of the Hochschild cochain complex (
2.70
) applied to the pullback diagonal bimodule
(F,G)^{*}\mathcal{D}_{\Delta}
.
∎

There is a canonical quasi-isomorphism of chain complexes

(2.75)

\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}-\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{P}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P})

induced by the collapse quasi-isomorphism (
2.58
).
There is also a canonical chain map

(2.76)

\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P})\to\hom_{\mathcal{C}\mathrm{\!-\!mod\!-\!}\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{C}_{\Delta},\mathcal{P})

This map is a quasi-isomorphism if
\mathcal{C}
is cohomologically unital (
[
29
, Proposition 2.5]
).

Lemma 2.19
.

Let
\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q}
be
\mathcal{C}-\mathcal{C}
-bimodules.
Then the differential
d_{\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{Q})}
on
\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{Q})
defined by (
2.68
) applied to the bimodule
\mathcal{P}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{Q}
with bimodule structure maps (
2.51
), (
2.52
), (
2.53
) exactly agrees with the differential
d_{\mathcal{P}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}-\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{Q}}
(
2.56
).

Proof.

This follows from the definition of the bimodules structure maps for
\mathcal{P}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{Q}
in (
2.51
), (
2.52
), (
2.53
),
and the general formula (
2.68
) for differential on the Hochschild chain complex.

First recall from (
2.50
) that an element
p\otimes\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;l}\otimes q\in\mathcal{P}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{Q}
has grading

|p\otimes\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;l}\otimes q|=|p|+\maltese^{\prime}_{1;l}+|q|.

Since the bimodule structure maps
\mu^{r,s}
for
\mathcal{P}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{Q}
with
r>0,s>0
are all zero,
we find that the first term on the right hand side of (
2.68
) becomes

(2.77)

\begin{split}&(-1)^{\maltese_{1;k}}\mu_{\mathcal{P}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{Q}}^{0,0}(p\otimes\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;l}\otimes q)\otimes\mathbf{x}_{1;k}\\
+&\sum_{r>0}(-1)^{\maltese_{1;r}\cdot(|p|+\maltese^{\prime}_{1,l}+|q|+\maltese_{r+1;k})+\maltese_{r+1;k}}\mu_{\mathcal{P}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{Q}}^{r,0}(\mathbf{x}_{1;r},p\otimes\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;l}\otimes q)\otimes\mathbf{x}_{r+1;k}\\
+&\sum_{s>0}(-1)^{\maltese_{1;k-s}}\mu_{\mathcal{P}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{Q}}^{0,s}(p\otimes\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;l}\otimes q,\mathbf{x}_{k-s+1;k})\otimes\mathbf{x}_{1;k-s}.\end{split}

Now using (
2.51
), (
2.52
), (
2.53
),
we may further write these terms as

(2.78)

\begin{split}&(-1)^{\maltese_{1;k}}\mu_{\mathcal{P}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{Q}}^{0,0}(p\otimes\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;l}\otimes q)\otimes\mathbf{x}_{1;k}\\
=&\sum(-1)^{\maltese_{1;k}+\maltese^{\prime}_{j+1;l}+|q|-1}\mu_{\mathcal{P}}^{0,j}(p,\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;j})\otimes\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{j+1;l}\otimes q\\
+&\sum(-1)^{\maltese_{1;k}}p\otimes\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;l-i}\otimes\mu_{\mathcal{Q}}^{i,0}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{l-i+1;l},q)\\
+&\sum(-1)^{\maltese_{1;k}+\maltese^{\prime}_{i+j+1;l}}p\otimes\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;i}\otimes\mu_{\mathcal{D}}^{j}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{i+1;i+j})\otimes\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{i+j+1;m}\otimes q,\end{split}

(2.79)

\begin{split}&\sum_{r>0}(-1)^{\maltese_{1;r}\cdot(|p|+\maltese^{\prime}_{1,l}+|q|+\maltese_{r+1;k})+\maltese_{r+1;k}}\mu_{\mathcal{P}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{Q}}^{r,0}(\mathbf{x}_{1;r},p\otimes\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;l}\otimes q)\otimes\mathbf{x}_{r+1;k}\\
=&\sum_{r>0,j}(-1)^{\maltese_{1;r}\cdot(|p|+\maltese^{\prime}_{1,l}+|q|+\maltese_{r+1;k})+\maltese_{r+1;k}+\maltese^{\prime}_{j+1;l}+|q|-1}\mu_{\mathcal{P}}^{r,j}(\mathbf{x}_{1;r},p,\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;j})\otimes\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{j+1;l}\otimes q\end{split}

and

(2.80)

\begin{split}&\sum_{s>0}(-1)^{\maltese_{1;k-s}}\mu_{\mathcal{P}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{Q}}^{0,s}(p\otimes\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;l}\otimes q,\mathbf{x}_{k-s+1;k})\otimes\mathbf{x}_{1;k-s}\\
=&\sum_{s>0,i}(-1)^{\maltese_{1;k-s}}p\otimes\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;l-i}\otimes\mu_{\mathcal{Q}}^{i,s}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{l-i+1;l},q,\mathbf{x}_{k-s+1;k})\otimes\mathbf{x}_{k-s;1}.\end{split}

The second term on the right hand side of (
2.68
) applied to the bimodule
\mathcal{P}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{Q}
is

(2.81)

\sum(-1)^{\maltese_{1}^{i}}p\otimes\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;l}\otimes q\otimes\mathbf{x}_{i+j+1;k}\otimes\mu^{j}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathbf{x}_{i+1;i+j})\otimes\mathbf{x}_{1;i}.

Now add the terms (
2.78
), (
2.79
), (
2.80
) and (
2.81
),
and we get exactly (
2.56
).
∎

Corollary 2.20
.

As chain complexes,
\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{Q})=\mathcal{P}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}-\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{Q}
,
with differentials carrying the same signs.

Definition 2.21
.

We say that a tensor
p\otimes\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;l}\otimes q\otimes\mathbf{x}_{1;k}
in the two-sided bar complex
\mathcal{P}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}-\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{Q}
has length zero in the
\mathbf{x}
-entries and the
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}
-entries, if
k=l=0
.
The following statement is helpful in clarifying some important terms in the formula (
2.56
).

Corollary 2.22
.

The terms in the output of the differential
d_{\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{Q})}
that have length zero in the
\mathbf{x}
-entries and the
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}
-entries are

(2.82)

(-1)^{\maltese_{1;k}\cdot(|p|+\maltese^{\prime}_{1;l}+|q|)+|q|-1}\mu_{\mathcal{P}}^{k,l}(\mathbf{x}_{1;k},p,\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;l})\otimes q+p\otimes\mu_{\mathcal{Q}}^{l,k}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;l},q,\mathbf{x}_{1;k}).

We can also define Hochschild chain and cochain complexes with coefficients in multi-modules.
Fix an
A_{\infty}
-category
\mathcal{C}
.
Let

(2.83)

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{\mathcal{C}}_{1}

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{C}_{1,1},\ldots,\mathcal{C}_{1,r}),

(2.84)

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{\mathcal{C}}_{2}

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{C}_{2,1},\ldots,\mathcal{C}_{2,s})

be ordered tuples of
A_{\infty}
-categories,
and
\mathcal{P}
a
\overrightarrow{\mathcal{C}}_{1}-\overrightarrow{\mathcal{C}}_{2}
multi-module.
Suppose for some
1\leq i\leq r,1\leq j\leq s
we have
\mathcal{C}_{1,i}=\mathcal{C}_{2,j}=\mathcal{C}
.
Denote by

(2.85)

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{\mathcal{C}}_{1}^{(i)}

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{C}_{1,1},\ldots,\widehat{\mathcal{C}_{1,i}},\ldots,\mathcal{C}_{1,r}),

(2.86)

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{\mathcal{C}}_{2}^{(j)}

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{C}_{2,1},\ldots,\widehat{\mathcal{C}_{2,j}},\ldots,\mathcal{C}_{2,s}),

the ordered tuples of
A_{\infty}
-categories with the particular entries removed.

Definition 2.23
.

The Hochschild chain complex of
\mathcal{C}
with cofficients in
\mathcal{P}

(2.87)

\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P})

is defined to be the
\overrightarrow{\mathcal{C}}_{1}^{(i)}-\overrightarrow{\mathcal{C}}_{2}^{(j)}
multi-module whose underlying chain complex is

(2.88)

\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P})(\vec{X}_{1}^{i,-};\vec{X}_{2}^{j,-})=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\geq 0\\
X_{0},\ldots,X_{k}\in\mathrm{ob\ }\mathcal{C}\end{subarray}}\mathcal{P}(\vec{X}_{1}^{i,+,0};\vec{X}_{2}^{j,+,k})\otimes\mathcal{C}(X_{k-1},X_{k})\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{C}(X_{0},X_{1}),

where

(2.89)

\displaystyle\vec{X}_{1}^{i,-}=(X_{1,1;0},\ldots,X_{1,i-1;0},X_{1,i+1;0},\ldots,X_{1,r;0})\in\prod_{t\neq i}\mathrm{ob\ }\mathcal{C}_{1,t},

(2.90)

\displaystyle\vec{X}_{2}^{j,-}=(X_{2,1;0},\ldots,X_{2,j-1;0},X_{1,j+1;0},\ldots,X_{2,s;0})\in\prod_{t\neq j}\mathrm{ob\ }\mathcal{C}_{2,t},

and

(2.91)

\displaystyle\vec{X}_{1}^{i,+,0}=(X_{1,1;0},\ldots,X_{1,i-1;0},X_{1,i;0}=X_{0},X_{1,i+1;0},\ldots,X_{1,r;0})\in\prod_{t=1}^{r}\mathrm{ob\ }\mathcal{C}_{1,t},

(2.92)

\displaystyle\vec{X}_{2}^{j,+,k}=(X_{2,1;0},\ldots,X_{2,j-1;0},X_{2,j;0}=X_{k},X_{1,j+1;0},\ldots,X_{2,s;0})\in\prod_{t=1}^{s}\mathrm{ob\ }\mathcal{C}_{2,t}.

The structure maps are defined as follows.
For

(2.93)

\displaystyle\vec{k}_{i}=(k_{1},\ldots,k_{i-1},k_{i+1},\ldots,k_{r}),

(2.94)

\displaystyle\vec{l}_{j}=(l_{1},\ldots,l_{j-1},l_{j+1},\ldots,l_{s}),

we put

(2.95)

\displaystyle\vec{k}_{i,h}=(k_{1},\ldots,k_{i-1},h,k_{i+1},\ldots,k_{r}),

(2.96)

\displaystyle\vec{l}_{j,h}=(l_{1},\ldots,l_{j-1},h,l_{j+1},\ldots,l_{s}).

The
(\vec{0};\vec{0})
-th term of the structure maps is the Hochschild differential defined by

(2.97)

\mu_{\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P})}^{\vec{0};\vec{0}}:\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P})(\vec{X}_{1}^{(i)},\vec{X}_{2}^{(j)})\to\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P})(\vec{X}_{1}^{(i)},\vec{X}_{2}^{(j)})[-1]

by

(2.98)

\begin{split}\mu_{\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P})}^{\vec{0};\vec{0}}(p\otimes\mathbf{x}_{1;k})&=\sum(-1)^{\dagger}\mu_{\mathcal{P}}^{\vec{0}_{i,h};\vec{0}_{j,l}}(\mathbf{x}_{1;h};p;\mathbf{x}_{k-l+1;k})\otimes\mathbf{x}_{1;k-l}\\
&+\sum(-1)^{\maltese_{m}}p\otimes\mathbf{x}_{m+d+1;k}\otimes\mu^{d}(\mathbf{x}_{m+1;m+d})\otimes\mathbf{x}_{1;m}.\end{split}

Here the symbols are

(2.99)

\vec{0}_{i,h}=0,\ldots,0,h,0,\ldots,0,\text{ where }h\text{ appears at the }i\text{-th entry},

and similarly for
\vec{0}_{j,l}
.
The higher order multi-module structure maps are induced from the original multi-module structure maps for
\mathcal{P}
,

(2.100)

\mu_{\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P})}^{\vec{k}_{i};\vec{l}_{j}}(\vec{\mathbf{x}}_{1};p\otimes\mathbf{x}_{1;k};\vec{\mathbf{x}}_{2})=\sum(-1)^{\dagger}\mu^{\vec{k}_{i,h};\vec{l}_{j,l}}(\vec{\mathbf{x}}_{1}^{1,h,+};p;\vec{\mathbf{x}}_{2}^{k-l+1,k,+})\otimes\mathbf{x}_{1;k-l}.

Here the symbol
\vec{\mathbf{x}}_{1}^{1,h,+}
is obtained by inserting the tensor
\mathbf{x}_{1;h}\in\mathcal{C}_{1,i}(\ldots)
into the tensor
\vec{\mathbf{x}}_{1}\in\mathcal{C}_{1,1}(\ldots)\otimes\cdots\mathcal{C}_{1,i-1}(\ldots)\otimes\mathcal{C}_{1,i+1}(\ldots)\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{C}_{1,r}(\ldots)
between the
\mathcal{C}_{1,i-1}
and
\mathcal{C}_{1,i+1}
entries.

Definition 2.24
.

The Hochschild cochain complex of
\mathcal{C}
with coefficients in
\mathcal{P}

(2.101)

\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P})

is defined to be the
\overrightarrow{\mathcal{C}}_{1}^{(i)}-\overrightarrow{\mathcal{C}}_{2}^{(j)}
multi-module whose underlying chain complex is

(2.102)

(\vec{X}_{1}^{i,-};\vec{X}_{2}^{j,-})=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\geq 0\\
X_{0},\ldots,X_{k}\in\mathrm{ob\ }\mathcal{C}\end{subarray}}\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{C}(X_{k-1},X_{k})\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{C}(X_{0},X_{1}),\mathcal{P}(\vec{X}_{1}^{i,+,k};\vec{X}_{2}^{j,+,0})).

The structure maps are defined in a way similar to those on the Hochschild chain complex.

2.5.
Operations on Hochschild complexes

The Hochschild cochain complex
\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C})=\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{\Delta})
with diagonal bimodule coefficient carries has carries a cup product

(2.103)

\cup:\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{\Delta})\otimes\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{\Delta})\to\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}),

defined by

(2.104)

(\phi_{2}\cup\phi_{1})^{k}(\mathbf{x}_{1;k})=\sum(-1)^{\diamond}\mu^{k-r-s+2}(\mathbf{x}_{j+r+1;k},\phi_{2}^{r}(\mathbf{x}_{j+1;j+r}),\mathbf{x}_{i+s+1;j},\phi_{1}^{s}(\mathbf{x}_{i+1;i+s}),\mathbf{x}_{1;i}),

where

(2.105)

\diamond=(|\phi_{2}|-1)\cdot(\maltese_{i+s+1;j}+|\phi_{1}|+\maltese_{1;i})+(|\phi_{1}|-1)\cdot\maltese_{1;i}.

The cup product can be regarded as as consequence of the following family of binary operations on Hochschild cochain complexes with arbitrary bimodules

(2.106)

\sqcup:\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P})\otimes\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{Q})\to\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{Q}).

Now if
\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{Q}
and there is a morphism of bimodules
\mathcal{P}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{P}
,
we can define a product on
\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P})
by composing (
2.106
) with the induced map on Hochschild cochain complexes by the bimodule morphism.
The discovery of the map (
2.106
) actually dates back to early work of Eilenberg and MacLane
[
24
]
(in the case of group algebras) and Gerstenhaber
[
32
]
(in the case of associative algebras).
For
\phi\in\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P}),\psi\in\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{Q})
, their product

(2.107)

\phi\sqcup\psi

is defined to be the element of
\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{Q})
such that for every
\mathbf{x}_{1;m}
,

(2.108)

(\phi\sqcup\psi)^{k}(\mathbf{x}_{1;k})=\sum_{i\leq j}\phi^{k-j+1}(\mathbf{x}_{j;k})\otimes\mathbf{x}_{i+1;j}\otimes\psi^{i}(\mathbf{x}_{1;i}).

Proposition 2.25
.

The map
\sqcup
(
2.106
) defined by (
2.108
) is a chain map.

Proof.

This is a straightforward computation based on the definitions of the Hochschild cochain differential (
2.72
) and the structure maps on the convolution tensor product.
∎

In the case where
\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{Q}=\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}
, the quasi-isomorphism of bimodules
\mu_{\Delta,\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}}:\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}\to\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}
given by (
2.58
) induces a map on Hochschild cochain complexes

\mu_{\Delta,\mathcal{C}_{\Delta},*}:\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{C}_{\Delta})\to\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}),

such that the overall composition

\mu_{\Delta,\mathcal{C}_{\Delta},*}\circ\sqcup:\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{\Delta})\otimes\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{\Delta})\to\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{\Delta})

is the usual cup product on Hochschild cohomology with diagonal coefficients.

There is also a cap product between a Hochschild cochain complex with diagonal bimodule coefficient and a Hochschild chain complex with arbitrary bimodule coefficient:

(2.109)

\cap:\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{\Delta})\otimes\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{Q})\to\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{Q}),

defined by

(2.110)

\phi\cap(q\otimes\mathbf{x})=\sum(-1)^{\$}\mu_{\mathcal{P}}^{j,k-j-r+1}(\mathbf{x}_{1;j},q,\mathbf{x}_{k-i+1;k},\phi^{r}(\mathbf{x}_{k-i-r+1;k-i}),\mathbf{x}_{s+1;k-i-r})\otimes\mathbf{x}_{j+1;s}

where

(2.111)

\$=(|\phi|-1)\maltese_{1;k-i-r}+\maltese_{1;j}\cdot(|q|+|\phi|+\maltese_{j+1;k})+\maltese_{j+1;s}.

Th cap product can also be realized by a more general kind of operations:

(2.112)

\sqcap:\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P})\otimes\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{Q})\to\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{Q}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{P})

defined by

(2.113)

\phi\sqcap(q\otimes\mathbf{x}_{1;k})=\sum(-1)^{\dagger}q\otimes\mathbf{x}_{i+r+1;k}\otimes\phi^{r}(\mathbf{x}_{i+1;i+r})\otimes\mathbf{x}_{1;i},

where the sign

(2.114)

\dagger=(|\phi|-1)\maltese_{1,i}

comes from standard Koszul sign twist similar to the one in (
2.72
).
In the case where
\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}
, the composition of (
2.112
) with the map
\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{Q}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{C}_{\Delta})\to\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{Q})
induced by the collapse map
\nu_{\Delta,\mathcal{Q}}:\mathcal{Q}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathcal{Q}
(
2.61
) gives the cap product
\cap
(
2.109
).
From this point of view, there is another cap product

(2.115)

\cap:\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P})\otimes\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{\Delta})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sqcup}}{{\to}}\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{P})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mu_{\Delta,\mathcal{P}}}}{{\to}}\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P}),

but there is no module structure to speak of since
\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P})
is not generally a differential graded algebra.

Similar to the classical case of associative algebras,
the cup/cap products (
2.103
), (
2.109
) makes
\mathrm{HH}_{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P})
a module over the algebra
\mathrm{HH}^{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{\Delta})
(
[
29
, Proposition 2.4]
).
For the operations
\sqcup
(
2.106
) and
\sqcap
(
2.112
),
they also satisfy a similar associativity relation,
which leads to the following statement.

Proposition 2.26
.

The map
\sqcap
(
2.112
) defined by (
2.113
) is a chain map.
Moreover, we have

(2.116)

(\phi\sqcup\psi)\sqcap\gamma=\phi\sqcap(\psi\sqcap\gamma),

in
\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{Q}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{R})
,
where
\phi\in\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P}),\psi\in\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{Q}),\gamma\in\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{R})
.

Proof.

The proof is a standard computation using the formulas (
2.108
) (
2.113
),
in which no application of
\mu
’s are involved.
∎

2.6.
Calabi-Yau structures

In this subsection, we review the definition of Calabi-Yau structures on
A_{\infty}
-categories following
[
33
]
.
See also
[
11
]
for definitions on relative Calabi-Yau structures.
We write
\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{C})_{hS^{1}}
and resp.
\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{C})^{hS^{1}}
for the homotopy orbit complex and resp. homotopy fixed point complex for the (non-unital) Hochschild chain complex for
\mathcal{C}
,
which compute the positive cyclic homology
\mathrm{HC}_{*}^{+}(\mathcal{C})
and resp. negative cyclic homology
\mathrm{HC}_{*}^{-}(\mathcal{C})
.
In this paper, we are not going to prove results concerning strong Calabi-Yau structures,
so we will not specify these chain models for
S^{1}
-complexes;
see
[
10
,
21
,
31
,
56
]
for various models.

Definition 2.27
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be an
A_{\infty}
-category.
A weak proper Calabi-Yau structure of dimension
n
is a chain map
\phi:\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{C})\to{\mathbf{k}}[-n]
of degree
-n
,
such that the composition

(2.117)

\mathcal{C}^{*}(X,Y)\otimes\mathcal{C}^{n-*}(Y,X)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mu_{\mathcal{C}}^{2}}}{{\to}}\mathcal{C}^{n}(Y,Y)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i}}{{\to}}\mathrm{CC}_{n}(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\phi}}{{\to}}{\mathbf{k}}

induced a nondegenerate pairing on cohomology:

(2.118)

H^{*}(\mathcal{C}(X,Y))\otimes H^{n-*}(\mathcal{C}(Y,X))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle[\mu_{\mathcal{C}}^{2}]}}{{\to}}H^{n}(\mathcal{C}(Y,Y))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle[i]}}{{\to}}\mathrm{HH}_{n}(\mathcal{C})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle[\phi]}}{{\to}}{\mathbf{k}}.

A strong proper Calabi-Yau structure is a chain map
\tilde{\phi}:\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{C})_{hS^{1}}\to{\mathbf{k}}[-n]
such that
\tilde{\phi}\circ pr
is weak proper Calabi-Yau structure,
where
\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{C})_{hS^{1}}
is the homotopy orbit complex computing positive cyclic homology,
and
pr:\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{C})_{hS^{1}}
is the projection to homotopy orbits.

Suppose
\mathcal{C}
is smooth. A weak smooth Calabi-Yau structure of dimension
n
on
\mathcal{C}
is a chain map
\sigma:{\mathbf{k}}[n]\to\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{C})
, or equivalent a cycle
\sigma\in\mathrm{CC}_{-n}(\mathcal{C})
,
such that the induced map of bimodules

(2.119)

\mathcal{C}^{!}\to\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}[-n]

is a quasi-isomorphism.

A strong smooth Calabi-Yau structure of dimension
n
on
\mathcal{C}
is a chain map
\tilde{\sigma}:{\mathbf{k}}[n]\to\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{C})^{hS_{1}}
,
or equivalently a cycle
\tilde{\sigma}\in\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{C})^{hS_{1}}[-n]

such that
\sigma=i(\tilde{\sigma})
is a weak Calabi-Yau structure of dimension
n
,
where
i:\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{C})^{hS_{1}}
is the inclusion of homotopy fixed points.

Let
\mathcal{P}
be any
\mathcal{C}
-bimodule.
Given any Hochschild chain
\sigma\in\mathrm{CC}_{-n}(\mathcal{C})=\mathrm{CC}_{-n}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{\Delta})
, we can use the operation
\sqcap
(
2.112
) to define a chain map

(2.120)

-\cap\sigma:\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle-\sqcap\sigma}}{{\to}}\mathrm{CC}_{*-n}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{P})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mu_{\Delta,\mathcal{P},*}}}{{\to}}\mathrm{CC}_{*-n}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P}),

where
\mu_{\Delta,\mathcal{P}}
is the collapse map (
2.58
), which is a quasi-isomorphism of bimodules.
The condition for
\sigma
being a weak smooth Calabi-Yau structure implies:

Lemma 2.28
.

Capping with a weak smooth Calabi-Yau structure
\sigma\in\mathrm{CC}_{-n}(\mathcal{C})
induces a natural quasi-isomorphism

(2.121)

-\cap\sigma:\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathrm{CC}_{*-n}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P}).

Here naturality means it commutes with chain maps induced by maps bimodules
\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{Q}
.
It is a chain homotopy equivalence, i.e., has a chain homotopy inverse.

Proof.

By Definition
2.27
,
\sigma
induces a quasi-isomorphism of
A_{\infty}
-bimodules
\mathcal{C}^{!}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}[-n]
.
Then apply the quasi-isomorphisms (
2.75
) and (
2.76
).

Since quasi-isomorphisms of
A_{\infty}
-bimodules are invertible (when
{\mathbf{k}}
is a field,
[
46
]
),
we also have a quasi-isomorphism of
A_{\infty}
-bimodules
\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}[-n]\to\mathcal{C}^{!}
, inducing a chain homotopy inverse of (
2.121
).
∎

An
A_{\infty}
-functor
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
induces a chain map

(2.122)

F_{*}:\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C})\to\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{D}),

which is moreover a morphism of
S^{1}
-complexes.
Thus by
[
31
, Corollary 3]
, it induces chains maps

(2.123)

\displaystyle F_{*,hS^{1}}:\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C})_{hS^{1}}

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{D})_{hS^{1}},

(2.124)

\displaystyle F_{*}^{hS^{1}}:\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C})^{hS^{1}}

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{D})^{hS^{1}}.

Definition 2.29
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},\sigma),(\mathcal{D},\tau)
be
A_{\infty}
-categories equipped with weak smooth Calabi-Yau structures of the same dimension
n
.
We say that
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
is a weak smooth Calabi-Yau functor,
if
[F_{*}(\sigma)]=[\tau]\in\mathrm{HH}_{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C})
.

Suppose
\phi,\psi
admit lifts to strong proper Calabi-Yau structures
\tilde{\phi},\tilde{\psi}
.
We say that
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
is a strong proper Calabi-Yau functor,
if
[F_{*}^{hS^{1}}(\tilde{\psi})]=[\tilde{\phi}]\in\mathrm{HC}_{*}^{+}(\mathcal{C})
.

A weak/strong smooth Calabi-Yau functor
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
