is called a weak/strong smooth Calabi-Yau equivalence,
if there exists a weak/strong smooth Calabi-Yau functor
G:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
such that
G\circ F
is homotopic to
\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}}
and
F\circ G
is homotopic to
\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{D}}
.

Definition 2.30
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},\phi),(\mathcal{D},\psi)
be
A_{\infty}
-categories equipped with weak proper Calabi-Yau structures of the same dimension
n
.
We say that
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
is a weak proper Calabi-Yau functor,
if
[\psi\circ F_{*}]=[\phi]\in\mathrm{HH}_{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C})^{\vee}[-n]
.

Suppose
\phi,\psi
admit lifts to strong proper Calabi-Yau structures
\tilde{\phi},\tilde{\psi}
.
We say that
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
is a strong proper Calabi-Yau functor,
if
[\tilde{\psi}\circ F_{*}^{hS^{1}}]=[\tilde{\phi}]\in\mathrm{HC}_{*}^{+}(\mathcal{C})^{\vee}
.

A weak/strong proper Calabi-Yau functor
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
is called a weak/strong proper Calabi-Yau equivalence,
if there exists a weak/strong smooth Calabi-Yau functor
G:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
such that
G\circ F
is homotopic to
\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}}
and
F\circ G
is homotopic to
\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{D}}
.

2.7.
A tautological pairing

To better understand a proper Calabi-Yau structure,
we take a closer look at the first piece of information it provides, i.e. a non-degenerate pairing between morphisms spaces of
\mathcal{C}
.

We first introduce some terminologies.

Definition 2.31
.

Let
A,B,C
be graded chain complexes over
{\mathbf{k}}
.
A bilinear map
\pi:A\times B\to C
is said to be compatible with gradings,
if there exists
m\in{\mathbb{Z}}
such that for every
i,j
and
a\in A^{i},b\in B^{j}
, the output
f(a,b)
has degree
i+j+m
.
When such an
m
exists, we call
m
the degree of the bilinear map
\pi
,
\deg(\pi)=m
.

A bilinear map
\pi:A\times B\to C
is said to be compatible with differentials, if

(2.125)

d_{C}(f(a,b))=f((-1)^{|b|-1}d_{A}a,b)+f(a,d_{B}b)

for all
a\in A,b\in B
.

When
C={\mathbf{k}}
the ground field, a bilinear map
\pi:A\times B\to{\mathbf{k}}
that is compatible with gradings and differentials is called a bilinear pairing between
A
and
B
.
If furthermore
A=B
, a bilinear pairing between
A
and
B
is simply called a bilinear pairing on
A
.

The most basic yet important example is the following:

Example 2.1
.

Let
A^{\vee}=\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(A^{-*},{\mathbf{k}})
be the linear dual chain complex of
A
,
with its grading as indicated, i.e.,
(A^{\vee})^{i}=\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(A^{-i},{\mathbf{k}})
.
The evaluation pairing

(2.126)

ev:A^{\vee}\times A\to{\mathbf{k}}

defined by

(2.127)

ev(f,a)=f(a)

is a bilinear pairing between
A^{\vee}
and
A
of degree
0
.
It is clear by definition that the pairing has degree
0
,
so it remains to check the condition (
2.125
).
We following the sign convention for
A_{\infty}
-categories when defining the differential on chain complexes,
in which case we have
\mu^{1}_{A}=-d_{A}
, and

(2.128)

(\delta_{A^{\vee}}f)(a)=(-1)^{|a|-1}f(\mu^{1}_{A}(a))=(-1)^{|a|}f(d_{A}a),

following the pattern for the structure maps (
2.30
) for the linear dual bimodule
\mathcal{C}^{\vee}
.
So

(2.129)

ev((-1)^{|a|-1}\delta_{\mathcal{A}^{\vee}}f,a)+ev(f,d_{A}a)=(-1)^{|a|-1+|a|}f(d_{A}a)+f(d_{A}a)=0.

which is (
2.125
) for the bilinear map
ev
.

The other pairing

(2.130)

\tilde{ev}:A\times A^{\vee}\to{\mathbf{k}}

is defined by

(2.131)

\tilde{ev}(a,f)=(-1)^{(|a|-1)(|f|-1)}f(a)=(-1)^{|a||f|+|a|+|f|}f(a).

Using the above map
ev
,
we can define a tautological pairing as follows.
Let
C
be a graded chain complex over
{\mathbf{k}}
,
which is either finite or infinite dimensional.
Consider the graded chain complex

(2.132)

V=C^{\vee}[1-n]\oplus C.

We define a bilinear pairing on
V
of degree
1-n

(2.133)

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{taut}:V\otimes V\to{\mathbf{k}}[1-n],

by

(2.134)

\langle(f_{1},x_{1}),(f_{2},x_{2})\rangle_{taut}=f_{1}(x_{2})+(-1)^{1+(|x_{1}|-1)(|x_{2}|-1)}f_{2}(x_{1})=f_{1}(x_{2})+(-1)^{|x_{1}||x_{2}+|x_{1}|+|x_{2}|}f_{2}(x_{1}).

Noting that the grading on the direct sum complex
V
is determine by the
C
-factor, we see that the pairing is graded symmetric

(2.135)

\langle(f_{1},x_{1}),(f_{2},x_{2})\rangle_{taut}=(-1)^{1+(|x_{1}|-1)(|x_{2}|-1)}\langle(f_{2},x_{2}),(f_{1},x_{1})\rangle_{taut}.

In fact, the following is almost straightforward from the definition:

Lemma 2.32
.

The pairing
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{taut}
(
2.133
) is nondegenerate.

Proof.

Suppose

(2.136)

f_{1}(x_{2})+(-1)^{|x_{1}||x_{2}+|x_{1}|+|x_{2}|}f_{2}(x_{1})=0

for all
(f_{1},x_{1})
.
By fixing
x_{1}=0
and considering all
f_{1}\in V^{\vee}[1-n]
, we get from (
2.136
)
f_{1}(x_{2})=0,\forall f_{1}
so
x_{2}=0
.
By fixing
f_{1}=0
and considering all
x_{1}\in V
, we get
f_{2}(x_{1})=0,\forall x_{1}
so
f_{2}=0
.
∎

More generally, we can consider for a given
A_{\infty}
-category
\mathcal{C}
the following bimodule

(2.137)

\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{C}^{\vee}[1-n]\oplus\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}.

\mathcal{B}
always has a tautological pairing,

(2.138)

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{taut}:\mathcal{B}(X,Y)\times\mathcal{B}(Y,X)\to{\mathbf{k}}.

for all objects
X,Y\in\mathrm{ob\ }\mathcal{C}
, defined in the same manner as (
2.133
).
More generally, we can allow a ‘twist’ on the bimodule structure on
\mathcal{B}
by viewing
\mathcal{B}
as a mapping cone of a map
\mathcal{C}^{\vee}[-n]\to\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}

which comes from a categorical copairing

(2.139)

{\mathbf{k}}\to\mathcal{C}(X,Y)\otimes\mathcal{C}(Y,X)[n].

When
\mathcal{C}
is proper, a cyclic
A_{\infty}
-category structure on
\mathcal{B}
with respect to this pairing is equivalent to the data of a pre-Calabi-Yau structure on
\mathcal{C}
(
[
34
]
).
However, since we will be mainly interested in the non-proper case,
hoping to find a strictly cyclic
A_{\infty}
-structure on
\mathcal{B}
is requiring too much symmetry for those categories
\mathcal{C}
appearing naturally in Floer theory, for which current geometric constructions are insufficient.
Instead, we shall proceed to §
3
,
to find
A_{\infty}
-structures on a homotopy replacement of
\mathcal{B}
,
which possesses a weak proper Calabi-Yau structure.

2.8.
Canonical pairing systems

In this subsection, we discuss a generalization of the pairings (
2.126
),
(
2.133
) and (
2.138
),
and its relation to Hochschild invariants.

Definition 2.33
.

Let
\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q}
be
\mathcal{C}-\mathcal{C}
-bimodules.
A canonical pairing system
\pi
between
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{Q}
is a family of pairings of degree
m

(2.140)

\pi_{X_{0},X_{1}}:\mathcal{P}(X_{0},X_{1})\times\mathcal{Q}(X_{1},X_{0})\to{\mathbf{k}}[m],

one for every pair of objects
X_{0},X_{1}
,
such that for all
\mathbf{x}_{1;k},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;l},p,q
we have

(2.141)

(-1)^{\maltese_{1;k}\cdot(|p|+\maltese^{\prime}_{1;l}+|q|)+|q|-1}\pi(\mu_{\mathcal{P}}^{k,l}(\mathbf{x}_{1;k},p,\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;l}),q)+\pi(p,\mu^{l,k}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;l},q,\mathbf{x}_{1;k}))=0.

Remark 2.34
.

The first set of equations in the condition (
2.141
) is

(2.142)

\pi((-1)^{|q|-1}\mu_{\mathcal{P}}^{0,0}(p),q)+\pi(p,\mu_{\mathcal{Q}}^{0,0}(q))=0.

In view that the
(0,0)
-th order map on an
A_{\infty}
-bimodule is a differential,
we can thus understand the condition (
2.141
) as a generalization of the usual skew-symmetry relation for pairings between chain complexes.

Suppose
\pi
is a canonical pairing system between
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{Q}
of degree
m
.
We define an induced map

(2.143)

\pi_{*}:\mathcal{P}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}-\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{Q}=\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{Q})\to{\mathbf{k}}[m]

as follows.

(2.144)

\pi_{*}(p\otimes\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;l}\otimes q\otimes\mathbf{x}_{1;k})=\begin{cases}\pi(p,q),&\text{ if }k=l=0,\\
0,&\text{ if }k>0\text{ or }l>0.\end{cases}

Proposition 2.35
.

The map
\pi_{*}:\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{Q})\to{\mathbf{k}}
(
2.143
) defined by (
2.144
) is a chain map of degree
m
.

Proof.

Consider an element
p\otimes\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;l}\otimes q\otimes\mathbf{x}_{1;k}
.
We must show that

\pi_{*}(d_{\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{Q})}(p\otimes\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;l}\otimes q\otimes\mathbf{x}_{1;k}))=0,

where the Hochschild chain differential agrees with the differential
d_{\mathcal{P}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}-\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{Q}}
by Lemma
2.19
.
Since the map
\pi_{*}
defined by (
2.144
) sends all tensors that have non zero length in the
\mathbf{x}
-entries and the
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}
-entries,
it suffices to consider the terms in the output of
d_{\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{Q})}(p\otimes\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;l}\otimes q\otimes\mathbf{x}_{1;k})
that have zero length in the
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}
-entries and the
\mathbf{x}
-entries in the sense of Definition
2.21
.
By Corollary
2.22
,
these terms are (
2.82
).
Apply
\pi_{*}
to the sum of these terms, in view of its definition (
2.144
), we obtain

(2.145)

(-1)^{\maltese_{1;k}\cdot(|p|+\maltese^{\prime}_{1;l}+|q|)+|q|-1}\pi(\mu_{\mathcal{P}}^{k,l}(\mathbf{x}_{1;k},p,\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;l}),q)+\pi(p,\mu^{l,k}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;l},q,\mathbf{x}_{1;k}))

which is exactly equal to zero because of the condition (
2.141
).
∎

The first main example of a canonical pairing system is the following:

Lemma 2.36
.

The evaluation pairings

(2.146)

ev:\mathcal{C}^{\vee}(X_{0},X_{1})\times\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}(X_{1},X_{0})=\mathcal{C}(X_{0},X_{1})^{\vee}\times\mathcal{C}(X_{0},X_{1})\to{\mathbf{k}},

defined by

(2.147)

ev(f,w)=f(w)

form a canonical pairing system of degree
0
.

The other pairings

(2.148)

\tilde{ev}:\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}(X_{1},X_{0})\times\mathcal{C}^{\vee}(X_{0},X_{1})=\mathcal{C}(X_{0},X_{1})\times\mathcal{C}(X_{0},X_{1})^{\vee}\to{\mathbf{k}}

defined by

(2.149)

\tilde{ev}(w,f)=(-1)^{|w||f|+|w|+|f|}f(x)

also form a canonical pairing system of degree
0
.

Proof.

Recall that the bimodule structure maps for
\mathcal{C}^{\vee}
are defined by (
2.30
), so we have

(2.150)

\begin{split}&(-1)^{\maltese_{1;k}\cdot(|f|+\maltese^{\prime}_{1;l}+|w|)+|w|-1}ev(\mu_{\mathcal{C}^{\vee}}^{k,l}(\mathbf{x}_{1;k},f,\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;l}),w)\\
=&(-1)^{\maltese_{1;k}\cdot(|f|+\maltese^{\prime}_{1;l}+|w|)+|w|-1}(-1)^{\maltese_{1;k}\cdot(|f|+\maltese^{\prime}_{1;l}+|w|-1)+|w|-1}f(\mu^{k+l+1}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;l},w,\mathbf{x}_{1;k}))\\
=&(-1)^{\maltese_{1;k}}f(\mu^{k+l+1}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;l},w,\mathbf{x}_{1;k})).\end{split}

The structure maps for
\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}
are defined by (
2.26
), so we have

(2.151)

ev(f,\mu_{\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}}^{l,k}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;l},w,\mathbf{x}_{1;k}))=(-1)^{\maltese_{1;k}+1}f(\mu_{\mathcal{C}}^{k+l+1}(\mathbf{x}_{1;k},w,\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;l})).

It follows that

(-1)^{\maltese_{1;k}\cdot(|f|+\maltese^{\prime}_{1;l}+|w|)+|w|-1}ev(\mu_{\mathcal{C}^{\vee}}^{k,l}(\mathbf{x}_{1;k},f,\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;l}),w)+ev(f,\mu_{\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}}^{l,k}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;l},w,\mathbf{x}_{1;k}))=0,

which is (
2.141
).

For the other pairing
\tilde{ev}
(
2.148
), we first compute

(2.152)

\mu_{\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}}^{k,l}(\mathbf{x}_{1;k},w,\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;l})=(-1)^{\maltese^{\prime}_{1;l}+1}\mu_{\mathcal{C}}^{k+l+1}(\mathbf{x}_{1;k},w,\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;l}),

and

(2.153)

\mu_{\mathcal{C}^{\vee}}^{l,k}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;l},f,\mathbf{x}_{1;k})(w)=(-1)^{\maltese^{\prime}_{1;l}(|f|+\maltese_{1;k}+|w|-1)+|w|-1}f(\mu_{\mathcal{C}}^{l+k+1}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;l},w,\mathbf{x}_{1;k})).

Note that the degree satisfy

(2.154)

\displaystyle|\mu_{\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}}^{k,l}(\mathbf{x}_{1;k},w,\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;l})|=|w|+\maltese_{1;k}+\maltese^{\prime}_{1;l}+1,

(2.155)

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathcal{C}^{\vee}}^{l,k}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;l},f,\mathbf{x}_{1;k})=|f|+\maltese^{\prime}_{1;l}+\maltese_{1;k}+1.

We then compute

(2.156)

\begin{split}&(-1)^{\maltese_{1;k}(|w|+\maltese^{\prime}_{1;l}+|f|-1)+|f|-1}\tilde{ev}(\mu_{\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}}^{k,l}(\mathbf{x}_{1;k},w,\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;l}),f)\\
=&(-1)^{\maltese_{1;k}(|w|+\maltese^{\prime}_{1;l}+|f|-1)+|f|-1+\maltese^{\prime}_{1;l}+1+(|w|+\maltese_{1;k}+\maltese^{\prime}_{1;l}+1)|f|+|w|+\maltese_{1;k}+\maltese^{\prime}_{1;l}+1+|f|}f(\mu_{\mathcal{C}}^{k+l+1}(\mathbf{x}_{1;k},w,\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;l}))\\
=&(-1)^{\maltese_{1;k}|w|+\maltese_{1;k}\maltese^{\prime}_{1;l}+\maltese^{\prime}_{1;l}|f|+|w||f|+|w|+|f|+\maltese_{1;k}+1}f(\mu_{\mathcal{C}}^{k+l+1}(\mathbf{x}_{1;k},w,\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;l})),\end{split}

and

(2.157)

\begin{split}&\tilde{ev}(w,\mu_{\mathcal{C}^{\vee}}^{l,k}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;l},f,\mathbf{x}_{1;k}))\\
=&(-1)^{\maltese^{\prime}_{1;l}(|f|+\maltese_{1;k}+|w|-1)+|w|-1+|w|(|f|+\maltese^{\prime}_{1;l}+\maltese_{1;k}+1)+|w|+|f|+\maltese^{\prime}_{1;l}+\maltese_{1;k}+1}f(\mu_{\mathcal{C}}^{k+l+1}(\mathbf{x}_{1;k},w,\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;l}))\\
=&(-1)^{\maltese^{\prime}_{1,l}|f|+\maltese_{1;k}\maltese^{\prime}_{1;l}+|w|+|w||f|+\maltese_{1;k}|w|+|f|+\maltese_{1;k}}\end{split}

So (
2.156
) and (
2.157
) add up to zero.
∎

It is illustrate to see the case where
k=0
, in which case the equation is

(-1)^{|w|-1}ev(\mu^{0,0}_{\mathcal{C}^{\vee}}(f),w)+ev(f,\mu^{0,0}_{\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}})=(-1)^{|w|-1}(-1)^{|w|-1}f(\mu^{1}_{\mathcal{C}}(w))+(-1)^{-1}f(\mu^{1}_{\mathcal{C}}(w)=0,

which is (
2.129
).

By Proposition
2.35
, the canonical pairing systems
ev
(
2.146
) and
\tilde{ev}
(
2.148
) induce chain maps of degree zero:

(2.158)

ev_{*}:\mathcal{C}^{\vee}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}-\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}=\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}^{\vee}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{C}_{\Delta})\to{\mathbf{k}},

and

(2.159)

\tilde{ev}_{*}:\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}-\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{C}^{\vee}=\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{C}^{\vee})\to{\mathbf{k}}.

A similar example is the following:

Corollary 2.37
.

The tautological pairings
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{taut}
(
2.138
) form a canonical pairing system,
which induces a map of chain complexes

(2.160)

\mathcal{B}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}-\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{B}=\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{B}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{B})\to{\mathbf{k}}[1-n]

of degree
1-n
, where
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{C}^{\vee}[1-n]\oplus\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}
.

Proof.

Since the pairing is only between
\mathcal{C}^{\vee}[1-n](X_{0},X_{1})
from the first factor of the left hand side of (
2.181
) and
\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}(X_{1},X_{0})
from the second factor,
as well as between
\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}(X_{0},X_{1})
from the first factor and
\mathcal{C}^{\vee}[1-n](X_{1},X_{1})
from the second factor,
the result follows from Lemma
2.36
.
∎

Example 2.2
.

If
(\mathcal{C},\mu^{k},\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{cyc})
is a cyclic
A_{\infty}
-category,
then the pairing defines a canonical pairing system between
(\mathcal{C})_{\Delta}
and itself.
The sign in the equation (
2.141
) comes from the graded symmetry of the cyclic pairing
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{cyc}
.

However, one should be careful about the difference between this example and Corollary
2.37
:
it does not say that
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{C}^{\vee}[1-n]\oplus\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}
itself carries a cyclic
A_{\infty}
-structure,
that pairing is between
\mathcal{C}-\mathcal{C}
-bimodules, and we do not even have an
A_{\infty}
-category structure on
\mathcal{B}

More importantly, we consider the
\mathcal{C}^{op}-\mathcal{C}^{op}
-bimodules
\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{1}}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{0}})^{\vee}
(
2.35
) and
\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{1}},\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{0}})
(
2.39
).

Definition 2.38
.

For each pair of objects
Y_{1},Y_{0}\in\mathrm{ob\ }\mathcal{C}^{op}
, we define a pairing

(2.161)

\pi^{ev}_{Y_{1},Y_{0}}:(\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{1}}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{0}})^{\vee})(Y_{1},Y_{0})\times(\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{1}},\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{0}}))(Y_{0},Y_{1})\to{\mathbf{k}}

by the formula

(2.162)

\pi^{ev}_{Y_{1},Y_{0}}(z\otimes f,\psi)=(-1)^{|z|}f(\psi(z)).

In the standard terms, we call this the composition trace pairing,
which comes from the supertrace of the composition of a finite-rank linear map (in this case rank-one) and a general linear map with opposite degrees.
It is clearly nondegenerate.

Lemma 2.39
.

The pairings
\pi^{ev}_{Y_{1},Y_{0}}
form a canonical pairing system of degree
0
between the
\mathcal{C}^{op}-\mathcal{C}^{op}
-bimodules
\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{1}}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{0}})^{\vee}
and
\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{1}},\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{0}})
.

Proof.

We must verify the condition (
2.141
) for the pairings
\pi^{ev}_{Y_{1},Y_{0}}
.
Since the formula (
2.162
) is not sensitive to the objects
Y_{1},Y_{0}
,
we shall omit the subscripts
Y_{1},Y_{0}
throughout the proof for clearness of notation.
By definitions of the bimodule structure maps (
2.37
) and (
2.41
),
the
\mu^{k,l}
maps all vanish for
k,l
both positive.
So the only conditions to check are

(2.163)

(-1)^{|\psi|-1}\pi^{ev}(\mu_{\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{1}}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{0}})^{\vee}}^{0,0}(z\otimes f),\psi)+\pi^{ev}(z\otimes f,\mu_{\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{1}},\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{0}})}^{0,0}(\psi))=0,

(2.164)

(-1)^{\maltese_{1;k}\cdot(|f|+|z|+|\psi|)+|\psi|-1}\pi^{ev}(\mu_{\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{1}}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{0}})^{\vee}}^{k,0}(\mathbf{x}_{1;k},z\otimes f),\psi)+\pi^{ev}(z\otimes f,\mu_{\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{1}},\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{0}})}^{0,k}(\psi,\mathbf{x}_{1;k}))=0,

(2.165)

\pi^{ev}(\mu_{\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{1}}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{0}})^{\vee}}^{0,l}(z\otimes f,\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;l}),\psi)+\pi^{ev}(z\otimes f,\mu_{\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{1}},\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{0}})}^{l,0}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;l},\psi))=0.

First we check (
2.163
),
in which the two terms are possibly nonzero only if the degrees satisfy

(2.166)

|z|+|f|+|\psi|+1=0.

By definition we have

(2.167)

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{1}}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{0}})^{\vee}}^{0,0}(z\otimes f)(w)=(-1)^{|f|-1}\mu^{1}_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}(z)\otimes f(w)+(-1)^{|w|-1}z\otimes f(\mu^{1}_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}(w)),

(2.168)

\displaystyle\mu_{\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{1}},\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{0}})}^{0,0}(\psi)(w)=(-1)^{|w|-1}\phi(\mu^{1}_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}(w))+(-1)^{|w|-1}\mu^{1}_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}(\phi(w)).

Then we compute

(2.169)

\begin{split}&(-1)^{|\psi|-1}\pi^{ev}(\mu_{\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{1}}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{0}})^{\vee}}^{0,0}(z\otimes f),\psi)\\
=&(-1)^{|\psi|-1}\pi^{ev}((-1)^{|f|-1}\mu^{1}_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}(z)\otimes f+z\otimes(w\mapsto(-1)^{|w|-1}f(\mu^{1}_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}(w))),\psi)\\
=&(-1)^{|\psi|+|f|+|\mu^{1}_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}(z)|}f(\psi(\mu^{1}_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}(z)))+(-1)^{|\psi|+|z|+|\psi(z)|}f(\mu^{1}_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}(\psi(z))),\\
=&f(\psi(\mu^{1}_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}(z)))+f(\mu^{1}_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}(\psi(z))),\end{split}

where the last equality is because of (
2.166
),
|\mu^{1}_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}(z)|=|z|+1
and
|\psi(z)|=|\psi|+|z|
, and

(2.170)

\begin{split}&\pi^{ev}(z\otimes f,\mu_{\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{1}},\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{0}})}^{0,0}(\psi))\\
=&\pi^{ev}(z\otimes f,(w\mapsto(-1)^{|w|-1}\phi(\mu^{1}_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}(w))+(-1)^{|w|-1}\mu^{1}_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}(\phi(w))))\\
=&(-1)^{|z|+|z|-1}f(\psi(\mu^{1}_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}(z)))+(-1)^{|z|+|z|-1}f(\mu^{1}_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}(\psi(z)))\\
=&(-1)^{1}f(\psi(\mu^{1}_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}(z)))+(-1)^{1}f(\mu^{1}_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}(\psi(z))).\end{split}

So (
2.169
) and (
2.170
) add up to zero.

Let us check (
2.164
); the other is completely analogous.
These terms in (
2.164
) are possibly nonzero only if the degrees satisfy

(2.171)

\maltese_{1;k}+|z|+|f|+1+|\psi|=0.

By definition we have

(2.172)

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{1}}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{0}})^{\vee}}^{k,0}(\mathbf{x}_{1;k},z\otimes f)(w)=(-1)^{|f|-1}\mu^{k+1}_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}(\mathbf{x}_{1;k},z)\otimes f(w),

(2.173)

\displaystyle\mu_{\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{1}},\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{0}})}^{0,k}(\psi,\mathbf{x}_{1;k})(w)=(-1)^{|w|-1}\psi(\mu^{k+1}_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}(\mathbf{x}_{1;k},w)).

Note that the degree of
\mu^{k+1}_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}(\mathbf{x}_{1;k},z)
is

(2.174)

|\mu^{k+1}_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}(\mathbf{x}_{1;k},z)|=|z|+\maltese_{1;k}+1.

Now apply the pairing (
2.161
) to obtain

(2.175)

\begin{split}&(-1)^{\maltese_{1;k}\cdot(|z|+|f|+|\psi|)+|\psi|-1}\pi^{ev}(\mu_{\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{1}}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{0}})^{\vee}}^{k,0}(\mathbf{x}_{1;k},z\otimes f),\psi)\\
=&(-1)^{\maltese_{1;k}\cdot(|z|+|f|+|\psi|)+|\psi|-1}\pi^{ev}((-1)^{|f|-1}\mu^{k+1}_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}(\mathbf{x}_{1;k},z)\otimes f(\cdot),\psi)\\
=&(-1)^{\maltese_{1;k}(|z|+|f|+|\psi|))+|\psi|+|f|+|z|+\maltese_{1;k}+1+|f|}f(\mu_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}^{k+1}(\psi(z),\mathbf{x}_{1;k}),\\
=&(-1)^{\maltese_{1;k}(-\maltese_{1;k}-1)}f(\mu_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}^{k+1}(\psi(z),\mathbf{x}_{1;k})\\
=&f(\mu_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}^{k+1}(\psi(z),\mathbf{x}_{1;k}).\end{split}

where the fourth equality is because of (
2.171
).
Similarly, we have

(2.176)

\begin{split}&\pi^{ev}(z\otimes f,\mu_{\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{1}},\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{0}})}^{0,k}(\psi,\mathbf{x}_{1;k}))\\
=&\pi^{ev}(z\otimes f,w\mapsto(-1)^{|w|-1}\psi(\mu^{k+1}_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}(\mathbf{x}_{1;k},w))\\
=&(-1)^{|z|}(-1)^{|z|-1}f(\mu_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}^{k+1}(\phi(z),\mathbf{x}_{1;k}))\\
=&(-1)^{1}f(\mu_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}^{k+1}(\phi(z),\mathbf{x}_{1;k})).\end{split}

So (
2.175
) and (
2.176
) add up to zero.

For the other equation (
2.165
),
by definition we have

(2.177)

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{1}}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{0}})^{\vee}}^{0,l}(z\otimes f,\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;l})(w)=(-1)^{|w|-1}z\otimes f(\mu_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}^{l+1}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;l},w)),

(2.178)

\displaystyle\mu_{\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{1}},\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{0}})}^{l,0}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;l},\psi)(w)=(-1)^{|w|-1}\mu^{l+1}_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;l},\psi(w)).

Then we compute

(2.179)

\begin{split}&(-1)^{|\psi|-1}\pi^{ev}(\mu_{\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{1}}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{0}})^{\vee}}^{0,l}(z\otimes f,\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;l}),\psi)\\
=&(-1)^{|\psi|-1}\pi^{ev}(z\otimes(w\mapsto(-1)^{|w|-1}f(\mu_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}^{l+1}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;l},w))),\psi)\\
=&(-1)^{|\psi|+|z|+|\psi(z)|}f(\mu_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}^{l+1}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;l},\psi(z)))\\
=&f(\mu_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}^{l+1}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;l},\psi(z))),\end{split}

and

(2.180)

\begin{split}&\pi^{ev}(z\otimes f,\mu_{\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{1}},\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{0}})}^{l,0}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;l},\psi)\\
=&\pi^{ev}(z\otimes f,(w\mapsto(-1)^{|w|-1}\mu^{l+1}_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;l},\psi(w))))\\
=&(-1)^{|z|+|z|-1}f(\mu_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}^{l+1}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;l},\psi(z)))\\
=&(-1)^{1}f(\mu_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}^{l+1}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;l},\psi(z))).\end{split}

∎

Similar to the tautological pairing
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{taut}
(
2.138
),
we can use the pairing the other way round with a sign twist to define a pairing of degree
1
:

(2.181)

\begin{split}\pi^{ev,+}_{Y_{0},Y_{1}}:&(\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{1}}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{0}})^{\vee}[1]\bigoplus\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{0}},\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{1}}))(Y_{1},Y_{0})\\
&\times(\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{0}}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{1}})^{\vee}[1]\bigoplus\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{1}},\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{0}}))(Y_{1},Y_{0})\to{\mathbf{k}}[1],\end{split}

(2.182)

\pi^{ev,+}_{Y_{0},Y_{1}}((z\otimes f,\phi),(w\otimes g,\psi))=(-1)^{|z|}f(\psi(z))+(-1)^{|w|+|\phi|\psi|+|\phi|+|\psi|}g(\phi(w)).

where the second sign follows the pattern of the sign in (
2.134
).

Immediately from the definition (
2.182
), we see that

Lemma 2.40
.

The pairing
\pi^{ev,+}_{Y_{0},Y_{1}}
(
2.181
) is graded symmetric with sign
(-1)^{1+(|\phi|-1)(|\psi|-1)}
.

∎

Corollary 2.41
.

The pairings
\pi^{ev,+}
(
2.181
) form a canonical pairing system of degree
1
between the
\mathcal{C}-\mathcal{C}
-bimodules

\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{1}}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{0}})^{\vee}[1]\bigoplus\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{0}},\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{1}})

and

\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{0}}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{1}})^{\vee}[1]\bigoplus\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{1}},\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{0}}).

Proof.

Since the pairing is only between
\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{1}}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{0}})^{\vee}[1](Y_{1},Y_{0})
from the first factor of the left hand side of (
2.181
) and
\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{1}},\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{0}})(Y_{0},Y_{1})
from the second factor,
as well as between
\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{0}},\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{1}})(Y_{1},Y_{0})
from the first factor and
\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{0}}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{1}})^{\vee}[1](Y_{0},Y_{1})
from the second factor,
the result follows from Lemma
2.39
.
∎

3.
The categorical punctured neighborhood of infinity

In this section, we review the definition of the
(algebraizable) categorical formal punctured neighborhood of infinity
of a given
A_{\infty}
-catetegory
\mathcal{C}
,
whose definition was first due to
[
23
]
in the case of dg-categories,
and has a straightforward generalization to
A_{\infty}
-categories in
[
28
]
.
We develop further studies of it which lead to several important Hochschild invariants related to Calabi-Yau structures.

3.1.
The definition

The notion of the Calkin algebra was originally introduced in
[
12
]
to study bounded operators in Hilbert spaces,
and has the following algebraic analogue (
[
22
,
23
]
).
Define the category of
Calkin modules
over any
A_{\infty}
-category
\mathcal{C}
to be

(3.1)

\mathrm{Calk}_{\mathcal{C}}=\mathrm{mod\!-\!}\mathcal{C}/\mathrm{Perf}\mathcal{C}.

When
\mathcal{C}={\mathbf{k}}
is the
A_{\infty}
-category with one object whose endomorphism is the ground ring
{\mathbf{k}}
on which all
A_{\infty}
-operations vanish except
\mu^{1}
and
\mu^{2}
;
we call the resulting category
\mathrm{Calk}_{{\mathbf{k}}}
the category of Calkin complexes over
{\mathbf{k}}
.
To give a concrete description of
\mu^{1},\mu^{2}
, we first compute the morphism space in
\mathrm{Calk}_{{\mathbf{k}}}
to be:

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
M,N\in\mathrm{ob\ }\mathrm{Calk}_{{\mathbf{k}}}=\mathrm{ob\ }\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathbf{k}}}
, i.e., chain complexes over
{\mathbf{k}}
thought of as objects in the category
\mathrm{Calk}_{{\mathbf{k}}}
.
The morphism space in
\mathrm{Calk}_{{\mathbf{k}}}
is given by

(3.2)

\mathrm{Calk}_{{\mathbf{k}}}(M,N):=\hom_{\mathrm{Calk}_{{\mathbf{k}}}}(M,N)=\mathrm{cone}(N\otimes M^{\vee}\to\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(M,N))=N\otimes M^{\vee}[1]\bigoplus\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(M,N).

Proof.

Since the derived tensor product over
{\mathbf{k}}
is the ordinary tensor product,
the bar complex for computing the morphism spaces in the quotient category
\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathbf{k}}}/\mathrm{Perf}{\mathbf{k}}
reduces to

\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}({\mathbf{k}},N)\otimes\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(M,{\mathbf{k}})\to\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(M,N)
,
which gives the desired complex (
3.2
).
∎

The differential of
a=(z\otimes f,\phi)\in\mathrm{Calk}_{{\mathbf{k}}}(M,N)
is defined to be

(3.3)

\begin{split}d_{\mathrm{Calk}_{{\mathbf{k}}}}(z\otimes f,\phi)=&((-1)^{|f|}d_{N}z\otimes f+z\otimes\delta f,\delta\phi)\\
=&((-1)^{|f|}d_{N}z\otimes f-(-1)^{|f|}z\otimes f\circ d_{M},d_{N}\circ\phi-(-1)^{|\phi|}\phi\circ d_{M}).\end{split}

For
a_{i}=(z_{i}\otimes f_{i},\phi_{i})\in\mathrm{Calk}_{{\mathbf{k}}}(M_{i-1},M_{i}),i=1,2
,
their
Calkin composition
, induced from the dg category structure on
\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathbf{k}}}/\mathrm{Perf}{\mathbf{k}}
, is defined to be

(3.4)

(f_{2}\otimes z_{2},\phi_{2})\circ_{\mathrm{Calk}_{{\mathbf{k}}}}(f_{1}\otimes z_{1},\phi_{1})=(\phi_{1}^{*}(f_{2})\otimes z_{2}+f_{1}\otimes\phi_{2}(z_{1}),\phi_{2}\circ\phi_{1}).

Passing from the dg category structure to the
A_{\infty}
-category structure introduces a sign twist:

(3.5)

\displaystyle\mu^{1}_{\mathrm{Calk}_{{\mathbf{k}}}}(a)

\displaystyle=-d_{\mathrm{Calk}_{{\mathbf{k}}}}(a),

(3.6)

\displaystyle\mu^{2}_{\mathrm{Calk}_{{\mathbf{k}}}}(a_{2},a_{1})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|a_{1}|}a_{2}\circ_{\mathrm{Calk}_{{\mathbf{k}}}}a_{1}.

Composed with the natural quotient functor
\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathbf{k}}}\to\mathrm{Calk}_{{\mathbf{k}}}
, the Yoneda functor for
\mathcal{C}
induces

(3.7)

\bar{y}:\mathcal{C}\to\mathrm{mod\!-\!}\mathcal{C}\to\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{op},\mathrm{Calk}_{{\mathbf{k}}}).

We define the categorical formal punctured neighborhood of infinity
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}
of
\mathcal{C}
to be the essential image of (
3.7
), i.e.

(3.8)

\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}=ess-im(\bar{y}:\mathcal{C}\to\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{op},\mathrm{Calk}_{{\mathbf{k}}}).

Lemma 3.2
.

The
A_{\infty}
-category
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}
is a dg category, i.e., has
\mu^{k}_{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}}=0
for all
k\geq 3
.

Proof.

Since
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{op},\mathrm{Calk}_{{\mathbf{k}}})
has
\mu^{k}=0
for all
k\geq 3
,
it follows from the definition (
3.8
) that
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}
has
\mu^{k}_{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}}=0
for all
k\geq 3
as well.
∎

By construction, it comes with a canonical
A_{\infty}
-functor, the induced Yoneda functor,

(3.9)

\bar{y}:\mathcal{C}\to\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty},

which sends (right) proper objects to zero,
where we say an object
X\in\mathrm{ob\ }\mathcal{C}
is (right) proper if
\mathcal{C}(Y,X)\in\mathrm{Perf}{\mathbf{k}}
for all
Y
;
in other words, the right Yoneda module of
X
is a proper
\mathcal{C}
-module.
Denote by
\mathcal{C}_{prop}
the subcategory of (right) proper objects.
Thus the functor (
3.9
) induces a functor

(3.10)

\tilde{\bar{y}}:\mathcal{C}/\mathcal{C}_{prop}\to\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}.

It follows immediately by definition that

Lemma 3.3
.

\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}=0
if and only if
\mathcal{C}
is proper.

Proof.

If
\mathcal{C}
is proper, then every right Yoneda module
Y^{r}_{X},X\in\mathrm{ob\ }\mathcal{C}
is proper.
Thus
\bar{y}(L)=0
, where
\bar{y}
is (
3.7
).

Conversely, if
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}=0
, then
\bar{y}(X)=pr(Y^{r}_{X})=0
, where
pr:\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathbf{k}}}\to\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathbf{k}}}/\mathrm{Perf}{\mathbf{k}}
is the quotient functor.
Thus
\mathcal{C}(Y,X)\in\mathrm{Perf}{\mathbf{k}}
for all
X,Y
, so
\mathcal{C}
is proper.
∎

Let
\mathrm{Prop}\mathcal{C}\subset\mathrm{mod\!-\!}\mathcal{C}
denote the full subcategory of proper right
\mathcal{C}
-modules,
also called the
pseudo-perfect
modules.
If
\mathcal{C}
is smooth,
then by
[
52
]

\mathrm{Prop}\mathcal{C}\xhookrightarrow{}\mathrm{Perf}\mathcal{C}
,
and the functor (
3.10
) can be rewritten as

(3.11)

\tilde{\bar{y}}:\mathcal{C}/\mathrm{Prop}\mathcal{C}\to\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}.

Since the morphism spaces in the functor category are given by Hochschild cochain complexes,
we see that

Lemma 3.4

(
[
28
, §2.4]
)
.

For any
X_{0},X_{1}\in\mathrm{ob\ }\mathcal{C}=\mathrm{ob\ }\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}
, we have strict equalities of chain complexes

(3.12)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}(X_{0},X_{1})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C}^{op},\mathrm{Calk}_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{0}},\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{1}}))

(3.13)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C}^{op},\mathrm{cone}(\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{1}}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{0}})^{\vee}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i}}{{\to}}\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{0}},\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{1}})))

(3.14)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{cone}(\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C}^{op},\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{1}}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{0}})^{\vee})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i_{*}}}{{\to}}\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C}^{op},\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{0}},\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{1}}))),

where the bimodule map
i
is (
2.43
),
and the differential on the Hochschild cochain complex is defined in (
2.72
).

Moreover, the cone (
3.13
) carries a natural product structure giving an explicit formula for the product structure on
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}

(3.15)

\begin{split}\mu_{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}}^{2}(\psi_{2},\psi_{1})^{k}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})=&\sum_{l}\mu^{2}_{\mathrm{Calk}_{{\mathbf{k}}}}(\psi_{2}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{l}),\psi_{1}(x_{l+1},\ldots,x_{k}))\\
=&\sum_{l}(-1)^{|\psi_{1}(x_{l+1},\ldots,x_{k})|}\psi_{2}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{l})\circ_{\mathrm{Calk}_{{\mathbf{k}}}}\psi_{1}(x_{l+1},\ldots,x_{k}))\\
=&\sum_{l}(-1)^{|\psi_{1}|+\maltese_{l+1;k}}\psi_{2}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{l})\circ_{\mathrm{Calk}_{{\mathbf{k}}}}\psi_{1}(x_{l+1},\ldots,x_{k}).\end{split}

Proof.

This follows from the definition of
\mu^{2}
in the functor category
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{op},\mathrm{Calk}_{{\mathbf{k}}})
(
2.15
), Lemma
2.18
and Lemma
3.1
.
∎

For simplicity of notation, for each
X_{0},X_{1}\in\mathrm{ob\ }\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}
, we denote the
\mathcal{C}^{op}-\mathcal{C}^{op}
-bimodule in the coefficient of (
3.13
) by

(3.16)

\mathcal{Z}_{X_{0}}^{X_{1}}:=\mathrm{cone}(\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{1}}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{0}})^{\vee}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i}}{{\to}}\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{0}},\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{1}}))=\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{1}}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{0}})^{\vee}[1]\bigoplus\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{0}},\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{1}})),

so that
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}(X_{0},X_{1})=\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C}^{op},\mathcal{Z}_{X_{0}}^{X_{1}})
.

Lemma 3.5
.

As chain complexes, we have

(3.17)

\begin{split}\mathcal{Z}_{X_{0}}^{X_{1}}(Y_{1},Y_{0})=&\mathrm{cone}(\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{1}}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{0}})^{\vee}(Y_{1},Y_{0})\to\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{0}}(Y_{0}),\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{1}}(Y_{1})))\\
=&\mathrm{cone}(\mathcal{C}(Y_{1},X_{1})\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}\mathcal{C}(Y_{0},X_{0})^{\vee}\to\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{C}(Y_{0},X_{0}),\mathcal{C}(Y_{1},X_{1})))\end{split}

Proof.

This is a straightforward calculation by the definition of the bimodules
\mathcal{Z}
(
3.16
).
Also see Definition
2.9
and Definition
2.11
for the relevant definitions of the linear tensor product bimodule and the linear hom bimodule.
∎

The bimodule structure maps on this cone (
3.16
) are induced from the bimodule structure maps (
2.37
) for
\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{1}}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{0}})^{\vee}
and (
2.41
) for
\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{0}},\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{1}})
,
as well as the bimodule map
i
(
2.43
) (which has vanishing higher order terms),

(3.18)

\begin{split}&\mu^{k,l}_{\mathcal{Z}_{X_{0}}^{X_{1}}}(\mathbf{x}_{1;k},(z\otimes f,\phi),\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;l})=\\
&\begin{cases}&(\mu^{k,l}_{\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{1}}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{0}})^{\vee}}(\mathbf{x}_{1;k},z\otimes f,\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;l}),\mu^{k,l}_{\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{0}},\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{1}})}(\mathbf{x}_{1;k},\phi,\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;l})),\text{ if }k>0\text{ or }l>0,\\
&(\mu^{0,0}_{\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{1}}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{0}})^{\vee}}(z\otimes f),\mu^{0,0}_{\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{0}},\mathcal{Y}^{l}_{X_{1}})}(\phi)+i(z\otimes f)).\end{cases}\end{split}

Phrasing Lemma
3.4
more functorially,
the composition in the Calkin category
\mathrm{Calk}_{{\mathbf{k}}}=\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathbf{k}}}/\mathrm{Perf}{\mathbf{k}}
induces a morphism of
\mathcal{C}^{op}-\mathcal{C}^{op}
-bimodules

(3.19)

c_{\mathrm{Calk}_{{\mathbf{k}}}}:\mathcal{Z}_{X_{1}}^{X_{2}}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}\mathcal{Z}_{X_{0}}^{X_{1}}\to\mathcal{Z}_{X_{0}}^{X_{2}}.

Now the second half of Lemma
3.4
can be restated as:

Corollary 3.6
.

The
\mu^{2}_{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}}
-product on
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}
is given by the composition

(3.20)

\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}(X_{1},X_{2})\otimes\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}(X_{0},X_{1})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sqcup}}{{\to}}\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C}^{op},\mathcal{Z}_{X_{1}}^{X_{2}}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}\mathcal{Z}_{X_{0}}^{X_{1}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c_{\mathrm{Calk}_{{\mathbf{k}}},*}}}{{\to}}\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C}^{op},\mathcal{Z}_{X_{0}}^{X_{2}})=\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}(X_{0},X_{2})

up to the sign twist
(-1)^{|c_{1}|}
,
where
\sqcup
is (
2.106
),
and the map
c_{\mathrm{Calk}_{{\mathbf{k}}},*}
is map on Hochschild cochain complexes induced by the morphism of bimodules
c_{\mathrm{Calk}_{{\mathbf{k}}}}
(
3.19
).
That is

(3.21)

\mu^{2}_{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}}(c_{2},c_{1})=(-1)^{|c_{1}|}c_{\mathrm{Calk}_{{\mathbf{k}}},*}(c_{2}\sqcup c_{1}).

Proof.

This follows immediately from Lemma
3.4
and the formula (
2.108
) for the product
\sqcup
.
∎

From this, it is also clear that
\mu^{2}_{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}}
is associative on the chain level with the standard
A_{\infty}
-sign twist,
because
\sqcup
and the composition in the Calkin category
\mathrm{Calk}_{{\mathbf{k}}}
are both associative.

Corollary 3.7
.

As dg categories, or
A_{\infty}
-categories with
\mu^{k}=0
for
k\geq 3
, there are quasi-equivalence

(3.22)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}\cong

\displaystyle\mathrm{cone}(\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C}^{op},\mathrm{Calk}_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{C}^{op},\mathcal{C}^{op})))

(3.23)

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\mathrm{cone}(\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C}^{op},\mathcal{C}^{op}_{\Delta}\otimes(\mathcal{C}^{op})^{\vee})\to\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C}^{op},\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{C}^{op}_{\Delta},\mathcal{C}^{op}_{\Delta})))

∎

In the case where
\mathcal{C}
is smooth, we also have

Lemma 3.8

(
[
28
, Proposition 2.11]
)
.

Suppose
\mathcal{C}
is a smooth
A_{\infty}
-category.
Then we have a distinguished triangle of
\mathcal{C}^{op}
-bimodules

(3.24)

(\mathcal{C}^{op})^{!}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}(\mathcal{C}^{op})^{\vee}\to\mathcal{C}^{op}_{\Delta}\to\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}.

∎

Sometimes it will be convenient to switch from
\mathcal{C}^{op}
-bimodules to
\mathcal{C}
-bimodules.
Noting that the convention for the diagonal bimodule is
(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})_{\Delta}(X,Y)=\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}(Y,X)
,
one obtains the following corollary by interchanging the left and right actions to go from a
\mathcal{C}^{op}
-bimodule to a
\mathcal{C}
-bimodule.

Corollary 3.9
.

If
\mathcal{C}
is a smooth
A_{\infty}
-category,
we have a distinguished triangle of
\mathcal{C}
-bimodules

(3.25)

\mathcal{C}^{!}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{C}^{\vee}\to\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}\to(\bar{y},\bar{y})^{*}(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})_{\Delta}

where
\bar{y}:\mathcal{C}\to\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}
is (
3.9
).

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{C}^{op}
-bimodule is naturally a
\mathcal{C}
-bimodule by interchanging the left and right actions,
the result follows by applying the interchange to (
3.24
).
∎

3.2.
An extended product

Viewing
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}
as a bimodule over
\mathcal{C}
via the canonical functor
\bar{y}:\mathcal{C}\to\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}
(
3.7
),
we can consider the Hochschild cohomology
\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C},\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})
with coefficients in
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}
, i.e.,

(3.26)

\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C},\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}):=\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C},(\bar{y},\bar{y})^{*}(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})_{\Delta}).

The
A_{\infty}
category structure on
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}
induces a morphism of
\mathcal{C}
-bimodules

(3.27)

\wedge_{\infty}:\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}\to\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}

by means of pullback by
\bar{y}
;
the formula is similar to (
2.59
) for the map (
2.58
),
where
\mu^{k+s+1,l}_{(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})_{\Delta}}=(-1)^{\maltese^{\prime}_{l}+1}\mu^{k+s+1+l+1}_{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}}
,
since
(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})_{\Delta}
is a diagonal bimodule itself whose structure maps are defined in (
2.26
)
(in fact, most of the maps vanish since
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}
is a dg category by Lemma
3.2
).
In other words, we define
\wedge_{\infty}
to be the pullback of the bimodule collapse map
\mu_{\Delta,(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})_{\Delta}}:(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})_{\Delta}\otimes_{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}}(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})_{\Delta}\to(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})_{\Delta}
for
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}
by
(\bar{y},\bar{y})^{*}
.

Thus we can define an extended cup product on
\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C},\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})
by

(3.28)

\cup_{\infty}:\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C},\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})\otimes\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C},\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sqcup}}{{\to}}\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C},\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\wedge_{\infty}}}{{\to}}\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C},\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}),

where
\sqcup:\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C},\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})\otimes\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C},\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})\to\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C},\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})
is the product (
2.106
) defined in §
2.5
.
Compare to a similar given in
[
41
]
using a method of graphical calculus.

Now we relate this cup product to the usual cup product (
2.103
) on
\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty},\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})
,
defined on the chain level as the composition

(3.29)

\widehat{\cup}_{\infty}:\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty},\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})\otimes\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty},\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sqcup}}{{\to}}\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty},\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}\otimes_{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}}\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mu_{\Delta,(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})_{\Delta}}}}{{\to}}\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty},\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}),

where
\mu_{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}}
is the map induced by the canonical collapse map of
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}
-bimodules

(3.30)

\mu_{\Delta,\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}}:(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})_{\Delta}\otimes_{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}}(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})_{\Delta}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})_{\Delta}

defined as in (
2.58
),
which is a quasi-isomorphism.

Proposition 3.10
.

The pullback

(3.31)

\mathrm{HH}^{*}(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty},\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})\to\mathrm{HH}^{*}(\mathcal{C},\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})

is a map of unital rings.

Proof.

Under the pullback by
\bar{y}:\mathcal{C}\to\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}
,
the bimodule
(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})_{\Delta}\otimes_{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}}(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})_{\Delta}
becomes
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}:=(\bar{y},\bar{y})^{*}(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})_{\Delta}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}(\bar{y},\bar{y})^{*}(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})_{\Delta}
,
such that the pullback of
\mu_{\Delta,(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})_{\Delta}}
agrees with
\wedge_{\infty}
(this is how
\wedge_{\infty}
is defined).
The statement then follows by the definition of (
3.28
) and the definition of the usual cup product on
\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty},\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})
by (
3.29
).
∎

3.3.
The residue

In this subsection, we shall present our main construction of a chain map of degree
1-n

(3.32)

\mathrm{res}:\mathrm{CC}_{n-1}(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})\to{\mathbf{k}},

which gives the weak proper Calabi-Yau structure on
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}
under the hypothesis of Theorem
1.7
.

Throughout the remaining subsections of §
3
,
we assume
\mathcal{C}
has a weak smooth Calabi-Yau structure
\sigma
of dimension
n
,
so that Lemma
2.28
provides a canonical quasi-isomorphism

-\cap\sigma:\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathrm{CC}_{*-n}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P})

for any
\mathcal{C}
-bimodule
\mathcal{P}
.
We will define a map

(3.33)

\mathrm{res}_{pre}:\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty},\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}\otimes_{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}}\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})\to{\mathbf{k}}[1-n]

of degree
1-n
.
By Proposition
2.35
, it suffices to construct a canonical pairing system (in the sense of Definition
2.33
) of degree
1-n

(3.34)

\pi_{\infty,X_{0},X_{1}}:\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}(X_{0},X_{1})\times\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}(X_{1},X_{0})\to{\mathbf{k}}[1-n]

for all
X_{0},X_{1}\in\mathrm{ob\ }\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}
.

Recall from (
3.13
) in Lemma
3.4
that the morphism spaces in
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}
can be computed as Hochschild cochain complexes of
\mathcal{C}^{op}
with coefficients in certain cones of
\mathcal{C}^{op}
-bimodules.
Writing the Hochschild chain complex in the shifted form (
2.67
), we have

(3.35)

\displaystyle\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})=

\displaystyle\bigoplus(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})_{\Delta}(X_{0},X_{k})\otimes\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}[1](X_{k-1},X_{k})\otimes\cdots\otimes\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}[1](X_{0},X_{1})

(3.36)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigoplus\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}(X_{k},X_{0})\otimes\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}[1](X_{k-1},X_{k})\otimes\cdots\otimes\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}[1](X_{0},X_{1})

(3.37)

\displaystyle=\bigoplus\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C}^{op},\mathcal{Z}_{X_{k}}^{X_{0}})\otimes\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C}^{op},\mathcal{Z}_{X_{k-1}}^{X_{k}})[1]\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C}^{op},\mathcal{Z}_{X_{0}}^{X_{1}})[1].

Here
\mathcal{Z}_{X}^{Y}
are defined in (
3.16
).

Lemma 3.11
.

The Hochschild differential on
\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})
,
with respect to the direct sum decomposition (
3.36
),
has the following formula

(3.38)

\begin{split}&d_{\mathrm{CC}_{*}}(a_{0}\otimes\mathbf{x}_{1;k})\\
=&(-1)^{\star_{0}^{0}}\mu_{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}}^{1}(a_{0})\otimes\mathbf{x}_{1;k}+(-1)^{\star_{1}^{0}}\mu_{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}}^{2}(x_{1},a_{0})\otimes\mathbf{x}_{2;k}+(-1)^{\star_{0}^{1}+|x_{k}|-1}\mu_{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}}^{2}(a_{0},x_{k})\otimes\mathbf{x}_{1;k-1}\\
&+\sum(-1)^{\maltese_{1;i}}a_{0}\otimes\mathbf{x}_{i+2;k}\otimes\mu_{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}}^{1}(x_{i+1})\otimes\mathbf{x}_{1;i}+\sum(-1)^{\maltese_{1;i}}a_{0}\otimes\mathbf{x}_{i+3;k}\otimes\mu_{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}}^{2}(x_{i+2},x_{i+1})\otimes\mathbf{x}_{1;i}.\end{split}

Proof.

By Lemma
3.2
,
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}
has
\mu^{k}_{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}}=0
for all
k\geq 3
.
In particular, the formula (
2.26
) implies that the only non-trivial structure maps for the diagonal bimodule
(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})_{\Delta}
(where
(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})_{\Delta}(X,Y)=\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}(Y,X)
) are

(3.39)

\mu^{0,0}_{(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})_{\Delta}}(a)=(-1)^{1}\mu^{1}_{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}}(a)=-\mu^{1}_{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}}(a),

(3.40)

\mu^{1,0}_{(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})_{\Delta}}(x,a)=(-1)^{1}\mu^{2}_{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}}(x,a)=-\mu^{2}_{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}}(x,a),

and

(3.41)

\mu^{0,1}_{(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})_{\Delta}}(a,x^{\prime})=(-1)^{|x^{\prime}|-1+1}\mu^{2}_{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}}(a,x^{\prime})=(-1)^{|x^{\prime}|}\mu^{2}_{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}}(a,x^{\prime}).

where we use the letter
a
to denote an element of the diagonal bimodule, and
x,x^{\prime}
from morphism spaces in
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}
.

The formula (
3.38
) follows from the general formula (
2.68
) for Hochschild chain differentials:

(i)

The first sum on the right hand side of (
2.68
) has only terms in three cases
i=0,j=0
or
i=1,j=0
or
i=0,j=1
, because the diagonal bimodule
(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})_{\Delta}
has only the above non-trivial structure maps (
3.39
), (
3.40
) and (
3.41
)

(ii)

The second sum has only terms in two cases:
j=1
or
j=2
, because the category
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}
has only non-trivial structure maps
\mu_{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}}^{1}
and
\mu_{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}}^{2}
.

∎

Corollary 3.12
.

The Hochschild differential on
\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})
,
with respect to the direct sum decomposition (
3.37
),
is the sum of the tensor product differential of the Hochschild differentials on the various Hochschild cochain complexes
\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C}^{op},\mathcal{Z}_{X_{i}}^{X_{i+1}})
(where
X_{k+1}=X_{0}
),
as well as maps

(3.42)

\begin{split}&\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C}^{op},\mathcal{Z}_{X_{k}}^{X_{0}})\otimes\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C}^{op},\mathcal{Z}_{X_{k-1}}^{X_{k}})[1]\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C}^{op},\mathcal{Z}_{X_{0}}^{X_{1}})[1]\\
\to\bigoplus&\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C}^{op},\mathcal{Z}_{X_{k}}^{X_{0}})\otimes\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C}^{op},\mathcal{Z}_{X_{k-1}}^{X_{k}})[1]\otimes\cdots\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C}^{op},\mathcal{Z}_{X_{i-1}}^{X_{i+1}})[1]\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C}^{op},\mathcal{Z}_{X_{0}}^{X_{1}})[1],\end{split}

induced by maps

(3.43)

\mu^{2}_{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}}:\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C}^{op},\mathcal{Z}_{X_{i}}^{X_{i+1}})\otimes\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C}^{op},\mathcal{Z}_{X_{i-1}}^{X_{i}})\to\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C}^{op},\mathcal{Z}_{X_{i-1}}^{X_{i+1}}).

Proof.

This follows from Lemma
3.11
and Lemma
3.4
.
∎

Now we return to the construction of the canonical pairing system (
3.34
).
First take the product (
2.106
)

(3.44)

\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}(X_{0},X_{1})\otimes\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}(X_{1},X_{0})=\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C}^{op},\mathcal{Z}_{X_{0}}^{X_{1}})\otimes\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C}^{op},\mathcal{Z}_{X_{1}}^{X_{0}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sqcup}}{{\to}}\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C}^{op},\mathcal{Z}_{X_{0}}^{X_{1}}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}\mathcal{Z}_{X_{1}}^{X_{0}}).

Since
\mathcal{C}
has a weak smooth Calabi-Yau structure, so does
\mathcal{C}^{op}
(via
\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{C})\cong\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{C}^{op})
on the chain level),
and by Lemma we have
2.28
a canonical quasi-isomorphism of chain complexes

(3.45)

-\cap\sigma:\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C}^{op},\mathcal{Z}_{X_{0}}^{X_{1}}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}\mathcal{Z}_{X_{1}}^{X_{0}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathrm{CC}_{*-n}(\mathcal{C}^{op},\mathcal{Z}_{X_{0}}^{X_{1}}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}\mathcal{Z}_{X_{1}}^{X_{0}}).

Now it suffices to define a chain map of degree
1
:

(3.46)

\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{C}^{op},\mathcal{Z}_{X_{0}}^{X_{1}}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}\mathcal{Z}_{X_{1}}^{X_{0}})\to{\mathbf{k}}[1].

This will be constructed using Proposition
2.35
.

Now we define the map (
3.46
) as follows.
By Lemma
3.5
, a typical element of
\mathcal{Z}_{X_{0}}^{X_{1}}(Y_{1},Y_{0})
is of the following form

(3.47)

(z\otimes f,\phi)\in\mathrm{cone}(\mathcal{C}(Y_{1},X_{1})\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}\mathcal{C}(Y_{0},X_{0})^{\vee}\to\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{C}(Y_{0},X_{0}),\mathcal{C}(Y_{1},X_{1}))),

and a typical element of
\mathcal{Z}_{X_{1}}^{X_{0}}(Y_{0},Y_{1})
is of the form

(3.48)

(w\otimes g,\psi)\in(\mathcal{C}(Y_{0},X_{0})\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}\mathcal{C}(Y_{1},X_{1})^{\vee}\to\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{C}(Y_{1},X_{1}),\mathcal{C}(Y_{0},X_{0}))).

Because of the grading convention on the mapping cone complex, the degrees should satisfy

(3.49)

\displaystyle|f|+|z|

\displaystyle=|\phi|+1,

(3.50)

\displaystyle|g|+|w|

\displaystyle=|\psi|+1.

We define a bilinear pairing:

(3.51)

\pi^{\mathcal{Z}}_{Y_{0},Y_{1}}:\mathcal{Z}_{X_{0}}^{X_{1}}(Y_{1},Y_{0})\times\mathcal{Z}_{X_{1}}^{X_{0}}(Y_{0},Y_{1})\to{\mathbf{k}}[1]

by the formula

(3.52)

\pi^{\mathcal{Z}}_{Y_{0},Y_{1}}((z\otimes f,\phi),(w\otimes g,\psi))=(-1)^{|z|+|f|}f(\psi(z))+(-1)^{|w|+|g|+|\phi||\psi|+|\phi|+|\psi|}g(\phi(w)).

That is, this is the pairing from (
2.181
) with formula (
2.182
).

Lemma 3.13
.

The pairings
\pi^{\mathcal{Z}}_{Y_{0},Y_{1}}
(
3.51
) form a canonical pairing system of degree
1
for the
\mathcal{C}^{op}-\mathcal{C}^{op}
-bimodules
\mathcal{Z}_{X_{0}}^{X_{1}}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{X_{1}}^{X_{0}}
.

Proof.

Since the
\mathcal{C}^{op}-\mathcal{C}^{op}
-bimodule structure on
\mathcal{Z}_{X_{0}}^{X_{1}}
is just formal consequence of the fact that a
\mathcal{C}-\mathcal{C}
-bimodule is automatically a
\mathcal{C}^{op}-\mathcal{C}^{op}
-bimodule when switching the left and right actions,
and since the condition (
2.141
) is completely symmetric in the entries from the category
\mathcal{C}
or
\mathcal{C}^{op}
,
Lemma
3.13
is equivalent to the statement that the pairings
\pi^{\mathcal{Z}}_{Y_{0},Y_{1}}
defined by the same formula (
3.51
) form a canonical pairing system of degree
1
for the
\mathcal{C}-\mathcal{C}
-bimodules.

Now we appeal to Lemma
2.39
and Corollary
2.41
to draw the conclusions.
The only difference is that the
\mu^{0,0}
-terms for
\mathcal{Z}_{X_{0}}^{X_{1}}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{X_{0}}^{X_{1}}
(
3.18
) have an extra term coming from the bimodule map
i
(
2.43
),
which has only a
i^{0,0}
-th term.
When applying
\mu^{0,0}_{\mathcal{Z}_{X_{0}}^{X_{1}}}
to
(z\otimes f,\phi)
and pairing it with
(w\otimes g,\psi)
we get an extra term of the form
(-1)^{*}f(w)g(z)
.
Similarly, the pairing
(z\otimes f,\phi)
with
\mu^{0,0}_{\mathcal{Z}_{X_{1}}^{X_{0}}}(w\otimes g,\psi)
also yields the same term,
where the signs (
2.37
), (
2.41
), (
3.51
) formula ensure that these terms add up to zero.
∎

Now apply Proposition
2.35
to the canonical pairing system
\pi^{\mathcal{Z}}_{Y_{0},Y_{1}}
(
3.51
) to obtain:

Corollary 3.14
.

The induced map

(3.53)

\pi^{\mathcal{Z}}_{*}:\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{C}^{op},\mathcal{Z}_{X_{0}}^{X_{1}}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}\mathcal{Z}_{X_{1}}^{X_{0}})=\mathcal{Z}_{X_{0}}^{X_{1}}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}^{op}-\mathcal{C}^{op}}\mathcal{Z}_{X_{1}}^{X_{0}}\to{\mathbf{k}}[1]

defined by

(3.54)

\pi^{\mathcal{Z}}_{*}((z\otimes f,\phi)\otimes\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1;l}\otimes(w\otimes g,\psi)\otimes\mathbf{x}_{1;k})=\begin{cases}\pi^{\mathcal{Z}}_{Y_{0},Y_{1}}((z\otimes f,\phi),(w\otimes g,\psi)),&\text{ if }k=l=0,\\
0,&\text{ if }k>0\text{ or }l>0,\end{cases}

is a chain map of degree
1
.

∎

There is one additional cyclically associativity property or the pairing
\pi^{\mathcal{Z}}
in the following sense.

Lemma 3.15
.

The following diagram commutes

(3.55)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Z}_{X_{2}}^{X_{0}}(Y_{2},Y_{0})\times\mathcal{Z}_{X_{1}}^{X_{2}}(Y_{1},Y_{2})\times\mathcal{Z}_{X_{0}}^{X_{1}}(Y_{0},Y_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Z}_{X_{1}}^{X_{0}}(Y_{1},Y_{0})\times\mathcal{Z}_{X_{0}}^{X_{1}}(Y_{0},Y_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Z}_{X_{2}}^{X_{0}}(Y_{2},Y_{0})\times\mathcal{Z}_{X_{0}}^{X_{1}}(Y_{0},Y_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{k}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{\mathrm{Calk}_{{\mathbf{k}}}}\times\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}\times c_{\mathrm{Calk}_{{\mathbf{k}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\mathcal{Z}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\mathcal{Z}}}

Proof.

This is a straightforward computation following the definition of the Calkin composition (
3.4
) and the formula of the pairing (
3.52
).
∎

Now we compose the chain map
\pi^{\mathcal{Z}}_{*}
(
3.53
)
with the quasi-isomorphism (
3.45
) and the product (
3.44
) to get a chain map of degree
1-n
:

(3.56)

\pi_{\infty,X_{0},X_{1}}:\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}(X_{0},X_{1})\otimes\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}(X_{1},X_{0})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sqcup}}{{\to}}\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C}^{op},\mathcal{Z}_{X_{0}}^{X_{1}}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}\mathcal{Z}_{X_{1}}^{X_{0}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle-\cap\sigma}}{{\to}}\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{C}^{op},\mathcal{Z}_{X_{0}}^{X_{1}}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}\mathcal{Z}_{X_{1}}^{X_{0}})[-n]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi^{\mathcal{Z}}_{*}}}{{\to}}{\mathbf{k}}[1-n].

which gives the pairing
\pi_{\infty,X_{0},X_{1}}
(
3.34
).

Lemma 3.16
.

The pairing (
3.56
) is graded symmetric with sign
(-1)^{1+(|c_{0}|-1)(|c_{1}|-1)}
.

Proof.

Since the pairing
\pi^{\mathcal{Z}}_{Y_{0},Y_{1}}
(
3.51
) which was defined by (
2.181
) is graded symmetric by Lemma
2.40
,
it follows by the definition (
3.54
) that
\pi^{\mathcal{Z}}_{*}
,
which only takes nonzero values on the length zero tensors (Definition
2.21
) in the subspace
\mathcal{Z}_{X_{0}}^{X_{1}}(Y_{0},Y_{1})\otimes\mathcal{Z}_{X_{1}}^{X_{0}}(Y_{1},Y_{0})
of the two-sided bar complex
\mathcal{Z}_{X_{0}}^{X_{1}}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}^{op}-\mathcal{C}^{op}}\mathcal{Z}_{X_{1}}^{X_{0}}=\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{C}^{op},\mathcal{Z}_{X_{0}}^{X_{1}}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}\mathcal{Z}_{X_{1}}^{X_{0}})
,
is also graded symmetric in the sense that it takes the same value as the other map

(3.57)

\pi^{\mathcal{Z}}_{*}:\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{C}^{op},\mathcal{Z}_{X_{1}}^{X_{0}}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}\mathcal{Z}_{X_{0}}^{X_{1}})\to{\mathbf{k}},

defined in the same manner, up to the sign twist specified by Lemma
2.40
.
∎

To get the desired map (
3.32
), we need to appeal to Proposition
2.35
again,
which requires that we prove the following:

Lemma 3.17
.

The pairings
\pi_{\infty,X_{0},X_{1}}
(
3.34
) defined by (
3.56
) form a canonical pairing system between the diagonal bimodule
(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})_{\Delta}
and itself.

Proof.

We need to check the condition (
2.141
) for
\pi_{\infty,X_{0},X_{1}}
.
By construction, the map
\pi_{\infty,X_{0},X_{1}}
is a chain map from the tensor product
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}(X_{0},X_{1})\otimes\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}(X_{1},X_{0})
to
{\mathbf{k}}
,
so the first set of equations in (
2.141
) with
k=l=0
is satisfied.
Since
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}
has
\mu^{k}_{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}}=0
for all
k\geq 3
,
the only remaining equations to check are

(3.58)

(-1)^{|c_{1}|-1}\pi_{\infty}(\mu^{0,1}_{(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})_{\Delta}}(c_{0},x^{\prime}),c_{1})+\pi_{\infty}(c_{0},\mu^{1,0}_{(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})_{\Delta}}(x^{\prime},c_{1}))=0,

and

(3.59)

(-1)^{(|x|-1)(|c_{0}|+|c_{1}|)+|c_{1}|-1}\pi_{\infty}(\mu^{1,0}_{(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})_{\Delta}}(x,c_{0}),c_{1})+\pi_{\infty}(c_{0},\mu^{0,1}_{(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})_{\Delta}}(c_{1},x))=0.

Let us check (
3.58
);
the other is analogous, which is essentially the same as (
3.58
) using Lemma
3.16
.
By (
3.40
) and (
3.41
),
the equation (
3.58
) is equivalent to

(3.60)

\pi_{\infty}((-1)^{|x^{\prime}|+|c_{1}|}\mu^{2}_{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}}(c_{0},x^{\prime}),c_{1})=\pi_{\infty}(c_{0},\mu^{2}_{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}}(x^{\prime},c_{1})),

or

(3.61)

\pi_{\infty}((-1)^{|x^{\prime}|}\mu^{2}_{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}}(c_{0},x^{\prime}),c_{1})=\pi_{\infty}(c_{0},(-1)^{|c_{1}|}\mu^{2}_{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}}(x^{\prime},c_{1})),

Recall from (
2.120
) that the map
-\cap\sigma
is defined as the following composition

(3.62)

\begin{split}&\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C}^{op},\mathcal{Z}_{X_{0}}^{X_{1}}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}\mathcal{Z}_{X_{1}}^{X_{0}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle-\sqcap\sigma}}{{\to}}\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{C}^{op},(\mathcal{C}^{op})_{\Delta}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}\mathcal{Z}_{X_{0}}^{X_{1}}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}\mathcal{Z}_{X_{1}}^{X_{0}})[-n]\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mu_{\Delta,*}}}{{\to}}\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{C}^{op},\mathcal{Z}_{X_{0}}^{X_{1}}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}\mathcal{Z}_{X_{1}}^{X_{0}})[-n],\end{split}

where
\mu_{\Delta}=\mu_{\Delta,\mathcal{Z}_{X_{0}}^{X_{1}}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}\mathcal{Z}_{X_{1}}^{X_{0}}}
is the collapse map (
2.58
) for the bimodule
\mathcal{Z}_{X_{0}}^{X_{1}}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}\mathcal{Z}_{X_{1}}^{X_{0}}
.
Thus
\pi_{\infty}
is given as the following composition

(3.63)

\begin{split}&\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C}^{op},\mathcal{Z}_{X_{0}}^{X_{1}})\otimes\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C}^{op},\mathcal{Z}_{X_{1}}^{X_{0}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sqcup}}{{\to}}\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C}^{op},\mathcal{Z}_{X_{0}}^{X_{1}}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}\mathcal{Z}_{X_{1}}^{X_{0}})\\
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle-\sqcap\sigma}}{{\to}}&\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{C}^{op},(\mathcal{C}^{op})_{\Delta}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}\mathcal{Z}_{X_{0}}^{X_{1}}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}\mathcal{Z}_{X_{1}}^{X_{0}})[-n]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mu_{\Delta,*}}}{{\to}}\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{C}^{op},\mathcal{Z}_{X_{0}}^{X_{1}}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}\mathcal{Z}_{X_{1}}^{X_{0}})[-n]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi^{\mathcal{Z}}_{*}}}{{\to}}{\mathbf{k}}[1-n],\end{split}

i.e,

(3.64)

\pi_{\infty}(c_{0},c_{1})=\pi^{\mathcal{Z}}_{*}(\mu_{\Delta,*}((c_{0}\sqcup c_{1})\sqcap\sigma)).

Then (
3.61
) follows from the product relation (
2.116
) in Proposition
2.26
,
the sign twist in defining
\mu^{2}_{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}}
(
3.21
),
as well as Lemma
3.15
.
∎

By Lemma
3.17
and Proposition
2.35
, we get the desired chain map of degree
1-n

\mathrm{res}_{pre}:\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty},(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})_{\Delta}\otimes_{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}}(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})_{\Delta})\to{\mathbf{k}}[1-n]

as proposed in (
3.33
).
Since quasi-isomorphisms of
A_{\infty}
-bimodules are invertible,
we choose a homotopy inverse

(3.65)

\gamma:(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})_{\Delta}\to(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})_{\Delta}\otimes_{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}}(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})_{\Delta}

of the collapse map
\mu_{\Delta,(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})_{\Delta}}:(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})_{\Delta}\otimes_{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}}(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})_{\Delta}\to(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})_{\Delta}
(
2.58
),
which induces a quasi-isomorphism

(3.66)

\gamma_{*}:\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty},(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})_{\Delta})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty},(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})_{\Delta}\otimes_{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}}(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})_{\Delta}).

The composition of (
3.33
) with this map (
3.66
) yields the desire residue map (
3.32
):

(3.67)

\mathrm{res}=\mathrm{res}_{pre}\circ\gamma_{*}:\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})=\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty},(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})_{\Delta})\to{\mathbf{k}}[1-n].

Summarizing the above discussion throughout this subsection, we have thus proved:

Proposition 3.18
.

The map
\mathrm{res}=\mathrm{res}_{pre}\circ\gamma_{*}
(
3.67
) is a chain map of degree
1-n
.

∎

3.4.
Nondegeneracy of the induced pairing

Given the chain map of degree
1-n

\mathrm{res}:\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})\to{\mathbf{k}}[1-n]

defined by (
3.67
),
we define a pairing on
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}
as the following composition

(3.68)

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathrm{res}}:\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}^{*}(X,Y)\otimes\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}^{n-1-*}(Y,X)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mu^{2}_{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}}}}{{\to}}\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}^{n-1}(Y,Y)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i_{Y}}}{{\to}}\mathrm{CC}_{n-1}(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{res}}}{{\to}}{\mathbf{k}},

where
i_{Y}:\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}(Y,Y)\to\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})
is the canonical inclusion.

Proposition 3.19
.

The pairing
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathrm{res}}
(
3.68
) is nondegenerate on the level of cohomology groups.

Proof.

Observe that the map
i_{Y}:\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}(Y,Y)\to\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})
is the canonical inclusion of chain complexes, i.e.,
the map

(3.69)

\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}^{*}(Y,Y)\to\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\geq 0\\
X_{0},\ldots,X_{k}\in\mathrm{ob\ }\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}\end{subarray}}\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}^{*}(X_{k},X_{0})\otimes\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}^{*}(X_{k-1},X_{k})[1]\otimes\cdots\otimes\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}^{*}(X_{0},X_{1})[1],

including into the
k=0
piece.

We consider the following diagram

(3.70)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}^{*}(X,Y)\otimes\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}^{n-1-*}(Y,X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{CC}^{n-1}(\mathcal{C}^{op},\mathcal{Z}_{X}^{Y}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}\mathcal{Z}_{Y}^{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{CC}_{-1}(\mathcal{C}^{op},\mathcal{Z}_{X}^{Y}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}\mathcal{Z}_{Y}^{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}^{n-1}(Y,Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{CC}_{n-1}(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty},(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})_{\Delta}\otimes_{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}}(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})_{\Delta})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{CC}_{n-1}(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sqcup}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}}^{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\cap\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circ_{\mathrm{Calk}_{{\mathbf{k}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\mathcal{Z}}_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{res}_{pre}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{{\Delta},(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})_{\Delta},*}}

The arrow

(3.71)

\iota:\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}^{*}(X,Y)\otimes\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}^{n-1-*}(Y,X)\to\mathrm{CC}_{n-1}(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty},(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})_{\Delta}\otimes_{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}}(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})_{\Delta})

is the inclusion of “length zero” tensors in the two-sided bar complex
(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})_{\Delta}\otimes_{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}}(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})_{\Delta}
for computing
\mathrm{CC}_{n-1}(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty},(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})_{\Delta}\otimes_{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}-\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}}(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})_{\Delta})
.
By the formula (
2.59
) for the collapse map (
2.58
),
the left triangle strictly commutes.
Thus, by choosing the homotopy inverse
\gamma:(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})_{\Delta}\to(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})_{\Delta}\otimes_{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}}(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})_{\Delta}
(
3.65
) whose induced map on Hochschild cochain complexes
\gamma_{*}
(
3.66
),
the composition

(3.72)

\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}^{*}(X,Y)\otimes\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}^{n-1-*}(Y,X)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mu_{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}}^{2}}}{{\to}}\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}^{n-1}(Y,Y)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i_{Y}}}{{\to}}\mathrm{CC}_{n-1}(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\gamma_{*}}}{{\to}}\mathrm{CC}_{n-1}(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty},(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})_{\Delta}\otimes_{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}}(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})_{\Delta})

is chain homotopic to the inclusion of “length zero” tensors,
\iota
(
3.71
).

Recall from Corollary (
3.14
) that the formula (
3.54
) for the chain map
\pi^{\mathcal{Z}}_{*}:\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{C}^{op},\mathcal{Z}_{X_{0}}^{X_{1}}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}\mathcal{Z}_{X_{1}}^{X_{0}})\to{\mathbf{k}}[1]
(
3.53
)
only takes nonzero values only for tensors that have zero length in
\mathbf{x}
-entries and
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}
-entries in the two-sided bar complex for computing
\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{C}^{op},\mathcal{Z}_{X_{0}}^{X_{1}}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}\mathcal{Z}_{X_{1}}^{X_{0}})
.
Going along the top horizontal row then followed by
\pi^{\mathcal{Z}}_{*}
gives the pairing
\pi_{\infty,X,Y}
(
3.34
),
which induces the map
\mathrm{res}_{pre}
in the middle row,
whose restriction to “length zero” tensors via the map
\iota
(
3.71
) is exactly the nondegenerate pairing
\pi_{\infty,X,Y}
(
3.34
),

(3.73)

\pi_{\infty,X,Y}=\mathrm{res}_{pre}\circ\iota.

On the other hand, the composition of (
3.72
) with
\mathrm{res}_{pre}
is by definition the residue pairing
\langle\cdot,\cdot,\rangle_{\mathrm{res}}
, i.e.,

(3.74)

\langle\cdot,\cdot,\rangle_{\mathrm{res}}=\mathrm{res}_{pre}\circ\gamma_{*}\circ i_{Y}\circ\mu_{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}}^{2}.

Since
\gamma_{*}\circ i_{Y}\circ\mu_{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}}^{2}
is chain homotopic to
\iota
,
it follows that the cohomology-level pairing induced by
\langle\cdot,\cdot,\rangle_{\mathrm{res}}
agrees with the induced pairing by
\pi_{\infty,X,Y}
,
which is nondegenerate.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.7
.

By Proposition
3.18
, the map
\mathrm{res}:\mathcal{C}_{*}(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})\to{\mathbf{k}}[1-n]
(
3.67
) is a chain map.
Its induced pairing is nondegenerate on cohomology groups by Proposition
3.19
.
Thus the proof of Theorem
1.7
is complete.
∎

4.
Orlov’s singularity category on a proper singular scheme

4.1.
Some basic definitions

Let
f:X\to\mathrm{Spec}({\mathbf{k}})
be a separated scheme of finite type over a field
{\mathbf{k}}
.
Let
QCoh(X)
be the dg enhancement of unbounded derived category of quasi-coherent sheaves.

QCoh(X)
is compactly generated,
and the compact objects are exactly the perfect complexes
\mathrm{Perf}(X)
.
Let
Coh(X)\subset QCoh(X)
be the full dg subcategory of objects with bounded coherent cohomology sheaves.
For a smooth
X
, we have
\mathrm{Perf}(X)=Coh(X)
;
and for a singular
X
, the inclusions
\mathrm{Perf}(X)\subset Coh(X)\subset QCoh(X)
are strictly proper.
However, by
[
36
, Theorem 6.3]
, the dg category
Coh(X)
is always smooth regardless whether
X
is smooth or not.

The dg category
\mathrm{Ind}Coh(X)
ind-coherent sheaves is by definition the ind-completion of
Coh(X)
,
in which the compact objects are
Coh(X)
.
Let
D_{X}=f^{!}{\mathbf{k}}\in\mathrm{Ind}Coh(X)
be the dualizing complex,
where

f^{!}:\mathrm{Mod}_{{\mathbf{k}}}\to\mathrm{Ind}Coh(X)

is the right adjoint to pushforward

f_{*}:\mathrm{Ind}Coh(X)\to\mathrm{Mod}_{{\mathbf{k}}},

which is the unique colimit-preserving extension of
f_{*}:Coh(X)\to\mathrm{Mod}_{{\mathbf{k}}}
.
We say that
X
is
Cohen-Macaulay
of dimension
n
if
\omega_{X}=D_{X}[-n]
is a coherent sheaf.
We say that
X
is
Gorenstein
of dimension
n
if
\omega_{X}
is tensor-invertible, i.e., a line bundle.

Suppose now
f:X\to\mathrm{Spec}({\mathbf{k}})
is proper.
Then
f_{*}:\mathrm{Ind}Coh(X)\to\mathrm{Mod}_{{\mathbf{k}}}
sends compact objects to compact objects,
whose right adjoint
f^{!}
is therefore continuous and sends compact objects to compact objects.
In this case, the dualizing complex
D_{X}=f^{!}{\mathbf{k}}
lives in
Coh(X)
.
We say an object
\mathcal{E}\in Coh(X)
is
!
-perfect,
if the object
R\mathcal{H}om_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}(\mathcal{E},D_{X})
is perfect.
Denote by
!\!-\!\mathrm{Perf}(X)
the full dg subcategory of
!
-perfect objects.
Clearly, by Grothendieck-Verdier duality,
we have a quasi-equivalence

!\!-\!\mathrm{Perf}(X)\simeq\mathrm{Perf}(X)^{op}.

Thus we obtain a quasi-equivalence

(4.1)

\mathcal{D}^{b}_{sg}(X)^{op}\simeq Coh(X)/!\!-\!\mathrm{Perf}(X).

In addition, it is observed:

Proposition 4.1

(
[
23
, Proposition B.1]
)
.

Let
{\mathbf{k}}
be a perfect field,
and
X
a separated scheme of finite type over
{\mathbf{k}}
.
Then the dg category
\mathrm{Prop}Coh(X)
is quasi-equivalent to the dg category
!\!-\!\mathrm{Perf}(X)_{prop}
of
!
-perfect complexes with proper support.

By composing this with the functor from (
3.11
),
we get a composite functor

(4.2)

\mathcal{D}^{b}_{sg}(X)^{op}=\mathcal{D}^{b}Coh(X)/\mathrm{Perf}(X)^{op}\simeq\mathcal{D}^{b}Coh(X)/!\!-\!\mathrm{Perf}(X)\simeq\mathcal{D}^{b}Coh(X)/\mathrm{Prop}D^{b}Coh(X)\to\widehat{\mathcal{D}^{b}Coh(X)}_{\infty}.

Using this, Efimov proved:

Theorem 4.2

(
[
23
, Theorem 9.2]
)
.

The functor
\mathcal{D}^{b}_{sg}(X)^{op}\to\widehat{\mathcal{D}^{b}Coh(X)}_{\infty}
is a quasi-equivalence.

4.2.
Calabi-Yau structures on the singularity category

For
X
a proper scheme of finite type over
{\mathbf{k}}
,
the dg category
\mathcal{D}^{b}Coh(X)
is smooth but not proper,
(which will be proper if
X
is smooth).
It has the desirable property to support a smooth Calabi-Yau structure when
X
is Gorenstein:

Proposition 4.3

(
[
11
]
[Proposition 5.12])
.

Let
X
be a Gorenstein scheme of dimension
n
.
Then giving a strong smooth Calabi-Yau structure on
\mathcal{D}^{b}Coh(X)
is equivalent to giving a trivialization of
\omega_{X}\simeq\mathcal{O}_{X}
.

We can now prove Theorem
1.6
:

Proof.

By Proposition
4.3
,
a trivialization of
\omega_{X}\simeq\mathcal{O}_{X}
gives rise to a strong smooth Calabi-Yau structure on
\mathcal{D}^{b}Coh(X)
of dimension
n
,
which in particular induces a weak smooth Calabi-Yau structure on
\mathcal{D}^{b}Coh(X)
of dimension
n
,
Then Theorem
1.7
implies that
\widehat{\mathcal{D}^{b}Coh(X)}_{\infty}
has a weak proper Calabi-Yau structure of dimension
n-1
.
By the equivalence (
4.2
) from Theorem
4.2
,
we deduce that
\mathcal{D}^{b}_{sg}(X)^{op}
has a weak proper Calabi-Yau structure of dimension
n-1
,
so does
\mathcal{D}^{b}_{sg}(X)
.
∎

5.
The Rabinowitz Fukaya category

In this section, we provide a shortcut to the construction of the Rabinowitz Fukaya category.
Still, we set up Floer theory using quadratic Hamiltonians following
[
3
,
28
]
,
as we find it particular simple for our algebraic argument.

5.1.
Geometric preliminaries

A
Liouville domain
is a compact symplectic manifold-with-boundary with an exact symplectic form
\omega=d\lambda
,
where
\lambda
is called a
Liouville one-form
,
such that its dual vector field
Z
, called a
Liouville vector field
defined by
\lambda=i_{Z}\omega
,
is outward pointing along the boundary.
An exact symplectic manifold
(X,\omega)
is called a
Liouville manifold
,
if there exists a Liouville domain
X_{0}\subset X
such that the positive flow of
\partial X_{0}
under
Z
is defined for all time and the map

(5.1)

X_{0}\cup_{\partial X_{0}}\partial X_{0}\times[1,+\infty)\to X

is a diffeomorphism.
In other words,
X
has a positive cylindrical end,
modeled on the contact manifold
\partial X_{0}
equipped with the contact form
\alpha|_{\partial X_{0}}
.
Different choices of
\partial X_{0}
are contactomorphic,
so there is a well-defined contact manifold
(\partial_{\infty}X,\xi)
,
which we call the
boundary at infinity
of
X
.
We shall assume
c_{1}(X)=0
for the rest of the paper.

A Lagrangian submanifold
L\subset X
is
exact
,
if
\lambda|_{L}=df
for some
f:L\to{\mathbb{R}}
called a
primitive
for
L
.

L
is said to be
cylindrical
, if outside of a compact set,
L
is invariant under the flow of
Z
.
These conditions imply that an exact cylindrical submanifold
L
also has a positive cylindrical end

(5.2)

L=L_{0}\cup_{\partial L_{0}}\partial L_{0}\times[1,+\infty),

modeled on the Legendrian submanifold
l=\partial L_{0}\subset\partial X_{0}
.
Moreover, the primitive
f_{L}:L\to{\mathbb{R}}
is locally constant in the cylindrical end.
The Legendrian isotopy class is independent of choices of cylindrical ends,
so there is a well-defined Legendrian submanifold
\partial_{\infty}L\subset\partial_{\infty}X
,
which we call the
boundary at infinity
of
L
.
Suppose
L
comes with a grading in the sense of
[
44
]
,
and has a Spin structure.

We will fix, once for all, an at most countable collection

(5.3)

{\mathbb{L}}=\{L_{1},L_{2},\ldots|L_{i}\text{ exact, cylindrical, equipped with a grading and a Spin structure}\}.

of exact cylindrical Lagrangian submanifolds,
for which there exists a generic choice of Liouville form such that

(5.4)

\begin{split}&\text{ all Reeb orbits of }\alpha\text{ are non-degenerate, and }\\
&\text{all Reeb chords from the Legendrian }\partial_{\infty}L_{i}\text{ to }\partial_{\infty}L_{j}\text{ are non-degenerate},\forall L_{i},L_{j}\in{\mathbb{L}}.\end{split}

A Hamiltonian
H:X\to{\mathbb{R}}
is said to be
admissible
or
quadratic at infinity
,
if outside of a compact set,
H
only depends on the radial coordinate on
\partial X_{0}\times[1,+\infty)
via the identification (
5.1
),
and satisfies

(5.5)

H(y,r)=r^{2}.

The space of admissible Hamiltonians is denoted by

(5.6)

\mathcal{H}(X).

An
\omega
-compatible almost complex structure
J
is said to be
h
-rescaled contact type
on the cylindrical end,
if it satisfies

(5.7)

\frac{h}{r}\lambda\circ J=dr

for some
h>0
on the cylindrical end.
The space of all
h
-rescaled contact type almost complex structures is denoted by

(5.8)

\mathcal{J}_{h}(X).

5.2.
Popsicles

In this subsection we briefly review the definition of popsicles,
originally introduced in
[
2
]
,
and adapted in
[
28
]
to construct the
A_{\infty}
-structure on the Rabinowitz Fukaya category.

Let
k\geq 1
be a positive integer.
Let
\mathbf{p}:F\to\{1,\ldots,d\}
a map from a finite set,
and put
p_{f}=\mathbf{p}(f)
for
f\in F
.
Let
S
be a connected bordered Riemann surface of genus zero with
k+1
boundary punctures
z_{0},z_{1},\ldots z_{k}
ordered cyclically along the boundary.

Definition 5.1
.

A popsicle structure
\eta
on
S
of flavor
\mathbf{p}
consists of a collection of choices of preferred points
\{\eta_{f}\}_{f\in F}
,
with each
\eta_{f}
on the unique hyperbolic geodesic
C_{p_{f}}
connecting
z_{0}
to
z_{p}
.

We call the pair
(S,\eta)
a popsicle of flavor
\mathbf{p}
.

A popsicle
(S,\eta)
of flavor
\mathbf{p}
is
stable
if
k+|F|\geq 2
.
The stable popsicles form a moduli space

(5.9)

\mathcal{R}^{k+1,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{w}},

which can be compactified by adding broken popsicles.
Consider a ribbon tree
T
with
k
leaves and one root,
such that the valency
|v|
of each vertex
v\in V(T)
is at least two.
Let
\vec{F}=\{F_{v}\}_{v\in V(T)}
be a decomposition of
F
indexed vertices of
T
,
and
\mathbf{p}_{v}:F_{v}\to\{1,\ldots,|v|-1\}
be the map induced by
\mathbf{p}
.
A broken popsicle of type
T
of flavor
\vec{\mathbf{p}}=\{\mathbf{p}_{v}\}_{v\in V(T)}
is a collection of popsicles
\{(S_{v},\eta_{v})\}_{v\in V(T)}
,
each of flavor
F_{v}
for each vertex
v
.
A broken popsicle is stable if every component
(S_{v},\eta_{v})
is stable, which means
|v|+|F_{v}|\geq 3
.
The stable ones form a moduli space

(5.10)

\mathcal{R}^{T,\vec{\mathbf{p}}}=\prod_{v\in V(T)}\mathcal{R}^{|v|,\mathbf{p}_{v}}.

Define the moduli space of broken popsicles with
k
leaves and one root to be

(5.11)

\bar{\mathcal{R}}^{k+1,\mathbf{p}}=\coprod_{\begin{subarray}{c}T,\vec{F}\\
T\text{ has }k\text{ leaves and one root }\end{subarray}}\mathcal{R}^{T,\vec{\mathbf{p}}}.

This is a compact manifold with corners (
[
2
]
).
There is a natural action of the group of automorphisms
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbf{p})\subset\mathrm{Sym}(F)
of
F
preserving
\mathbf{p}
on the moduli space,
which is a trivial action if
\mathbf{p}
is injective.

Now we assign
weights
to popsicles.
Consider non-negative integers
w_{i}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\leq 0},i=0,1,\ldots,k
satisfying

(5.12)

w_{0}=w_{1}+\cdots+w_{k}+|F|

and

(5.13)

|\mathbf{p}^{-1}(i)\leq-w_{i},\forall i=1,\ldots,k.

Assign the weight
w_{i}
to the puncture
z_{i}
of a popsicle
(S,\eta)
of flavor
\mathbf{p}
.
Put
\mathbf{w}=(w_{0},\ldots,w_{k})
.
Denote the resulting moduli space with this decoration by
\mathcal{R}^{k+1,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{w}}
.

The weights are automatically inherited by broken popsicles,
such that both (
5.12
) and (
5.13
) are satisfied on each smooth component
S_{v}
labeled by some vertex
v\in V(T)
.
Denote by
\mathbf{w}_{v}
the collection of weights on that component.
