The compactified moduli space of broken popsicles is

(5.14)

\bar{\mathcal{R}}^{k+1,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{w}}=\coprod_{\begin{subarray}{c}T,\vec{F}\\
T\text{ has }k\text{ leaves and one root }\end{subarray}}\mathcal{R}^{T,\vec{\mathbf{p}},\vec{\mathbf{w}}}=\coprod_{\begin{subarray}{c}T,\vec{F}\\
T\text{ has }k\text{ leaves and one root }\end{subarray}}\prod_{v\in V(T)}\mathcal{R}^{|v|,\mathbf{p}_{v},\mathbf{w}_{v}}.

We shall be only interested in the case where
w_{i}\in\{-1,0\}
.
Although this condition is not always preserved when passing to boundary strata of the compactified moduli space
\bar{\mathcal{R}}^{k+1,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{w}}
,
but we will observe:

Lemma 5.2

(
[
47
, Lemma 4.2]
)
.

Let
\mathbf{w}
be a collection of weights satisfying
w_{i}\in\{-1,0\}
.
Consider a broken popsicle in the codimension-one boundary stratum of
\bar{\mathcal{R}}^{k+1,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{w}}
modeled on a tree
T
with two vertices
v_{2},v_{1}
with an edge going from
v_{2}
to
v_{1}
.
Suppose there is an inherited weight that does not belong to
-1,0
, i.e., for some
i
we have

(5.15)

w_{v_{1},i}=w_{v_{2},0}<-1.

Then exactly one of the following is true:

(i)

|\mathbf{p}_{v_{1}}^{-1}(i)|\geq 2
, or

(ii)

w_{v_{1},0}=w_{0}=-1,w_{v_{1},i}=w_{v_{2},0}=-2
,
and
\mathbf{p}_{v_{1}}^{-1}(i)=\{f\}
is a singleton set.
In this case, there are exactly two possible values for
k=\mathbf{p}(j_{v_{1}}(f))
satisfying
w_{k}=w_{v_{2},k-i+1}=-1
and
\mathbf{p}_{v_{2}}^{-1}(k-i+1)=\varnothing
.

5.3.
Pseudoholomorphic maps from popsicles

Fix a quadratic Hamiltonian
H\in\mathcal{H}(X)
and a time-dependent almost complex structure
J_{t}:[0,1]\to\mathcal{J}_{1}(X)
.
To define pseudoholomorphic maps from popsicles to the target Liouville manifold,
we shall introduce
Floer data
on popsicles.
The reader is referred to
[
3
,
29
,
28
]
for more detailed discussions regarding Floer data (in which the Hamiltonians are quadratic),
but for completeness and easy references in later sections,
we shall include the basic definitions here.
The weight
w_{i}
determines a rule for assigning either a positive or a negative strip-like end near the puncture
z_{i}
.
To describe the rule, we introduce a function

(5.16)

\delta_{i}=\begin{cases}0,&\text{ if }i=0,w_{i}<0\text{ or }i=1,w_{i}=0,\\
-1,&\text{ if }i=0,w_{i}=0\text{ or }i=1,w_{i}<0.\end{cases}

Also define the associated sign symbols

(5.17)

\mathfrak{s}_{i}=\begin{cases}+,&\text{ if }i=0,w_{i}<0\text{ or }i=1,w_{i}=0,\\
-,&\text{ if }i=0,w_{i}=0\text{ or }i=1,w_{i}<0.\end{cases}

Denote by

(5.18)

\displaystyle Z^{+}

\displaystyle=[0,+\infty)\times[0,1],

(5.19)

\displaystyle Z^{-}

\displaystyle=(-\infty,0]\times[0,1].

the positive and the negative infinite half-strips.

Definition 5.3
.

A Floer datum
\mathbf{D}_{S,\eta}
for a
\mathbf{w}
-weighted popsicle
(S,\eta)
of flavor
\mathbf{p}
consists of:

(i)

A collection of real numbers
\nu_{i}\geq 1,i=0,\ldots,k
satisfying

(5.20)

\sum_{i=0}^{k}(-1)^{\delta_{i}}\nu_{i}\leq 0.

(ii)

A collection of strip-like ends

(5.21)

\epsilon_{i}:Z^{\mathfrak{s}_{i}}:\to S,i=0,\ldots,k.

These depend on the weights
\mathbf{w}
following the rule by (
5.17
).

(iii)

A rescaling function
\rho_{S}:S\to[1,+\infty)
that is constant and equal to
\nu_{i}
over the strip-like end
\epsilon_{i}
, for every
i=0,\ldots,k
.

(iv)

A one-form
\alpha_{S}
on
S
such that
\alpha_{S}|_{\partial S}=0,d\alpha_{S}\leq 0
and compatible with strip-like ends in the sense that

(5.22)

\epsilon_{i}^{*}\alpha_{S}=\nu_{i}dt.

(v)

A family of Hamiltonians
H_{S}:S\to\mathcal{H}(X)
compatible with strip-like ends in the sense that

(5.23)

\epsilon_{i}^{*}H_{S}=\frac{H\circ\psi^{\nu_{i}}}{\nu_{i}^{2}},i=0,\ldots,k.

(vi)

A domain-dependent family of almost complex structures
J_{S}
such that at every point
z\in S
,
J_{S,z}\in\mathcal{J}_{\rho_{S}(z)}(X)
, and compatible with strip-like ends in the sense that

(5.24)

\epsilon_{i}^{*}J_{S}=(\psi^{\nu_{i}})^{*}J_{t},i=0,\ldots,k.

In addition, the data
\rho_{S},\alpha_{S},H_{S},J_{S}
must be invariant under the action of
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbf{p})
,
which is naturally lifted to the space of functions on
S
valued in
[1,+\infty)
, the space of one-forms on
S
,
the space of smooth maps from
S
to
\mathcal{H}(X)
,
and the space of smooth maps from
S
to
\mathcal{J}(X)
.
We call such a Floer datum
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbf{p})
-invariant.

We can also extend the definition to an unstable domain
Z
by requiring

(5.25)

\rho_{Z}\equiv 1,\alpha_{Z}\equiv dt,H_{Z}\equiv H\text{ and }J_{Z}\equiv J_{t}.

Definition 5.4
.

Two Floer data
\mathbf{D}_{S,\eta},\mathbf{D}^{\prime}_{S,\eta}
are said to be conformally equivalent,
if there exist
K>0,C>0
such that

(5.26)

\displaystyle\nu^{\prime}_{i}

\displaystyle=K\nu_{i},

(5.27)

\displaystyle\rho^{\prime}_{S}

\displaystyle=K\rho_{S},

(5.28)

\displaystyle\alpha^{\prime}_{S}

\displaystyle=K\alpha_{S},

(5.29)

\displaystyle H^{\prime}_{S}

\displaystyle=\frac{H_{S}\circ\psi^{K}}{K^{2}}+C,

(5.30)

\displaystyle J^{\prime}_{S}

\displaystyle=(\psi^{K})^{*}J_{S}.

To ensure that the moduli spaces of pseudoholomorphic maps defined with respect to choices of Floer data as above have good compactifications, we introduce the following notion, following
[
3
]
(see also
[
28
, §3.5]
):

Definition 5.5
.

A universal and conformally consistent choice of Floer data
\mathbf{D}_{\mathcal{R}}
for all disks with popsicle structures is a choice of Floer data,
one for each representative
(S,\eta)
of element in
\bar{\mathcal{R}}^{k+1,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{w}}
,
being
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbf{p})
-invariant and smoothly varying over
\mathcal{R}^{k+1,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{w}}
,
which at boundary strata agree with a product Floer data chosen for lower dimensional moduli spaces up to conformal equivalence.

All the choices as part of a Floer datum
D_{S,\eta}
as in Definition
5.3
form a contractible space;
so by a standard induction argument, a universal and conformally consistent choice of Floer data
\mathbf{D}_{\mathcal{R}}
for all disks with popsicle structures exists; see
[
3
,
46
,
29
]
, etc.

For boundary conditions of the pseudoholomorphic maps, we introduce:

Definition 5.6
.

A Lagrangian label for
(S,\eta)
is a collection of exact cylindrical Lagrangian submanifolds
L_{i}\in{\mathbb{L}},i=0,\ldots,k
where
{\mathbb{L}}
is defined in (
5.3
),
with each
L_{i}
assigned to the component of
\partial S
between the punctures
z_{i}
and
z_{i+1}
,
\mathrm{mod}

k+1
cyclically.

For
i=1,\ldots,k
, let

(5.31)

x_{i}\in\begin{cases}\chi(L_{i-1},L_{i};H),&\text{ if }\delta_{i}=0,\\
\chi(L_{i},L_{i-1};H),&\text{ if }\delta_{i}=-1,\end{cases}

For
i=0
, let

(5.32)

x_{0}\in\begin{cases}\chi(L_{0},L_{k};H),&\text{ if }\delta_{0}=-1,\\
\chi(L_{k},L_{0};H),&\text{ if }\delta_{0}=0.\end{cases}

Here the functions
\delta_{i}
are defined in (
5.16
).

A pseudoholomorphic map from a popsicle is a map
u:S\to X
satisfying
homogeneous Cauchy-Riemann equation

(5.33)

\begin{cases}&(du-X_{H_{S}}\otimes\alpha_{S})^{0,1}=\frac{1}{2}[(du-X_{H_{S}}\otimes\alpha_{S})+J_{S}\circ(du-X_{H_{S}}\otimes\alpha_{S})\circ j]=0,\\
&u(z)\in(\psi^{\rho_{S}(z)})^{*}L_{i},z\in\partial S,i=0,\ldots,k\text{ exponentially},\\
&\lim\limits_{s\to\mathfrak{s}_{i}\infty}u\circ\epsilon_{i}(s,\cdot)=(\psi^{\nu_{i}})^{*}x_{i}\text{ exponentially}.\end{cases}

Put
\mathbf{x}=(x_{0},x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})
.
Let

(5.34)

\mathcal{R}^{k+1,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{x})

be the moduli space of pseudoholomorphic maps from popsicles, i.e., maps
u:S\to X
satisfying (
5.33
).

Lemma 5.7

(
[
28
, Lemma 3.5]
)
.

The virtual dimension of the moduli space
\mathcal{R}^{k+1,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{x})
(
5.34
) is

(5.35)

v-\dim\mathcal{R}^{k+1,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{x})=k-2+|F|+n(1+\sum_{i=0}^{k}\delta_{i})-\sum_{i=0}^{k}(-1)^{\delta_{i}}|x_{i}|.

Suppose we have made a universal and conformally consistent choice of Floer data
\mathbf{D}_{\mathcal{R}}
for all disks with popsicle structures.
The moduli space
\mathcal{R}^{k+1,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{x})
(
5.34
) has a natural Gromov compactification

(5.36)

\bar{\mathcal{R}}^{k+1,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{x})

by adding stable maps from broken popsicles modeled on ribbon trees carrying non-positive weights,
as well as inhomogeneous pseudoholomorphic strips which are elements of
\mathcal{R}^{2,\varnothing,(0,0)}(x_{0},x_{1})
and
\mathcal{R}^{2,\varnothing,(-1,-1)}(x_{0},x_{1})
.
In particular, the codimension-one boundary strata are covered by

(5.37)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{j+1,\mathbf{p}_{1},\mathbf{w}_{1}}(\tilde{x},x_{i+1},\ldots,x_{i+j})

\displaystyle\times\mathcal{R}^{k-j+2,\mathbf{p}_{2},\mathbf{w}_{2}}(x_{0},x_{1},\ldots,x_{i},\tilde{x},x_{i+j+1},\ldots,x_{k}),

(5.38)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{2,\varnothing,(w_{i},w_{i})}(\tilde{x}_{i},x_{i})

\displaystyle\times\mathcal{R}^{k+1,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{w}}(x_{0},\ldots,x_{i-1},\tilde{x}_{i},x_{i+1},\ldots,x_{k}),

(5.39)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{k+1,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{w}}(\tilde{x}_{0},x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})

\displaystyle\times\mathcal{R}^{2,\varnothing,(w_{0},w_{0})}(x_{0},\tilde{x}_{0}),

for some ‘intermediate’ chords
\tilde{x},\tilde{x}_{i},\tilde{x}_{0}
.
Boundary strata of higher codimensions can be described inductively.

The following statement summarizes the above description of the structure of the compactified moduli spaces,
where the proof for transversality is by now standard using a Sard-Smale argument, following e.g.
[
46
]
.

Proposition 5.8

(
[
28
, Proposition 3.6]
)
.

There exists a universal and conformally consistent choice of Floer data
\mathbf{D}_{\mathcal{R}}
such that the following holds.

(i)

If
k-2+|F|+n(1+\sum_{i=0}^{k}\delta_{i})-\sum_{i=0}^{k}(-1)^{\delta_{i}}|x_{i}|=0
,
the moduli space
\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathcal{OC}}^{k,\mathbf{p}_{oc,k},\mathbf{w}_{oc,k}}(y;\mathbf{x})
(
8.21
) is a compact smooth manifold of dimension zero.

(ii)

If
k-2+|F|+n(1+\sum_{i=0}^{k}\delta_{i})-\sum_{i=0}^{k}(-1)^{\delta_{i}}|x_{i}|=1
,
the moduli space
\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathcal{OC}}^{k,\mathbf{p}_{oc,k},\mathbf{w}_{oc,k}}(y;\mathbf{x})
(
8.21
) is a compact smooth manifold-with-boundary of dimension one,
with boundary strata given by products of zero-dimensional moduli spaces.

(iii)

If
k-2+|F|+n(1+\sum_{i=0}^{k}\delta_{i})-\sum_{i=0}^{k}(-1)^{\delta_{i}}|x_{i}|=2
,
the moduli space
\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathcal{OC}}^{k,\mathbf{p}_{oc,k},\mathbf{w}_{oc,k}}(y;\mathbf{x})
(
8.21
) is a compact smooth manifold-with-boundary of dimension two,
with codimension-one boundary strata given by products of zero-dimensional and one-dimensional moduli spaces,
and codimension-two boundary strata given by products of zero-dimensional moduli spaces.

5.4.
Floer complexes

Choose an admissible Hamiltonian
H\in\mathcal{H}(X)
.
Let
L_{0},L_{1}
be a pair of exact cylindrical Lagrangian submanifolds of
X
.
Consider the set

(5.40)

\chi(L_{0},L_{1};H)

of all time-one
H
-chords from
L_{0}
to
L_{1}
,
which are maps
x:[0,1]\to X
satisfying Hamilton’s equation:

(5.41)

\begin{cases}&\frac{dx}{dt}+X_{H}(x(t))=0,\\
&x(0)\in L_{0},x(1)\in L_{1}.\end{cases}

Each time-one
H
-chord
x
a well-defined Maslov index,
which we will denote by
|x|\in{\mathbb{Z}}/2
.
When
c_{1}(X)=0
and the Lagrangians come with gradings,
this admits an integral lift
|x|\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.
To each chord
x\in\mathcal{X}(L_{0},L_{1};H)
, denote by
o_{x}
its orientation line,
and
|o_{x}|_{{\mathbf{k}}}
the associated
{\mathbf{k}}
-normalized orientation space.

Define

(5.42)

CW^{*}(L_{0},L_{1};H)=\bigoplus_{x\in\chi(L_{0},L_{1};H)}|o_{x}|_{{\mathbf{k}}}[-\deg(x)],

where the degree shift means that we are putting
|o_{x}|_{{\mathbf{k}}}[-\deg(x)]
in degree zero,
or equivalently
|o_{x}|_{{\mathbf{k}}}
in degree
\deg(x)
.
Elements of this graded
{\mathbf{k}}
-modules are called the
wrapped Floer cochains
.
The differential is defined by counting rigid elements in the moduli spaces of Floer trajectories
\mathcal{R}^{2}(x_{0},x_{1})
,

(5.43)

\displaystyle d:CW^{*}(L_{0},L_{1};H)

\displaystyle\to CW^{*+1}(L_{0},L_{1};H),

(5.44)

\displaystyle d([x_{1}])=

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{0}\\
|x_{0}|=|x_{1}|+1\end{subarray}}\sum_{u\in\mathcal{R}^{2,\varnothing,(0,0)}(x_{0},x_{1})}o_{u}([x_{1}]),

where
o_{u}:o_{x_{1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}o_{x_{0}}
is the induced isomorphism of orientation lines by each rigid element in the moduli space (see
[
46
,
3
]
),
and we denote the induced isomorphism on
{\mathbf{k}}
-normalized orientation space by the same notation.

Now consider the negative Hamiltonian
-H
.
It is not admissible in the usual sense, but we can still define a Floer complex for it as follows.
The negative reparametrization
\bar{x}(t):=x(1-t)
provides a canonical one-to-one correspondence between time-one
(-H)
-chords
x\in\chi(L_{0},L_{1};-H)
of Maslov index
i
to time-one
H
-chords
\bar{x}\in\chi(L_{1},L_{1};H)
of Maslov index
n-i
.
Let
o^{-}_{\bar{x}}
be the negative orientation line for the chord
\bar{x}
,
such that there is a canonical isomorphism
o_{x}\cong o^{-}_{\bar{x}}
.
Define

(5.45)

CW^{*}(L_{0},L_{1};-H)=\prod_{x\in\chi(L_{0},L_{1};-H)}|o_{x}|_{{\mathbf{k}}}[-\deg(x)]=\prod_{\bar{x}\in\chi(L_{1},L_{0};H)}|o^{-}_{\bar{x}}|_{{\mathbf{k}}}[n-\deg(\bar{x})].

Elements of this graded
{\mathbf{k}}
-modules are called the
wrapped Floer chains
.

Lemma 5.9
.

There is a canonical isomorphism of chain complexes, called the Poincaré duality isomorphism,

(5.46)

I:CW^{*}(L_{0},L_{1};-H)\to CW^{*}(L_{1},L_{1};H)^{\vee}[-n]=\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(CW^{n-*}(L_{1},L_{0};H),{\mathbf{k}}).

Proof.

On generators, it is exactly given by the identification
x\mapsto\bar{x}
.
By choosing the family of almost complex structures for
-H
to agree with the ones chosen for
H
when choosing Floer data,
we also get a natural identification of moduli spaces of Floer trajectories for
-H
and those for
H
.
Thus
I
intertwines
d
and
\partial
as well, i.e., is a chain map.
∎

An important feature in Floer theory is the existence of
a continuation map

(5.47)

c:CW^{*}(L_{0},L_{1};-H)\to CW^{*}(L_{0},L_{1};H)

associated to a monotone homotopy of Hamiltonians.
This is defined by counting rigid elements in the moduli space
\mathcal{R}^{2,\{1\},(0,-1)}(x_{0},x_{1})
, i.e.,

(5.48)

c([x_{1}^{-}])=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{0}\\
|x_{0}|=n-|x_{1}|\end{subarray}}\sum_{u\in\mathcal{R}^{2,\{1\},(0,-1)(x_{0},x_{1})}}\mathcal{R}^{2,\{1\},(0,-1)}_{u}([x_{1}^{-}]),

where we take the input
[x_{1}^{-}]\in CW^{*}(L_{0},L_{1};-H)
to be the generator associated with the chord
x_{1}^{-}\in\chi(L_{0},L_{1};-H)
which uniquely corresponds to the chord
x_{1}\in\chi(L_{1},L_{0};H)
by negative reparametrization.
Note that the wrapped Floer chain complex
CW^{*}(L_{0},L_{1};-H)
is a direct product,
we must therefore show that the map is well-defined.
This can be done by the following lemma.

Lemma 5.10
.

Let
x_{0}\in\chi(L_{1},L_{0};H)
and
x_{1}\in\chi(L_{0},L_{1};H)
satisfy
|x_{0}|+|x_{1}|=n
.
There are only finitely many pairs
(x_{0},x_{1})
for which the moduli space
\mathcal{R}^{2,\{1\},(0,-1)}(x_{0},x_{1})
is non-empty.

Proof.

If there exists some
u\in\mathcal{R}^{2,\{1\},(0,-1)}(x_{0},x_{1})
,
the domain of
u
has only two negative punctures (outputs) and no positive punctures (input),
this implies that the sum of the actions of
x_{0}
and
x_{1}
is positive.
However, all but finitely many
H
-chords have negative action (
[
3
, Lemma B.2]
).
∎

Corollary 5.11
.

The continuation map (
5.47
) is well-defined.

Proof.

Lemma
5.10
implies that
c([x_{1}^{-}])=0
for all but finitely many
x_{1}^{-}\in\chi(L_{0},L_{1};-H)
,
and that for each such
x_{1}^{-}
, the output
c([x_{1}^{-}])
is a finite sum as well.
∎

The Rabinowitz Floer complex is defined to be the mapping cone

(5.49)

RC^{*}(L_{0},L_{1})=\mathrm{cone}(c:CW^{*}(L_{0},L_{1};-H)\to CW^{*}(L_{0},L_{1};H)).

As a by product of the definitions, we immediately see that the Rabinowitz Floer complex defined in (
5.49
) is a locally linear compact vector space in the sense of Lefschetz
[
35
]
.

Proposition 5.12
.

Suppose
{\mathbf{k}}
is a field.
The Rabinowitz Floer complex
RC^{*}(L_{0},L_{1})
is a locally linearly compact vector space over
{\mathbf{k}}
.

Proof.

Since the wrapped Floer cochain space (
5.42
) is discrete,
it follows from Lefschetz-Tate duality (
[
35
, II. (28.2-29.1)]
,
[
42
, Theorem 1.25]
) that the wrapped Floer chain space (
5.45
) is linear compact because of the duality (
5.46
).
By definition, a locally linearly compact vector space is a direct sum of a discrete vector space with a locally linear compact vector space.
∎

In fact, this is a type of locally linearly compact vector space, or equivalently a Tate vector space in the sense of
[
9
]
, which is self dual (
[
19
, Proposition 3.15]
),
which in our context will imply

Corollary 5.13
.

The topological linear dual of the Poincaré duality isomorphism
I
(
5.46
) is still an isomorphism,
and combined with
I
itself gives an isomorphism between
RC^{*}(L_{0},L_{1})
and its topological linear dual,
which is itself.

While we expect an interesting category theory enriched in Tate vector spaces,
we do not plan to address this further in this paper as our main results are independent of it.

5.5.
A_{\infty}
-structures

Define maps

(5.50)

\mu^{k}_{\mathcal{RW}}:RC^{*}(L_{k-1},L_{k})\otimes\cdots RC^{*}(L_{0},L_{1})\to RC^{*}(L_{0},L_{k})

by countign rigid elements in the moduli space of pseudoholomorphic maps from popsicles.
This is defined first on components as maps of the form

(5.51)

\mu^{k,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{w}}:CW^{*}(L_{k-1},L_{k};\mathfrak{s}_{k}H)\otimes\cdots\otimes CW^{*}(L_{0},L_{1};\mathfrak{s}_{1}H)\to CW^{*}(L_{0},L_{k};\mathfrak{s}_{0}H),

where the symbols
\mathfrak{s}_{i}
are defined in (
5.17
).
On a basis of elements the map (
5.51
) takes the form:

(5.52)

\mu^{k}_{\mathcal{RW}}([x_{k}^{\mathfrak{s}_{k}}],\ldots,[x_{1}^{\mathfrak{s}_{1}}])=\prod_{x_{0}}\sum_{u\in\mathcal{R}^{k+1,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{w}}(\vec{x})}(-1)^{*_{k,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{w}}+\diamond_{k,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{w}}}o_{u}([x_{k}^{\mathfrak{s}_{k}}]\otimes\cdots\otimes[x_{1}^{\mathfrak{s}_{1}}]),

where the signs are

(5.53)

\displaystyle*_{k,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{w}}=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{k}(i+w_{1}+\ldots+w_{i-1})|x_{i}^{\mathfrak{s}_{i}}|+\sum_{i=1}^{k}(k-i)w_{i},

(5.54)

\displaystyle\diamond_{k,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{w}}=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{k}|\mathbf{p}^{-1}(\{i+1,\ldots,k\})|(w_{i}+|\mathbf{p}^{-1}(i)|).

Technically, each rigid element
u
would induce an isomorphism of orientation lines associated with the rescaled chords
(\psi^{\nu_{i}})^{*}x_{i}
, but there are canonical isomorphisms
o_{(\psi^{\nu_{i}})^{*}x_{i}}\cong o_{x_{i}}
induced by the Liouville rescaling (
[
3
]
); similarly for the negative orientation lines.
The maps (
5.52
) extend to well-defined maps (
5.50
) by
[
28
, Lemma 4.8]
,
which satisfy the
A_{\infty}
-equations by
[
28
, Lemma 4.9]
.

Definition 5.14
.

The Rabinowitz Fukaya category
\mathcal{RW}=\mathcal{RW}({\mathbb{L}})
is defined to be the
A_{\infty}
-category having objects being exact cylindrical Lagrangian usbmanifolds in the collection
{\mathbb{L}}
(
5.3
),
morphism spaces
\hom_{\mathcal{RW}}(L_{0},L_{1})=RC^{*}(L_{0},L_{1})
,
and
A_{\infty}
-structure maps
\mu^{k}_{\mathcal{RW}}
(
5.50
).

When no confusion may occur, we shall refer to this as the Rabinowitz Fukaya category of
X
,
and denote it by
\mathcal{RW}=\mathcal{RW}(X)
.

By construction, the Rabinowitz Fukaya categories
\mathcal{RW}
comes with a natural strict
A_{\infty}
-functor

(5.55)

j:\mathcal{W}\to\mathcal{RW}

which is the identity on objects,
has
j^{1}:CW(K,L;H)\to RC^{*}(K,L)
the obvious inclusion map,
and
j^{k}=0
for all
k\geq 2
.
This functor sends compact exact Lagrangians to zero,
and more generally all
right proper objects
(meaning those whose right Yoneda modules are proper modules) to zero.
In this sense, the non-triviality of
\mathcal{RW}
measures the failure of
\mathcal{W}
being proper.

5.6.
Relations to the wrapped Fukaya category

In addition to the functor
j
(
5.55
), we have the following result establishing a deeper connection between the Rabinowitz Fukaya category and the wrapped Fukaya category:

Theorem 5.15

(
[
28
, Theorem 1.1]
)
.

There is a canonical
A_{\infty}
-functor

(5.56)

\Phi:\mathcal{RW}\to\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\infty}

which is a quasi-equivalence whenever
X
is a non-degenerate Liouville manifold with
c_{1}(X)=0
.

Below we shall list some of the key properties that are both relevant to the proof of Theorem
5.15
and also useful in other places of this paper,
while refer the reader to
[
28
]
for full details of the construction of the functor (
5.56
) and the proof that it is a quasi-equivalence.

Recall from Corollary
3.7
that
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\infty}
naturally has the structure of a bimodule over
\mathcal{W}^{op}
,
together with a quasi-isomorphism

(5.57)

\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\infty}\cong\mathrm{cone}(\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{W}^{op},(\mathcal{W}^{op})^{\vee}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}\mathcal{W}_{\Delta}^{op})\to\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{W}^{op},\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{W}_{\Delta},\mathcal{W}_{\Delta}))).

Similarly, we can regard
\mathcal{RW}
as a
\mathcal{W}^{op}
-bimodule via the natural functor
j:\mathcal{W}\to\mathcal{RW}
(
5.55
),
by restricting all except for one (the main input) of the inputs to
\mathcal{W}
via the natural functor
j
(the resulting one would be a bimodule over
\mathcal{W}
,
but that is equivalent to a bimodule over
\mathcal{W}
by interchanging the left and right actions.

Remark 5.16
.

Here we are saying that
\mathcal{RW}
, whose underlying cochain complex is

(5.58)

\mathcal{RW}(K,L)=RC^{*}(K,L)

is a bimodule over
\mathcal{W}^{op}
.
The reader should notice that this is different from the diagonal bimodule
\mathcal{RW}_{\Delta}
of
\mathcal{RW}
itself,
which has underlying cochain complex

(5.59)

\mathcal{RW}_{\Delta}(K,L)=\mathcal{RW}(L,K)=RC^{*}(L,K).

This is one of the main results that we are treating most of the relevant bimodules as over
\mathcal{W}^{op}
instead of
\mathcal{W}
,
although it indeed brings up some inconvenience at times.

There is another distinguished
\mathcal{W}^{op}
-bimodule

(5.60)

\mathcal{W}_{-},

whose underlying cochain space is

(5.61)

\mathcal{W}_{-}(K,L)=CW^{*}(K,L;-H).

The bimodule structure maps are defined by counting rigid popsicle maps used to define structure maps for
\mathcal{RW}
subject to certain constructions on the flavors
\mathbf{p}
and weights
\mathbf{w}
,
giving an
A_{\infty}
-enhancement of the action of wrapped Floer cohomology on wrapped Floer homology
(see
[
28
, §5.6]
for details).
This comes with a map of bimodules

(5.62)

\mathfrak{c}:\mathcal{W}_{-}\to\mathcal{W}^{op}_{\Delta}

enhancing the continuation map.

Proposition 5.17

(
[
28
, Lemma 5.9]
)
.

There is a canonical quasi-isomorphism of
\mathcal{W}^{op}
-bimodules

(5.63)

\iota:\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{c}:\mathcal{W}_{-}\to\mathcal{W}^{op}_{\Delta})\to\mathcal{RW}.

∎

In addition to the bimodule continuation map, we also have a bimodule enhancement of the Poncaré duality isomorphism
I
(
5.46
).

Proposition 5.18

(
[
28
, Proposition 5.11]
)
.

There is a canonical quasi-isomorphism of
\mathcal{W}^{op}
-bimodules

(5.64)

\mathcal{I}:\mathcal{W}_{-}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}(\mathcal{W}^{op})^{\vee}[-n],

whose
(0,0)
-th term on chain complexes satisfies
\mathcal{I}^{0,0}=I
where
I
is the Poncaré duality isomorphism (
5.46
).

∎

The construction of the bimodule map
\mathcal{I}
(
5.64
) uses disks with two primary inputs pairing between
\mathcal{W}_{-}
and
\mathcal{W}^{op}
,
as well as arbitrarily many auxiliary inputs from
\mathcal{W}^{op}
; see §3.6 of
[
28
]
.

The functor
\Phi
(
1.9
) induces a map of
\mathcal{W}^{op}
-bimodules

(5.65)

\Phi_{\mathcal{W}^{op}}:\mathcal{RW}\to\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\infty},

which is a quasi-isomorphism when
X
is non-degenerate with
c_{1}(X)=0
.
In view of the quasi-isomorphism (
5.57
),
this bimodule map can be written as two components,

(5.66)

\Phi^{+}_{\mathcal{W}^{op}}:\mathcal{RW}\to\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{W}^{op},\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{W}_{\Delta},\mathcal{W}_{\Delta})),

and

(5.67)

\Phi^{-}_{\mathcal{W}^{op}}:\mathcal{RW}\to\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{W}^{op},(\mathcal{W}^{op})^{\vee}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}\mathcal{W}_{\Delta}^{op})[1].

Here
\Phi^{+}_{\mathcal{W}^{op}}
(
5.66
) is still a bimodule map,
but
\Phi^{-}_{\mathcal{W}^{op}}
(
5.67
) is not, but rather some kind of homotopy of between bimodule maps.
We consider the following two compositions

(5.68)

\mathcal{W}^{op}_{\Delta}\to\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{c}:\mathcal{W}_{-}\to\mathcal{W}^{op}_{\Delta})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\iota}}{{\to}}\mathcal{RW}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Phi^{-}_{\mathcal{W}^{op}}}}{{\to}}\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{W}^{op},\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{W}_{\Delta},\mathcal{W}_{\Delta})),

(5.69)

\mathcal{W}_{-}[1]\to\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{c}:\mathcal{W}_{-}\to\mathcal{W}^{op}_{\Delta})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\iota}}{{\to}}\mathcal{RW}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Phi^{-}_{\mathcal{W}^{op}}}}{{\to}}\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{W}^{op},(\mathcal{W}^{op})^{\vee}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}\mathcal{W}_{\Delta}^{op})[1].

The first map
\mathcal{W}_{-}[1]\to\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{c}:\mathcal{W}_{-}\to\mathcal{W}^{op}_{\Delta})
is not a bimodule map either just as (
5.67
) is not,
but the overall composition (
5.69
), with the grading shifted back, defines a bimodule map

(5.70)

\mathcal{W}_{-}\to\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{W}^{op},(\mathcal{W}^{op})^{\vee}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}\mathcal{W}_{\Delta}^{op}).

Lemma 5.19

(
[
28
, Corollary 5.15, Proposition 5.19, Lemma 5.22]
)
.

The compositions (
5.68
) and (
5.70
) are both quasi-isomorphism of
\mathcal{W}^{op}
-bimodules.

∎

Lemma 5.20
.

The quasi-equivalence
\Phi:\mathcal{RW}\to\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\infty}
induces a quasi-isomorphism of cones of bimodules

(5.71)

\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{c}:\mathcal{W}_{-}\to\mathcal{W}^{op}_{\Delta})\to\mathrm{cone}((\mathcal{W}^{op})^{!}\otimes_{\mathcal{W}^{op}}(\mathcal{W}^{op})^{\vee}\to\mathcal{W}^{op}_{\Delta}).

Proof.

Note that there is a canonical quasi-isomorphism

\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{W}^{op},(\mathcal{W}^{op})^{\vee}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}\mathcal{W}_{\Delta}^{op})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}(\mathcal{W}^{op})^{!}\otimes_{\mathcal{W}^{op}}(\mathcal{W}^{op})^{\vee}

given by (
2.76
).
Then apply Lemma
5.19
plus a filtration argument.
∎

5.7.
The geometric pairing

Finally, note that there is a tautological pairing on
\mathcal{RW}
by evaluation,

(5.72)

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{taut}:\mathcal{RW}^{*}(K,L)\otimes\mathcal{RW}^{n-1-*}(L,K)\to{\mathbf{k}},

which has degree
1-n
.
This is a consequence of the general algebraic construction in §
2.7
together with the Poincaré duality isomorphism
I
(
5.46
).
The explicit formula is given as follows.
Since by definition we have

(5.73)

\hom_{\mathcal{RW}}(K,L)=RC^{*}(K,L)=\mathrm{cone}(c:CW^{*}(K,L;-H)\to CW^{*}(K,L;H)),

it follows that we can define a pairing by

(5.74)

\langle(x_{-},x_{+}),(y_{-},y_{+})\rangle_{taut}=I(x_{-})(y_{+})+(-1)^{|x_{+}||y_{+}|+|x_{+}|+|y_{+}|}I(y_{-})(x_{+}).

This is a form of a tautological pairing defined in (
2.133
) in §
2.7
,
and Lemma
2.32
implies

Corollary 5.21
.

The tautological pairing (
5.72
) on
\mathcal{RW}
is nondegenerate.

∎

The above discussion is sufficient for showing that the cohomology category,
or the idempotent split-closed derived category of the Rabinowitz Fukaya category,
is a Calabi-Yau category as an ordinary category.
However, this pairing is too rigid for proving
A_{\infty}
-structures to be compatible with it strictly using direct geometric arguments,
which often fail due to lack of enough symmetries.
Relatedly, the continuation map
\mathcal{W}_{-}\to\mathcal{W}^{op}_{\Delta}
(
5.62
) has too many higher order terms for which compatibility with pairing cannot be guaranteed in the way we choose Floer data.
The chain-level Calabi-Yau structure on the Rabinowitz Fukaya category,
to be constructed in the next section,
will actually give rise to a slightly different yet closely related pairing.

6.
The Calabi-Yau structure on the Rabinowitz Fukaya category

The goal of this section is to construct a chain-level weak proper Calabi-Yau structure on the Rabinowitz Fukaya category and prove Theorem
1.2
.
With the algebraic techniques developed previously in §
3
,
it turns out that this weak proper Calabi-Yau structure is the obvious one that we may expect.

6.1.
The residue on the Rabinowitz Fukaya category

Suppose
X
is nondegenerate and has
c_{1}(X)=0
.
By
[
29
]
, the wrapped Fukaya category
\mathcal{W}=\mathcal{W}(X)
admits a weak smooth Calabi-Yau structure
\sigma\in\mathrm{CC}_{n}(\mathcal{W})
,
which is a canonical Hochschild cycle whose homology class is the preimage of the unit in symplectic cohomology under the open-closed map (
8.39
).
(In fact,
[
31
]
also shows that the open-closed map has an
S^{1}
-equivariant lift,
which leads to a strong smooth Calabi-Yau stricture
\tilde{\sigma}
lifting
\sigma
,
but we will not use that here.)

By the general algebra machinery developed in §
3.3
,
we get a chain map

(6.1)

\mathrm{res}:\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\infty})\to{\mathbf{k}}[1-n],

which induces a map on Hochschild homology

(6.2)

\mathrm{res}:\mathrm{HH}_{*}(\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\infty})\to{\mathbf{k}}[1-n].

Using this we also define a pairing on the Rabinowitz Fukaya category as follows.
Consider the quasi-equivalence
\Phi:\mathcal{RW}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\infty}
given by (
5.56
) from Theorem
5.15
.
The induced chain map

(6.3)

\Phi_{*}:\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{RW})\to\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\infty})

is a quasi-isomorphism,
where both Hochschild chain complexes are defined with their own diagonal coefficients.

Definition 6.1
.

The residue on the Rabinowitz Fukaya category is defined to be

(6.4)

\mathrm{res}_{\mathcal{RW}}=\mathrm{res}\circ\Phi_{*}:\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{RW})\to{\mathbf{k}}[1-n].

Proposition 6.2
.

The map
\mathrm{res}_{\mathcal{RW}}
(
6.4
) is a chain map.
The induced map only depends on the homology class
[\sigma]\in\mathrm{HH}_{-n}(\mathcal{W})
of the weak smooth Calabi-Yau structure
\sigma
on the wrapped Fukaya category
\mathcal{W}
.

Proof.

By Proposition
3.18
,
\mathrm{res}
(
6.1
) is a chain map,
so is
\mathrm{res}_{\mathcal{RW}}
(
6.4
) since
\Phi_{*}
is one.
Independence of chain representatives of
[\sigma]
follows from the fact that the induced map between Hochschild cohomology and Hochschild homology by the chain map
-\cap\sigma
(
3.45
),
which is a quasi-isomorphism by Lemma
2.28
,
depends only on the homology class
[\sigma]\in\mathrm{HH}_{-n}(\mathcal{C})
.
∎

6.2.
The induced pairing

Define a pairing on
\mathcal{RW}
by the following composition.

(6.5)

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathrm{res}_{\mathcal{RW}}}:\mathcal{RW}^{*}(K,L)\otimes\mathcal{RW}^{n-1-*}(L,K)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mu^{2}_{\mathcal{RW}}}}{{\to}}\mathcal{RW}^{n-1}(L,L)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i_{L}}}{{\to}}\mathrm{CC}_{n-1}(\mathcal{RW})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{res}_{\mathcal{RW}}}}{{\to}}{\mathbf{k}}.

Call this the residue pairing on
\mathcal{RW}
.

Lemma 6.3
.

The pairing
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathrm{res}_{\mathcal{RW}}}
(
6.5
) induces a nondegenerate pairing on cohomology groups

(6.6)

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathrm{res}_{\mathcal{RW}}}:H^{*}(\mathcal{RW})(K,L)\otimes H^{n-1-*}(\mathcal{RW})(L,K)\to{\mathbf{k}}.

Proof.

Consider the diagram

(6.7)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{RW}^{*}(K,L)\otimes\mathcal{RW}^{n-1-*}(L,K)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{RW}^{n-1}(L,L)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\infty}^{*}(K,L)\otimes\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\infty}^{n-1-*}(L,K)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\infty}^{n-1}(L,L)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{CC}_{n-1}(\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\infty})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{res}}}{{\to}}{\mathbf{k}}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu^{2}_{\mathcal{RW}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{1}\otimes\Phi^{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu^{2}_{\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\infty}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{L}}

Since
\Phi:\mathcal{RW}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\infty}
(
5.56
) is an
A_{\infty}
-functor (which is a quasi-equivalence),
the left square in (
6.7
) commutes up to chain homotopy,
where the chain homotopy is precisely given by
\Phi^{2}
.
It follows that the induced pairing (
6.6
) agrees with the pairing on
H^{*}(\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\infty})
induced by the residue on
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\infty}
,
which is nondegenerate by Proposition
3.19
.
∎

Remark 6.4
.

It is a straightforward but somewhat tedious computation to show that the pairing (
6.5
) is compatible with the tautological pairing (
5.72
) in an
A_{\infty}
-homotopic sense.
This would require one to go through details of the construction of the functor
\Phi
in
[
28
]
.
Since we do not need this result in this paper, we will not carry out this discussion.

Proof of Theorem
1.2
.

We already have a chain map
\mathrm{res}_{\mathcal{RW}}:\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{RW})\to{\mathbf{k}}[1-n]
(
6.4
).
By Lemma
6.3
, its induced pairing
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathrm{res}_{\mathcal{RW}}}
is nondegenerate on the cohomology category
H(\mathcal{RW})
.
∎

7.
Closed-string counterparts

In this section, we recall the definition of symplectic cohomology and provide a definition of the closed-string Rabinowitz Floer cohomology in a manner that is compatible with the geometric/analytic framework we used for constructing the Rabinowitz Fukaya category in §
5
.

7.1.
Popsicle structures for closed strings

Consider genus zero Riemann surfaces without boundary with two punctures.
Each of such surfaces is biholomorphic to
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
minus two points,
and topologically they can all be identified with a cylinder.
However, we want to think of them as carrying additional data similar to popsicle structures when we consider pseudoholomorphic maps from them.

Let
\mathbf{p}_{c}:F\to\{1\}
be a map from a finite set
F
to the singleton set
\{1\}
.
Technically all finite sets are allowed;
however for the purpose of extracting Floer-theoretic operations we will always consider injective maps,
so that
F
is either the empty set
\varnothing
or a singleton set.
Let
w_{0},w_{1}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\leq 0}
be non-positive integers, called weights, and put
\mathbf{w}_{c}=(w_{0},w_{1})
.
These should satisfy the following condition

(7.1)

w_{0}=w_{1}+|F|.

We shall only be interested in the case where
w_{0},w_{1}\in\{-1,0\}
,
which together with (
7.1
) implies that the are only three possibilities

(i)

w_{0}=w_{1}=0
, and
F=\varnothing
;

(ii)

w_{0}=w_{1}=-1
, and
F=\varnothing
;

(iii)

w_{0}=0,w_{1}=-1
, and
F
is a singleton set so that
\mathbf{p}_{c}
is a bijection.

Let
S
be a genus zero Riemann surface without boundary with two punctures
\zeta_{0},\zeta_{1}
,
where for
i=0,1
we assign the weight
w_{i}
to
\zeta_{i}
.
These determine the positive/negative choices cylindrical ends at
\zeta_{0},\zeta_{1}
as follows:

(7.2)

\kappa_{i}^{\mathfrak{s}_{i}}:C^{\mathfrak{s}_{i}}\to S,i=0,1,

where
C^{+}=[0,+\infty)\times{\mathbb{R}}/2\pi{\mathbb{Z}}
is the positive cylinder,
C^{-}=(-\infty,+]\times{\mathbb{R}}/2\pi{\mathbb{Z}}
is the negative cylinder,
and the symbols
\mathfrak{s}_{i}\in\{+,-\}
are defined as in (
5.17
).

A choice of a cylindrical end
\kappa
at a puncture
\zeta
determines a tangent ray at
\zeta
,
by taking the limit as
s\to\pm\infty
of the tangent rays containing the tangent vectors
\kappa_{*}\partial_{s}
along the line
\kappa(s,0)
.
In other words, one gets a framing at the puncture
\zeta
.
Since we will not be dealing with additional algebraic structures governed by the moduli spaces of framed genus zero Riemann surfaces,
there are no issues in, and we will be, fixing the choices of cylindrical ends (and therefore framings) such that the geodesics on
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}=\bar{S}
starting from
\zeta_{i}
in the direction of the tangent rays determined by the cylindrical ends are contained in the same geometric image.
For
w_{0}=w_{1}
and so
F=\varnothing
, this can simply be replaced with the requirement that the two cylindrical ends agree with the global trivialization of the cylinder
S\cong(-\infty,+\infty)\times{\mathbb{R}}/2\pi{\mathbb{Z}}
.
With such choices of cylindrical ends,
the tangent rays at both punctures
\zeta_{0},\zeta_{1}

In the last case where
w_{0}=0,w_{1}=-1
,

Definition 7.1
.

A popsicle structure on
S
of flavor
\mathbf{p}
is a choice of a preferred point along the geodesic determined by the choices of cylindrical ends as above.

Although the above definition is meaningful and useful,
there are not enough stable popsicles to form a ‘moduli space’.
The only stable case is when
|F|=1
, in which the moduli space
\mathcal{M}^{2,\{1\},(0,-1)}
is a singleton set.
Despite the insufficient supply of elements in the moduli spaces of domains,
we find that using popsicles make it easy for us to keep track of the choices of cylindrical ends and to study limits of pseudoholomorphic maps from such domains with different choices of cylindrical ends.

Remark 7.2
.

For genus zero Riemann surfaces with multiple inputs and outputs,
the notion of a popsicle structure is problematic,
so we will not use this model to discuss higher structures in Rabinowitz Floer theory.

7.2.
Pseudoholomorphic maps from twice-punctured spheres

Let
S
be a genus zero Riemann surface without boundary with two punctures,
with a popsicle structure
\eta
of flavor
\mathbf{p}_{c}
and weights
\mathbf{w}_{c}
.

Definition 7.3
.

A Floer datum for
(S,\eta)
of flavor
\mathbf{p}_{c}
and weights
\mathbf{w}_{c}
consists of

(i)

Cylindrical ends
\kappa_{i}:C^{\mathfrak{s}_{i}}\to S,i=0,1
.

(ii)

A domain-dependent family of Hamiltonians
H_{S}:S\to\mathcal{H}(X)
,
which is compatible with cylindrical ends:
\kappa_{i}^{*}H_{S}=H
.

(iii)

A domain-dependent family of
\omega
-compatible almost complex structures
J_{S}:S\to\mathcal{J}_{1}(X)
(see (
5.7
)),
which is compatible with cylindrical ends:
\kappa_{i}^{*}J_{S}=J_{t}
.

In the unstable case where
w_{0}=w_{1}
(so that the popsicle structure is trivial),

H_{S}
and
J_{S}
must be domain independent.

In Floer theory, we would like to extend the choices of Floer data across the moduli spaces.
Since the only non-empty moduli space is
\mathcal{M}^{2,\{1\},(0,-1)}
is a point,
and there are no rescaling factors as required by Definition
7.3
as opposed to Definition
5.3
,
so a
universal and conformally consistent choice of Floer data

\mathbf{D}_{\mathcal{M}}
for all closed strings,
which could have been defined in a way similar to Definition
5.5
,
is indeed the same as a choice of a Floer datum on
(S,\eta)
identified with the fiber of the universal curve over
\mathcal{M}^{2,\{1\},(0,-1)}
,
along with choices of Floer data for trivial cylinders subject to the requirement by Definition
7.3
.

To define the closed-string invariants, we must in addition break
S^{1}
-symmetry for non-trivial Hamiltonian orbits,
which can be done by introducing a perturbation term as below.

Definition 7.4
.

A perturbation datum for
S
is a smooth non-negative function

(7.3)

F:S^{1}\times X\to{\mathbb{R}}

such that
F
and
\lambda(X_{F})
are uniformly absolutely bounded,
and all time-one
H_{F}
-orbits are nondegenerate,
where
H_{F}(t,p)=H(p)+F(t,p)
is called the total Hamiltonian.

Let
\mathcal{O}(H_{F})
and
\mathcal{O}(-H_{F})
be the sets of time-one periodic orbits of
H_{F}
and
-H_{F}
respectively.
As is the case with chords, there is a natural one-to-one correspondence between
\mathcal{O}(H_{F})
and
\mathcal{O}(-H_{F})
,
given by negative reparametrization.
Consider maps
u:S\to X
satisfying

(7.4)

\begin{cases}&(du-dt\otimes X_{H_{F}})^{0,1}=0,\\
&\lim\limits_{s\to\mathfrak{s}_{i}\infty}u\circ\kappa_{i}(s,\cdot)=y_{i}(\cdot)\text{ exponentially for some }y_{i}\in\mathcal{O}(H).\end{cases}

When
w_{0}=0,w_{1}=-1
, both the cylindrical ends
\kappa_{0},\kappa_{1}
are negative cylindrical ends.
Since the cylindrical end
\kappa_{1}
is negative as opposed to its usual choice being positive,
we want to think of the orbit
y_{1}\in\mathcal{O}(H_{F})
as being the negatively parametrized orbit for a unique orbit
y_{1}^{-}\in\mathcal{O}(-H_{F})
.

Denote by

(7.5)

\mathcal{M}^{2,\mathbf{p}_{c},\mathbf{w}_{c}}(y_{0},y_{1})

the moduli space of maps satisfying (
7.4
).
It has a natural Gromov compactification by adding broken cylinders,
where the popsicle structure can go to any smooth component:

(7.6)

\bar{\mathcal{M}}^{2,\mathbf{p}_{c},\mathbf{w}_{c}}(y_{0},y_{1})=\coprod_{\begin{subarray}{c}m\\
y^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,y^{\prime}_{m}\end{subarray}}\mathcal{M}^{2}(y_{0},y^{\prime}_{1})\times\cdots\times\mathcal{M}^{2,\mathbf{p}_{c},\mathbf{w}_{c}}(y^{\prime}_{i-1},y^{\prime}_{i})\times\cdots\times\mathcal{M}^{2}(y^{\prime}_{m},y_{1}).

For generic choices of Floer data, this moduli space is a compact smooth manifold with boundary and corners.

7.3.
Symplectic cohomology and Rabinowitz Floer cohomology

The
symplectic cochain complex
is defined to be

(7.7)

SC^{*}(X;H_{F})=\bigoplus_{y\in\mathcal{O}(H)}|o_{y}|_{{\mathbf{k}}},

where the grading is given by

(7.8)

|y|=n-CZ(y),

where
CZ(y)
stands for the Conley-Zehnder index of
y
.
The differential
d:SC^{*}(X;H_{F})\to SC^{*}(X;H_{F})[1]
is defined by

(7.9)

d([y_{1}])=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y_{0}\\
|y_{0}|=|y_{1}|+1\end{subarray}}\sum_{u\in\mathcal{M}^{2,\varnothing,(0,0)}(y_{0},y_{1})}o_{u}([y_{1}]).

The
symplectic chain complex
is defined to be

(7.10)

SC^{*}(X;-H_{F})=\prod_{y\in\mathcal{O}(-H)}|o_{y}|_{{\mathbf{k}}}.

The differential
\partial:SC^{*}(X;-H_{F})\to SC^{*}(X;-H_{F})[1]
is defined by

(7.11)

\partial([y_{1}^{-}])=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}y_{0}\\
|y_{1}|=|y_{0}|+1\end{subarray}}\sum_{u\in\mathcal{M}^{2,\varnothing,(-1,-1)}(y_{0},y_{1})}o_{u}([y_{1}^{-}]).

Note that, despite its name,
SC^{*}(X;-H_{F})
is carrying a cohomological grading and the differential
\partial
increases grading.

Similar to (
5.46
), there is a Poincaré duality chain-level isomorphism

(7.12)

I:SC^{*}(X;-H_{F})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}SC^{*}(X;H_{F})^{\vee}[-2n]=\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(SC^{2n-*}(X;H_{F}),{\mathbf{k}}),

by negative reparametrization of orbits.

The above two closed-string invariants also come with a continuation map

(7.13)

c:SC^{*}(X;-H_{F})\to SC^{*}(X;H_{F}),

which is defined by counting rigid elements in the moduli space
\mathcal{M}^{2,\{1\},(0,-1)}(y_{0},y_{1})
, i.e.,

(7.14)

c([y_{1}^{-}])=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y_{0}\\
|y_{0}|=2n-|y_{1}|\end{subarray}}\sum_{u\in\mathcal{M}^{2,\{1\},(0,-1)(y_{0},y_{1})}}\mathcal{R}^{2,\{1\},(0,-1)}_{u}([y_{1}^{-}]).

We define the
closed-string Rabinowitz Floer cochain complex
to be the mapping cone

(7.15)

RFC^{*}(X)=\mathrm{cone}(c:SC^{*}(X;-H_{F})\to SC^{*}(X;H_{F})).

Similar to the tautological pairing (
5.72
),
we get from the tautological pairing (
2.133
) defined in §
2.7
a tautological pairing

(7.16)

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{taut}:RFC^{*}(X)\otimes RFC^{2n-1-*}(X)\to{\mathbf{k}},

using the isomorphism (
7.12
).
Just like the pairing on
\mathcal{RW}
(Corollary
5.21
), this pairing is nondegenerate as well.

8.
Open-closed relationships

In this section, we study relationships between the Rabinowitz Fukaya category and the closed-string Rabinowitz Floer cohomology.
This relies on a geometric construction of a map from the Hochschild homology of the wrapped Fukaya category with coefficients in the Rabinowitz Fukaya category.

8.1.
Open-closed strings

We first extend the definition of popsicles to domains of pseudoholomorphic maps that are commonly used to define the open-closed map.
Let
k\geq 1
be a positive integer.
Let
S
be a bordered Riemann surface isomorphic to a disk with
k
boundary punctures
z_{1},\ldots,z_{k}
ordered cyclically counterclockwise along the boundary, treated as inputs,
as well as one interior puncture
\zeta
equipped with a framing, treated as an output.
We want to think of
z_{k}
as a special input,
and
z_{1},\ldots,z_{k-1}
auxiliary inputs.

Such a surface will be a typical domain of a pseudoholomorphic map defining the open-closed map,
which we call an
open-closed string
.
Since we will not define an
S^{1}
-equivariant version of the open-closed map which would otherwise require one to consider all possible framings at the puncture
\zeta
,
we specify the choice of the framing at
\zeta
to be pointing to the direction of the special input
z_{k}
.

Define a popsicle structure on an open-closed string in a way similar to that discussed in §
7.1
.
Let
\mathbf{p}_{oc,k}:F\to\{d\}
be a map from a finite set
F
.
Let
w,w_{k}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\leq 0}
be a pair of non-positive integers satisfying the following conditions

(8.1)

w=w_{k}+|F|.

We shall be only interested in the case where
w,w_{k}\in\{-1,0\}
,
such that by (
8.1
) there are only three possibilities:

(i)

w=w_{k}=0
,
F=\varnothing
.

(ii)

w=w_{k}=-1
,
F=\varnothing
.

(iii)

w=0,w_{k}=-1
, and
F
is a singleton set so that
\mathbf{p}_{oc,k}
is a bijection.

Define the extended weights

(8.2)

\mathbf{w}_{oc,k}=(w,\underbrace{0,\ldots,0}_{k-1\text{ times }},w_{k}),

which means that we assign the weight
w
to
\zeta
,
w_{k}
to
z_{k}
, and
0
to
z_{i},i=1,\ldots,k
.

Definition 8.1
.

A popsicle structure of flavor
\mathbf{p}_{oc,k}
on an open-closed string
S
is a choice of a preferred point along the unqiue geodesic connecting
\zeta
and
z_{k}
determined by the chosen framing.

Let

(8.3)

\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{OC}}^{k,\mathbf{p}_{oc,k},\mathbf{w}_{oc,k}}

be the moduli space of stable open-closed strings equipped with popsicle structures of flavor
\mathbf{p}_{oc,k}
and weights
\mathbf{w}_{oc,k}
.
When
\mathbf{p}_{oc,k}=\varnothing
and
\mathbf{w}_{oc,k}=\mathbf{0}=(0,\ldots,0)
,
so we get the usual moduli spaces of open-closed strings, i.e., the ones without popsicle structures.
The other case where the popsicle structure is trivial is when
\mathbf{w}_{oc,k}=(-1,0,\ldots,0,-1)
.
In this case, we get a different moduli space,
but abstractly it can be identified with a copy of
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{OC}}^{k,\mathbf{p}_{oc,k},\mathbf{w}_{oc,k}}
if we forget the weights (note that we have not made choices of strip-like and cylindrical ends yet).

The moduli space
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{OC}}^{k,\mathbf{p}_{oc,k},\mathbf{w}_{oc,k}}
has a natural stable map compactification

(8.4)

\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathcal{OC}}^{k,\mathbf{p}_{oc,k},\mathbf{w}_{oc,k}}

by adding boundary strata which consist of
stable broken open-closed strings
with a popsicle structure,
appearing as limits of open-closed strings with popsicle structures.
Each stable broken open-closed string with a popsicle structure has a
main component
,
which is itself an open-closed string with a popsicle structure.
The other components are either closed strings with popsicle structures introduced in §
7.1
,
or the usual disks with popsicle structures introduced in §
5.2
.
In particular, the codimension one boundary strata,
which consist of stable broken open-closed strings with popsicles structures with two smooth surface components,
are covered the following types of moduli spaces

(8.5)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{OC}}^{k,\mathbf{p}^{\prime}_{oc,k},\mathbf{w}^{\prime}_{oc,k}}\times\mathcal{M}^{2,\mathbf{p}_{c},\mathbf{w}_{c}},

(8.6)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{j+1}{}_{0}\times_{i+1}\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{OC}}^{k-j,\mathbf{p}^{\prime}_{oc,k-j},\mathbf{w}^{\prime}_{oc,k-j}},1\leq i<k,i+j<k,

(8.7)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{j+1,\mathbf{p}^{\prime}_{k,i,j},\mathbf{w}^{\prime}_{k,i,j}}{}_{0}\times_{i+1}\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{OC}}^{k-j,\mathbf{p}^{\prime\prime}_{oc,k-j},\mathbf{w}^{\prime\prime}_{oc,k-j}},1\leq i<k,1\leq j<k,i+>k.

Some explanations about the boundary strata (
8.5
)-(
8.7
),
including the various notations for flavors and weights, are in order:

(i)

The boundary strata of type (
8.5
) consist of stable broken open-closed strings with a main component and the other component being a closed-string with popsicle structure introduced in §
7.1
,
such that the closed-string with popsicle structure itself is stable,
which implies that
\mathbf{p}_{c}=(0,-1)
and
\mathbf{p}_{c}:F_{c}\to\{1\}
is a bijection.
Consequently the flavor
\mathbf{p}^{\prime}_{oc,k}
is determined by
\mathbf{p}_{oc,k}
as follows.
Since
\mathbf{p}_{oc,k}
is at most a singleton set,
it follows that
\mathbf{p}^{\prime}_{oc,k}=\varnothing
.
The weights
\mathbf{w}^{\prime}_{oc,k}
and
\mathbf{w}_{c}
are automatically inherited from
\mathbf{w}_{oc,k}
,
such that both (
7.1
) and (
8.1
) are satisfied.

(ii)

The boundary strata of type (
8.6
) consist of stable broken open-closed strings with a main component and another disk component,
such that the special input
z_{0}
remains on the main component.
The notation
{}_{0}\times_{i}
in (
8.6
) means that the
0
-th boundary puncture of the disk component is identified with the
i
-th boundary puncture of the main component.
Since popsicle structures on open-closed strings are choices of points on the geodesic connecting
\zeta
and the special input
z_{k}
,
it follows that the other disk component must carry a trivial popsicle structure.
In addition, all the weights at the boundary punctures at the disk component must be zero,
which imply that that disk is an ordinary boundary-punctured disk.
The flavor and weight for the popsicle structure on the main component do not essentially change (except for having fewer boundary punctures).
That is,
\mathbf{p}^{\prime}_{oc,k-j}:F\to\{k-j\}
is the same map as
\mathbf{p}_{oc,k}
where we identify the singleton sets
\{k\}
with
\{k-j\}
,
and

(8.8)

\mathbf{w}^{\prime}_{oc,k-j}=(w,\underbrace{0,\ldots,0}_{k-j-1\text{times }},w_{k}),

where
w,w_{k}
remain unchanged when inherited from
\mathbf{w}_{oc,k}
.

(iii)

The boundary strata of type (
8.7
) are similar to (
8.6
),
except that the special input
z_{k}
goes to the disk component other than the main component.
Since the original popsicle structure has at most one sprinkle, i.e.,
|F|\leq 1
,
in this case exactly one component of a stable broken open-closed string can have a non-trivial popsicle structure.
The weights
\mathbf{w}^{\prime}_{k,i,j}
on the disk component are inherited from the weights from the original smooth open-closed string,
which takes the form

(8.9)

\mathbf{w}^{\prime}_{k,i,j}=(w^{\prime}_{0},0,\ldots,0,w_{k},0,\ldots,0),

where the weight
w_{k}
(the one coming from the original smooth open-closed string) is placed at the
(k-i)
-th entry in (
8.9
),
and
w^{\prime}_{0}
is the new weight at the
0
-th boundary puncture of the disk component,
which is glued to the main component at its
(k-j+1)
-th special input.
If the disk component carries the non-trivial popsicle structure,
then we must have
w^{\prime}_{0}=0
and
w_{k}=-1
;
otherwise the weights must satisfy
w^{\prime}_{0}=w_{k}=0
or
w^{\prime}_{0}=w_{k}=-1
.

8.2.
Pseudoholomorphic maps from open-closed strings

The definition of a pseudoholomorphic map from an open-closed string with a popsicle structure is similar to those from popsicles on disks as well as cylinders,
for which we need to introduce Floer data.
Fix
H\in\mathcal{H}(X),J\in\mathcal{J}_{1}(X)
as well as a perturbation datum
F\in C^{\infty}(X\times{\mathbb{R}}/2\pi{\mathbb{Z}},{\mathbb{R}})
introduced in Definition
7.4
.

Definition 8.2
.

A Floer datum on a open-closed string with a popsicle structure
(S,\eta)
of flavor
\mathbf{p}_{oc,k}
and weights
\mathbf{w}_{oc,k}
consists of the following data.

(i)

Choices of cylindrical and strip-like ends at the interior and boundary punctures

(8.10)

\displaystyle\kappa^{\mathfrak{s}}:

\displaystyle C^{\mathfrak{s}}\to S,

(8.11)

\displaystyle\epsilon^{+}_{i}:

\displaystyle Z^{+}\to S,i=1,\ldots,k,

(8.12)

\displaystyle\epsilon^{\mathfrak{s}_{0}}_{0}:

\displaystyle Z^{\mathfrak{s}_{0}}\to S,

such that the framing determined by the cylindrical end
\kappa^{\mathfrak{s}}
should be pointing to the puncture
z_{0}
.

(ii)

A collection of real numbers
\nu,\nu_{0},\ldots,\nu_{k}\geq 1
,
called rescaling factors,
which satisfy

(8.13)

(-1)^{\delta}\nu+(-1)^{\delta_{0}}\nu_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\nu_{i}\leq 0.

(iii)

A function
\rho_{S}:S\to[1,+\infty)
which is locally constant over cylindrical and strip-like ends,
equal to
\nu
over the cylindrical end
\kappa
,
\nu_{i}
over the the strip-like ends
\epsilon_{i},i=1,\ldots,k
,
and
\nu_{k}
over
\epsilon_{k}
.

(iv)

A sub-closed one-form
\alpha_{S}
with
d\alpha_{S}\leq 0
which is which is compatible with cylindrical end strip-like ends:

(8.14)

\displaystyle\kappa^{*}\alpha_{S}

\displaystyle=\nu dt,

(8.15)

\displaystyle\epsilon_{i}^{*}\alpha_{S}

\displaystyle=\nu_{i}dt,i=1,\ldots,k,

(8.16)

\displaystyle\epsilon_{0}^{*}\alpha_{S}

\displaystyle=\nu_{0}dt.

(v)

A domain-dependent family of Hamiltonians
H_{S}:S\to\mathcal{H}(X)
which is compatible with cylindrical and strip-like ends

(vi)

A domain-dependent family of perturbation data
F_{S}:S\to C^{\infty}(X\times{\mathbb{R}}/2\pi{\mathbb{Z}},{\mathbb{R}})
which is locally constant outside of the image of the cylindrical end
\kappa
,
and is compatible with the cylindrical end

(8.17)

\kappa^{*}F_{S}=\frac{F\circ\psi^{\nu}}{\nu^{2}}+C,

for some constant
C
that depends only on the cylindrical end
\kappa
.

(vii)

A domain-dependent family of almost complex structures
J_{S}:S\to\mathcal{J}(X)
which is compatible with cylindrical and strip-like ends

There is a notion of a universal and conformally consistent choice of Floer data, similar to Definition
5.5
.

Definition 8.3
.

Suppose we have made a universal and conformally consistent choice of Floer data
\mathbf{D}_{\mathcal{R}}
for all disks with popsicle structures,
as well as a universal and conformally consistent choice of Floer data
\mathbf{D}_{\mathcal{M}}
for all closed strings with popsicle structures.
A universal and conformally consistent choice of Floer data
\mathbf{D}_{\mathcal{OC}}
for open-closed strings with popsicle structures is a choice of Floer data,
one for each representative of element in
\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathcal{OC}}^{k,\mathbf{p}_{oc,k},\mathbf{w}_{oc,k}}
,
being
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbf{p}_{oc,k})
-invariant and smoothly varying over
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{OC}}^{k,\mathbf{p}_{oc,k},\mathbf{w}_{oc,k}}
,
which at boundary strata covered by (
8.5
)-(
8.7
) agree with a product Floer data chosen for lower dimensional moduli spaces up to conformal equivalence,
parts of which come from
\mathbf{D}_{\mathcal{R}}
and
\mathbf{D}_{\mathcal{M}}
.

Again, since all the choices involved in a Floer datum as in Definition
8.2
form a contractible space,
a universal and conformally consistent choice of Floer data
\mathbf{D}_{\mathcal{M}}
for all closed strings with popsicle structures exists.

Let
y\in\mathcal{O}(H_{F})
and
x_{i}\in\chi(L_{i-1},L_{i};H)
for
i=1,\ldots,k
and
x_{0}\in\chi(L_{k},L_{0};H)
.
The inhomogeneous Cauchy-Riemann equation for maps
u:S\to X
defined with respect to a choice of a Lagrangian label
L_{0},\ldots,L_{k}
,
a choice of a Floer datum as in Definition
8.2
,
as well as asymptotic conditions given by
y,x_{0},x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}
,
is the following

(8.18)

\begin{cases}&(du-X_{H_{S}}\otimes\alpha_{S})^{0,1}=\frac{1}{2}[(du-X_{H_{S}}\otimes\alpha_{S})+J_{S}\circ(du-X_{H_{S}}\otimes\alpha_{S})\circ j]=0,\\
&u(z)\in(\psi^{\rho_{S}(z)})^{*}L_{i},z\in\partial S,i=0,\ldots,k,\\
&\lim\limits_{s\to\mathfrak{s}\infty}u\circ\kappa(s,\cdot)=(\psi^{\nu})^{*}y\text{ exponentially},\\
&\lim\limits_{s\to+\infty}u\circ\epsilon_{i}(s,\cdot)=(\psi^{\nu_{i}})^{*}x_{i}\text{ exponentially},i=1,\ldots,k-1,\\
&\lim\limits_{s\to\mathfrak{s}_{k}\infty}u\circ\epsilon_{k}(s,\cdot)=(\psi^{\nu_{0}})^{*}x_{k}\text{ exponentially}.\end{cases}

Suppose we have made a universal and conformally consistent choice of Floer data
\mathbf{D}_{\mathcal{M}}
for all closed strings with popsicle structures.
Here we denote by
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})
.
Let

(8.19)

\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{OC}}^{k,\mathbf{p}_{oc,k},\mathbf{w}_{oc,k}}(y;\mathbf{x})

be the moduli space of pseudoholomorphic maps from open-closed strings with popsicle structures of flavor
\mathbf{p}_{oc,k}
and weights
\mathbf{w}_{oc,k}
,
i.e., the space of equivalence classes of maps
u:S\to X
satisfying the equation (
8.18
).

Lemma 8.4
.

The virtual dimension of the moduli space
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{OC}}^{k,\mathbf{p}_{oc,k},\mathbf{w}_{oc,k}}(y;\mathbf{x})
(
8.19
) is

(8.20)

v-\dim\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{OC}}^{k,\mathbf{p}_{oc,k},\mathbf{w}_{oc,k}}(y;\mathbf{x})=k+|F|+n(1+2\delta+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\delta_{i})-(-1)^{\delta}|y|-\sum_{i=1}^{k}(-1)^{\delta_{i}}|x_{i}|,

where the symbols
\delta,\delta_{i}
are defined in (
5.16
).

Proof.

This is a standard index calculation, e.g. following the argument of Proposition 11.13 of
[
46
]
.
The only difference is there is an interior puncture
\zeta
with asymptotic condition
y
,
but we can follow the same idea of the argument by gluing a Cauchy-Riemann operator on a sphere with one puncture with the asymptotic condition given by the negatively parametrized orbit
\bar{y}
.
Then (
8.20
) follows.
∎

The moduli space
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{OC}}^{k,\mathbf{p}_{oc,k},\mathbf{w}_{oc,k}}(y;\mathbf{x})
(
8.19
) has a natural Gromov compactification

(8.21)

\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathcal{OC}}^{k,\mathbf{p}_{oc,k},\mathbf{w}_{oc,k}}(y;\mathbf{x})

by adding stable maps from broken open-closed strings with popsicle structures.
The underlying domain of a representative of an element in
\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathcal{OC}}^{k,\mathbf{p}_{oc,k},\mathbf{w}_{oc,k}}(y;\mathbf{x})
(
8.21
) is not necessarily stable;
and in addition to the stable maps coming from domain degenerations,
there are also stable maps coming from strip and cylinder breakings.
The codimension-one boundary strata of
\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathcal{OC}}^{k,\mathbf{p}_{oc,k},\mathbf{w}_{oc,k}}(y;\mathbf{x})
are therefore covered by the following types of product moduli spaces:

(8.22)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{OC}}^{k,\mathbf{p}^{\prime}_{oc,k},\mathbf{w}^{\prime}_{oc,k}}(y^{\prime};\mathbf{x})\times\mathcal{M}^{2,\mathbf{p}_{c},\mathbf{w}_{c}}(y^{\prime},y),

(8.23)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{j+1}(x^{\prime},x_{i+1},\ldots,x_{i+j}){}_{0}\times_{i+1}\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{OC}}^{k-j,\mathbf{p}^{\prime}_{oc,k-j},\mathbf{w}^{\prime}_{oc,k-j}}(y;x_{1},\ldots,x_{i},x^{\prime},x_{i+j+1},\ldots,x_{k})

(8.24)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{j+1,\mathbf{p}^{\prime}_{k,i,j},\mathbf{w}^{\prime}_{k,i,j}}(x^{\prime},x_{i+1},\dots,x_{k},x_{1},\ldots,x_{i+j-k}){}_{0}\times_{i+1}\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{OC}}^{k-j,\mathbf{p}^{\prime\prime}_{oc,k-j},\mathbf{w}^{\prime\prime}_{oc,k-j}}(y;x_{i+j-k+1},\ldots,x_{i},x^{\prime}),

(8.25)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{OC}}^{k,\mathbf{p}_{oc,k},\mathbf{w}_{oc,k}}(y^{\prime};\mathbf{x})\times\mathcal{M}^{2,\varnothing,(w,w)}(y^{\prime},y)

(8.26)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{2,\varnothing,(0,0)}(x^{\prime}_{i},x_{i}){}_{0}\times_{i+1}\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{OC}}^{k,\mathbf{p}_{oc,k},\mathbf{w}_{oc,k}}(y;x_{0},\ldots,x^{\prime}_{i},\ldots,x_{k})

(8.27)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{2,\varnothing,(w_{k},w_{k})}(x^{\prime}_{k},x_{k}){}_{0}\times_{i+1}\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{OC}}^{k,\mathbf{p}_{oc,k},\mathbf{w}_{oc,k}}(y;x_{1},\ldots,x^{\prime}_{k})

In comparison to (
8.5
)-(
8.7
),
we give some addition explanations of the notations here:

(i)

The boundary strata (
8.22
) occur because of the domain degeneration corresponding to the boundary strata (
8.5
) in the compactified moduli space of domains.
Here
y^{\prime}
is a new orbit as the asymptotic condition at the interior puncture for a map in
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{OC}}^{k,\mathbf{p}_{oc,k},\mathbf{w}_{oc,k}}(y^{\prime};\mathbf{x})
,
and the asymptotic condition at
\zeta_{2,1}
for a map in
\mathcal{M}^{2,\varnothing,(w,w)}(y^{\prime},y)
.

(ii)

The boundary strata (
8.23
) occur because of the domain degeneration corresponding to the boundary strata (
8.6
) in the compactified moduli space of domains.
Here
x^{\prime}
is a new chord as the asymptotic condition at the
0
-th boundary puncture (output) for a map in
\mathcal{R}^{j+1}(x^{\prime},x_{i+1},\ldots,x_{i+j})
,
and the asymptotic condition at the
(i+1)
-th boundary puncture for a map in
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{OC}}^{k-j,\mathbf{p}^{\prime}_{oc,k-j},\mathbf{w}^{\prime}_{oc,k-j}}
.

(iii)

The boundary strata (
8.24
) occur because of the domain degeneration corresponding to the boundary strata (
8.7
) in the compactified moduli space of domains.
The role of the new chord
x^{\prime}
is similar to the previous case.

(iv)

The boundary strata (
8.25
) occur because of cylinder breakings at the puncture
\zeta
,
where
y^{\prime}
is a new orbit as the output for a map in
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{OC}}^{k,\mathbf{p}_{oc,k},\mathbf{w}_{oc,k}}(y^{\prime};\mathbf{x})
,
and the asymptotic condition at
\zeta_{2,1}
for a map in
\mathcal{M}^{2,\varnothing,(w,w)}(y^{\prime},y)
.

(v)

The boundary strata (
8.26
) and (
8.27
) occur because of strip breakings at the puncture
z_{i}
and
z_{0}
,
where
x^{\prime}_{i}
is a new chord as the asymptotic condition at
z_{1,1}
for a map in
\mathcal{R}^{2,\varnothing,(0,0)}(x^{\prime}_{i},x_{i})
or
\mathcal{R}^{2,\varnothing,(w_{0},w_{0})}(x^{\prime}_{0},x_{0})
,
and the asymptotic condition at
z_{2,i}
for a map
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{OC}}^{k,\mathbf{p}_{oc,k},\mathbf{w}_{oc,k}}(y;x_{0},\ldots,x^{\prime}_{i},\ldots,x_{k})
.
Such strip breakings do not affect the popsicle structure on the main component.

The following result for transversality and compactness of the moduli space
\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathcal{OC}}^{k,\mathbf{p}_{oc,k},\mathbf{w}_{oc,k}}(y;\mathbf{x})
(
8.21
) is similar to Proposition
5.8
,
where we do not need the relevant result for moduli spaces of dimension two.
The proof for transversality is a standard Sard-Smale argument (essentially following
[
3
,
46
]
),
which is possible since the Floer data are chosen to break
S^{1}
-symmetry over cylindrical ends,
and compactness follows from maximum principle plus Gromov compactness.

Proposition 8.5
.

There exists a universal and conformally consistent choice of Floer data
\mathbf{D}_{\mathcal{OC}}
such that the following holds.

(i)

If
k+|F|+n(1+2\delta+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\delta_{i})-(-1)^{\delta}|y|-\sum_{i=1}^{k}(-1)^{\delta_{i}}|x_{i}|=0
,
the moduli space
\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathcal{OC}}^{k,\mathbf{p}_{oc,k},\mathbf{w}_{oc,k}}(y;\mathbf{x})
(
8.21
) is a compact smooth manifold of dimension zero.

(ii)

If
k+|F|+n(1+2\delta+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\delta_{i})-(-1)^{\delta}|y|-\sum_{i=1}^{k}(-1)^{\delta_{i}}|x_{i}|=1
,
the moduli space
\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathcal{OC}}^{k,\mathbf{p}_{oc,k},\mathbf{w}_{oc,k}}(y;\mathbf{x})
(
8.21
) is a compact smooth manifold-with-boundary of dimension one.

8.3.
The open-closed map

The diagonal bimodule
\mathcal{RW}_{\Delta}
of
\mathcal{RW}
can be regarded as a
\mathcal{W}
-bimodule via the pullback by the pair of functors
(j,j)
,
where
j:\mathcal{W}\to\mathcal{RW}
is the canonical functor (
5.55
).
For simplicity of notation, we put

(8.29)

\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{RW}):=\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{W},(j,j)^{*}\mathcal{RW}_{\Delta}),

and call it the Hochschild chain complex of
\mathcal{W}
with coefficients in
\mathcal{RW}
.

Define the
Rabinowitz open-closed map

(8.30)

\mathcal{OC}_{R}:\mathrm{CC}_{*-n}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{RW})\to RFC^{*}(X)

by counting rigid elements in the moduli spaces
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{OC}}^{k,\mathbf{p}_{oc,k},\mathbf{w}_{oc,k}}(y;\mathbf{x})
.
That is, the map (
8.30
) is a direct sum of maps from
k
-fold tensor products

(8.31)

\mathcal{OC}_{R}=\bigoplus_{k\geq 0}\mathcal{OC}_{R}^{k},

where

(8.32)

\mathcal{OC}_{R}^{k}:\bigoplus_{L_{0},\ldots,L_{k-1}\in{\mathbb{L}}}RC^{*}(L_{k-1},L_{1})\otimes CW^{*}(L_{k-2},L_{k-1};H)\otimes\cdots\otimes CW^{*}(L_{0},L_{1};H)\to RFC^{*}(X).

This is further decomposed into components

(8.33)

\mathcal{OC}_{R}^{k}=\bigoplus_{\mathbf{p}_{oc,k},\mathbf{w}_{oc,k}}\mathcal{OC}_{R}^{k,\mathbf{p}_{oc,k},\mathbf{w}_{oc,k}},

where the sum is over all possible injective maps
\mathbf{p}_{oc,k}:F\to\{k\}
and weights
\mathbf{w}_{oc,k}
defined in (
8.2
),
satisfying (
8.1
), such that the component

(8.34)

\begin{split}\mathcal{OC}_{R}^{k,\mathbf{p}_{oc,k},\mathbf{w}_{oc,k}}:\bigoplus_{L_{0},\ldots,L_{k-1}\in{\mathbb{L}}}&CW^{*}(L_{k-1},L_{1};\mathfrak{s}_{k}H)\otimes CW^{*}(L_{k-2},L_{k-1};H)\\
&\otimes\cdots\otimes CW^{*}(L_{0},L_{1};H)\to SC^{*}(X;\mathfrak{s}H_{F})\end{split}

is defined on a basis of elements by

(8.35)

\begin{split}&\mathcal{OC}_{R}^{k,\mathbf{p}_{oc,k},\mathbf{w}_{oc,k}}([x_{k}^{\mathfrak{s}_{k}}]\otimes[x_{k-1}]\otimes\cdots\otimes[x_{1}])\\
=&\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\\
(-1)^{\delta}|y|=k+|F|+n(1+2\delta+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\delta_{i})-\sum_{i=1}^{k}(-1)^{\delta_{i}}|x_{i}|\end{subarray}}\sum_{u\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{OC}}^{k,\mathbf{p}_{oc,k},\mathbf{w}_{oc,k}}(y;\mathbf{x})}\\
&(-1)^{*_{k,\mathbf{p}_{oc,k},\mathbf{w}_{oc,k}}+\diamond_{k,\mathbf{p}_{oc,k},\mathbf{w}_{oc,k}}+|x_{k}^{\mathfrak{s}_{k}}|}o_{u}([x_{k}^{\mathfrak{s}_{k}}]\otimes[x_{k-1}]\otimes\cdots\otimes[x_{1}]).\end{split}

Here the symbols
\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{s}_{i}
are defined in (
5.17
),
and the signs are (
5.53
), (
5.54
).
In this case, since
w_{1}=\ldots=w_{k-1}=0
,
we have by (
8.1
) that
\mathbf{p}_{oc,k}^{-1}(i)=\varnothing,i=1,\ldots,k-1
.
It follows that the signs get simplified to

(8.36)

\displaystyle*_{k,\mathbf{p}_{oc,k},\mathbf{w}_{oc,k}}=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{k}i|x_{i}^{\mathfrak{s}_{i}}|,

(8.37)

\displaystyle\diamond_{k,\mathbf{p}_{oc,k},\mathbf{w}_{oc,k}}=0.

The formula (
8.35
) can be rewritten as

(8.38)

\begin{split}&\mathcal{OC}_{R}^{k,\mathbf{p}_{oc,k},\mathbf{w}_{oc,k}}([x_{0}^{\mathfrak{s}_{0}}]\otimes[x_{k}]\otimes\cdots\otimes[x_{1}])\\
=&\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\\
(-1)^{\delta}|y|=k+|F|+n(1+2\delta+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\delta_{i})-\sum_{i=1}^{k}(-1)^{\delta_{i}}|x_{i}|\end{subarray}}\sum_{u\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{OC}}^{k,\mathbf{p}_{oc,k},\mathbf{w}_{oc,k}}(y;\mathbf{x})}\\
&(-1)^{\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}i|x_{i}|+(k+1)|x_{k}^{\mathfrak{s}_{k}}|}o_{u}([x_{k}^{\mathfrak{s}_{k}}]\otimes[x_{k}]\otimes\cdots\otimes[x_{1}]).\end{split}

If we restrict the special input from
\mathcal{RW}
to
\mathcal{W}
via the functor
j:\mathcal{W}\to\mathcal{RW}
,
which, in terms of open-closed strings with popsicle structures introduced in §
8.1
, means that we require
w_{k}=0
,
then (
8.1
) implies
w=0
and
F=\varnothing
for the moduli spaces
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{OC}}^{k,\mathbf{p}_{oc,k},\mathbf{w}_{oc,k}}(y;\mathbf{x})
with
w_{k}=0
.
Therefore, all such moduli spaces are the usual moduli spaces of open-closed strings
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{OC}}^{k}(y;\mathbf{x})

In this case, the Rabinowitz open-closed string restricts to the (usual)
open-closed map

(8.39)

\mathcal{OC}:\mathrm{CC}_{*-n}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{W})\to SC^{*}(X;H_{F}).

Proposition 8.6
.

The map
\mathcal{OC}_{R}
(
8.30
), whose components
\mathcal{OC}_{R}^{k}
are defined in (
8.35
), is a chain map.

Proof.

This is a standard degeneration-gluing argument,
which follows from the study of the codimension-one boundary strata of the moduli spaces
\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathcal{OC}}^{k,\mathbf{p}_{oc,k},\mathbf{w}_{oc,k}}(y;\mathbf{x})
,
where the boundary strata are described in (
8.22
) - (
8.27
).
together with Proposition
8.5
.
∎

Proposition 8.7
.

Whenever the open-closed map
\mathcal{OC}
is a quasi-isomorphism,
the Rabinowitz open-closed map (
8.30
) is also a quasi-isomorphism.

Proof.

By Proposition
5.17
, we have a quasi-isomorphism

\iota:\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{c}:\mathcal{W}_{-}\to(\mathcal{W}^{op})_{\Delta})\to\mathcal{RW}

of
\mathcal{W}^{op}
-bimodules.
This implies that the diagonal bimodule of
\mathcal{RW}
, viewed as a
\mathcal{W}
-bimodule via the pullback by the pair of functors
(j,j)
, comes with a quasi-isomorphism of
\mathcal{W}
-bimodules

(8.40)

\iota:\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{c}:\mathcal{W}_{-}^{\top}\to\mathcal{W}_{\Delta})\to(j,j)^{*}\mathcal{RW}_{\Delta},

where
\mathcal{W}_{-}^{\top}
is defined as the
\mathcal{W}
-bimodule that is the transpose of
\mathcal{W}_{-}
,
whose underlying chain complex is

(8.41)

\mathcal{W}_{-}^{\top}(K,L)=\mathcal{W}_{-}(L,K)=CW^{*}(L,K;-H).

The underlying map of
\iota
is the canonical chain-level identity map on chain complexes underlying both sides of (
8.40
).
Thus we get a quasi-isomorphism of chain complexes

(8.42)

\iota_{*}:\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{W},\mathrm{cone}(\mathcal{W}_{-}^{\top}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathfrak{c}}}{{\to}}\mathcal{W}_{\Delta}))=\mathrm{cone}(\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{W}_{-}^{\top})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathfrak{c}_{*}}}{{\to}}\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{W}_{\Delta}))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{W},(j,j)^{*}\mathcal{RW}_{\Delta}).

The composition of (
8.42
) with the open-closed map
\mathcal{OC}_{R}
yields a chain map

(8.43)

\mathrm{cone}(\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{W}_{-}^{\top}[-n])\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathfrak{c}_{*}}}{{\to}}\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{W}_{\Delta}[-n]))\to RFC^{*}(X)=\mathrm{cone}(SC^{*}(X;-H_{F})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c}}{{\to}}SC^{*}(X;H_{F})).

To prove such a chain map on mappings cones is quasi-isomorphism, it suffices to prove that the filtered pieces

(8.44)

\displaystyle\mathcal{OC}_{-}[1]:\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{W}_{-}^{\top}[-n])[1]

\displaystyle\to SC^{*}(X;-H_{F})[1],

(8.45)

\displaystyle\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{W}_{\Delta}[-n])

\displaystyle\to SC^{*}(X;H_{F}),

are both quasi-isomorphisms.
The second map (
8.45
) is precisely the usual open-closed map
\mathcal{OC}
(
8.39
) (see the paragraph above (
8.39
) on how it is defined).

The first map (
8.44
) is defined in a similar way,
by counting rigid elements in the moduli space
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{OC}}^{k,\mathbf{p}_{oc,k},\mathbf{w}_{oc,k}}(y;\mathbf{x})
subject to the weight condition
w=w_{0}=-1
.
Note that by Propostion
5.18
, Poincaré duality (
5.46
) extends to a quasi-isomorphism of
\mathcal{W}
-bimodules

(8.46)

\mathcal{I}:\mathcal{W}_{-}^{\top}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathcal{W}^{\vee}[-n].

Since quasi-isomorphisms of
A_{\infty}
-bimodules are invertible, we also get an inverse

(8.47)

\mathcal{K}:\mathcal{W}^{\vee}[-n]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathcal{W}_{-}^{\top},

such that the
(0,0)
-th order terms of both (
8.46
) and (
8.47
) are the identity map on the chain complexes.
Thus we get a quasi-isomorphism

(8.48)

\mathcal{I}_{*}:\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{W}_{-}^{\top})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{W}^{\vee}[-n])

as well as its chain homotopy inverse

(8.49)

\mathcal{K}_{*}:\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{W}^{\vee}[-n])\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{W}_{-}^{\top}).

Since
\mathcal{W}
has a weak smooth Calabi-Yau structure
\sigma
,
we by Lemma
2.28
a quasi-isomorphism

(8.50)

-\cap\sigma:\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{W}^{\vee})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{W}^{\vee}[-n]).

Note that we have a canonical evaluation pairing

(8.51)

\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{W}^{\vee})\otimes\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{W})\to{\mathbf{k}}

giving a chain-level identification

(8.52)

\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{W}^{\vee})\cong\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{W})^{\vee}.

Thus we get the following composition

(8.53)

\begin{split}&\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{W})^{\vee}[-n]=\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{W}^{\vee}[-n])\stackrel{{\scriptstyle-\cap\sigma}}{{\to}}\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{W}^{\vee}[-2n])\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{K}_{*}}}{{\to}}\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{W}_{-}^{\top}[-n])\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{OC}_{-}}}{{\to}}SC^{*}(X;-H_{F})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle I^{-1}}}{{\to}}SC^{*}(X;H_{F})^{\vee}[-2n].\end{split}

Since
\mathcal{OC}
is assumed to be a quasi-isomorphism, so is its linear dual.
On the level of homology groups,
this composition (
8.53
) induces the same map as the inverse of the linear dual of
\mathcal{OC}
(
8.39
),
because of the way we count rigid elements in
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{OC}}^{k,\mathbf{p}_{oc,k},\mathbf{w}_{oc,k}}(y;\mathbf{x})
when
w=w_{0}=-1
:
we think of
y^{-}\in\mathcal{O}(-H_{F})
as an input that corresponds to the actual geometric output
y\in\mathcal{O}(H_{F})
,
and similarly for
x_{0}^{-}
.
Thus
\mathcal{OC}_{-}
(
8.44
) is a quasi-isomorphism,
which implies that the Rabinowitz open-closed map
\mathcal{OC}_{R}
(
8.56
) is a quasi-isomorphism.
∎

Summarizing the Proof of Proposition
8.7
,
we can regard the Rabinowitz open-closed map
\mathcal{OC}_{R}
(
8.30
),
in view of the identification (
8.42
),
as a chain map of mapping cones:

(8.54)

\mathrm{cone}(\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{W}_{-}^{\top}[-n])\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathfrak{c}_{*}}}{{\to}}\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{W}_{\Delta}[-n]))\to\mathrm{cone}(SC^{*}(X;-H_{F})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c}}{{\to}}SC^{*}(X;H_{F})).

Such a map has three terms, two of which are already seen as
\mathcal{OC}
(
8.45
) and
\mathcal{OC}_{-}
(
8.44
),
defined by counting rigid elements in the moduli spaces
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{OC}}^{k,\mathbf{p}_{oc,k},\mathbf{w}_{oc,k}}(y;\mathbf{x})
when
w=w_{0}=0
and when
w=w_{0}=-1
respectively.
There is another term

(8.55)

\mathcal{OC}_{-,+}:\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{W}_{-}^{\top}[-n])[1]\to SC^{*}(X;H_{F}),

which is precisely given by counting rigid elements in the moduli space
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{OC}}^{k,\mathbf{p}_{oc,k},\mathbf{w}_{oc,k}}(y;\mathbf{x})
when
w=0,w_{0}=-1
with non-trivial popsicle structures.
This is not a chain map, but rather a chain homotopy.
In other words, the way in which we define
\mathcal{OC}_{R}
(
8.30
) is a way of packaging the three maps
\mathcal{OC}
(
8.45
),
\mathcal{OC}_{-}
(
8.44
) and
\mathcal{OC}_{-,+}
(
8.55
).

Going one step beyond, we wonder what structures
\mathrm{HH}_{*}(\mathcal{RW},\mathcal{RW})
and
\mathrm{HH}^{*}(\mathcal{RW},\mathcal{RW})
themselves carry and how they are related to
RFH^{*}(X)
.
In an upcoming work, we shall show that the open-closed map factors through a map

(8.56)

\mathrm{HH}_{*-n}(\mathcal{RW}(X),\mathcal{RW}(X))\to RFH^{*}(X).

The reader should be warned that the current method by using the moduli spaces of pseudoholomorphic disks considered in this paper is not appropriate for defining this map.
The map (
8.56
) is always surjective, but not always an isomorphism.
However, it will play an important role in understanding the relation between the residue on
\mathcal{RW}
and the trace on
RFH^{*}(X)
.

8.4.
Relating pairings

We close this section by establishing one last property of the Rabinowitz open-closed map
\mathcal{OC}_{R}
,
which has to do with pairings.
First, we shall construct a pairing of degree
1-2n
on
\mathrm{CC}_{*-n}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{RW})
:

(8.57)

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\sigma}:\mathrm{CC}_{*-n}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{RW})\otimes\mathrm{CC}_{(2n-1-*)-n}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{RW})\to{\mathbf{k}}[1-2n]

which will depend on the weak smooth Calabi-Yau structure
\sigma
on
\mathcal{W}
.
Using the identification (
8.42
),
it suffices to construct a pairing of degree
1

(8.58)

\mathrm{cone}(\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{W}_{-}^{\top})\to\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{W}))\otimes\mathrm{cone}(\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{W}_{-}^{\top})\to\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{W}))\to{\mathbf{k}}[1].

Moreover, via the terms in the composition (
8.53
) before
\mathcal{OC}_{-}
(without degree shift by
-n
),
i.e., via the following composition

(8.59)

\mathcal{K}_{*}\circ(-\cap\sigma):\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{W})^{\vee}=\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{W}^{\vee})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle-\cap\sigma}}{{\to}}\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{W}^{\vee}[-n])\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{K}_{*}}}{{\to}}\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{W}_{-}^{\top}),

it suffices to construct a pairing

(8.60)

\mathrm{cone}(\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{W})^{\vee}\to\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{W}))\otimes\mathrm{cone}(\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{W})^{\vee}\to\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{W}))\to{\mathbf{k}}[1].

We define this pairing to be the tautological pairing
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{taut}
(
2.133
).
Thus we obtain the pairing (
8.57
),
which depends on the weak smooth Calabi-Yau structure in the way that the map (
8.59
) does.

Proposition 8.8
.

The map
\mathcal{OC}_{R}
(
8.30
) respects pairings on the level of cohomology groups.

Proof.

Upon the identifications (
8.59
) and (
8.42
) as well as the Poincaré duality isomorphism (
7.12
),
the map
\mathcal{OC}_{R}
(
8.30
) is equivalent to a chain map between mapping cones

(8.61)

\mathrm{cone}(\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{W})^{\vee}[-n]\to\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{W})[-n])\to\mathrm{cone}(SC^{*}(X;H_{F})^{\vee}[-2n]\to SC^{*}(X;H_{F}))

which is equivalent to a diagram

(8.62)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{W})^{\vee}[-n]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{W})[-n]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces SC^{*}(X;H_{F})^{\vee}[-2n]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces SC^{*}(X;H_{F}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\mathfrak{c}}_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\mathcal{OC}}_{-}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\mathcal{OC}}_{-+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{OC}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{c}}

Here
\bar{\mathfrak{c}}_{*}=\mathfrak{c}_{*}\circ\mathcal{K}_{*}\circ(-\cap\sigma)
,

\mathcal{K}_{*}
is (
8.49
),

\mathcal{K}_{*}\circ(-\cap\sigma)
is (
8.59
),
\mathfrak{c}:\mathcal{W}_{-}^{\top}\to\mathcal{W}_{\Delta}
is the bimodule continuation map (
5.62
);

\tilde{\mathcal{OC}}_{-}=I^{-1}\circ\mathcal{OC}_{-}\circ\mathcal{K}_{*}\circ(-\cap\sigma)
is the composition (
8.53
),

\bar{c}=c\circ I^{-1}
,

c:SC_{*}(X)\to SC^{*}(X)
is the continuation map and
I^{-1}
is the inverse of (
7.12
),

\tilde{\mathcal{OC}}_{-+}=\mathcal{OC}_{-+}\circ\mathcal{K}_{*}\circ(-\cap\sigma)
,
and
\mathcal{OC}_{-+}
is (
8.55
).

The induced map on homology by
\tilde{\mathcal{OC}}_{-}
is

(8.63)

(\tilde{\mathcal{OC}}_{-})_{*}:\mathrm{HH}_{*}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{W})^{\vee}[-n]\to SH^{*}(X)^{\vee}[-2n],

which is the inverse of the induced map
\mathcal{OC}^{\vee}_{*}
,
as argued in the Proof of Proposition
8.7
,
specifically for the map (
8.53
).

The map

\tilde{\mathcal{OC}}_{-+}:\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{W})^{\vee}[-n]\to SC^{*}(X;H_{F})[1]

has degree
1
and is not a chain map, but rather a chain homotopy between the two sides of the square (
8.62
).
Thus we get a commutative diagram of homology groups:

(8.64)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{HH}_{*}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{W})^{\vee}[-n]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{HH}_{*}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{W})[-n]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces SH^{*}(X)^{\vee}[-2n]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces SH^{*}(X),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\mathfrak{c}}_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\tilde{\mathcal{OC}}_{-})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathcal{OC})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{c}}

where both vertical arrows are isomorphisms,
and the left arrow is the inverse to the linear dual of the right arrow.

Both the pairing
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\sigma}
(
8.57
) on
\mathrm{CC}_{*-n}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{RW})
and the pairing
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{taut}
(
7.16
) on
RFC^{*}(X)
are defined in terms of the tautological pairing (
2.133
),
induced by the evaluation pairing
ev
(
2.126
).
Thus, on the level of homology, commutativity of (
8.64
) along with the fact that the left arrow is the inverse to the linear dual of the right arrow,
implies that
(\mathcal{OC}_{R})_{*}
respects pairings on homology groups.
∎

Now we are ready to prove Theorem
1.10
.

Proof of Theorem
1.10
.

The map
\mathcal{OC}_{R}
is a chain map by Lemma
8.6
,
and moreover a quasi-isomorphism by Proposition
8.7
.
It respects pairings by Proposition
8.8
.
∎

One thing to notice is that the domain of the homology level open-closed map
\mathcal{OC}_{R}
indeed carries a ring structure.
When
X
is nondegenerate with
c_{1}(X)=0
, the existence of a weak smooth Calabi-Yau structure
\sigma
on
\mathcal{W}
implies that there is an isomorphism

(8.65)

\mathrm{HH}_{*-n}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{RW})\cong\mathrm{HH}_{*-n}(\mathcal{W},\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\infty})\cong\mathrm{HH}^{*}(\mathcal{W},\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\infty}),

where the first arrow is induced by
\Phi_{*}
and the second arrow is induced by capping with
\sigma
.
Finally, notice that the Hochschild cohomology
\mathrm{HH}^{*}(\mathcal{W},\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\infty})
carries a product from (
3.28
),
which is in fact graded commutative.
This answers the first part of Question 1.8 in
[
28
]
.
For the second part of that question,
general structural properties of Fukaya categories could be used to prove that
\mathcal{OC}_{R}
is ring homomorphism ((e.g.
[
40
]
),
but issues with duality (e.g. as address in
[
19
]
) remain to be resolved for
A_{\infty}
-categories enriched in topological vector spaces.
